However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment directly, and so its direct relevance to predicting the effect of enabling punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for understanding the role of social information and learning in public goods games, and suggest that efficiency may be lower when participants can observe and learn from successful free-riding.

- source: Alenaizi, R., Contu, D., Elshareif, E., Kaya, O., & Kaya, I. (2024). Assumed similarity in personality traits has a short-lived positive effect on cooperation in absence of reciprocity. *Applied Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when participants are told they are matched with others who have similar personality traits, their contributions to the public good increase in the short term (notably in round 7 compared to round 4). When told they are matched with dissimilar personalities, contributions decrease. However, the effect of similarity framing is short-lived and diminishes quickly. The game includes a penalty for non-contribution, but this is a fixed, automatic penalty rather than a peer-administered punishment. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The penalty mechanism is automatic and not peer-administered, so it does not map directly to the punishment dimension of interest. The findings are relevant for understanding how framing and perceived similarity affect contributions, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment mechanisms. The game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, and penalty structure are described, but the absence of a peer punishment treatment means this paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2025). Empirical evidence for the spread of Cooperation through copying successful groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that providing information about a successful, cooperative out-group increases participants' contributions in a repeated PGG, but this effect is transient and does not persist or spread within the group. The absence of mechanisms such as punishment means that cooperation is not stably maintained. The authors argue that additional mechanisms (e.g., punishment, reputation) are likely necessary for the stable diffusion and maintenance of cooperation. The cross-cultural survey finds that greater press freedom is associated with higher impersonal prosociality (e.g., general trust), but not with personal prosociality (e.g., family trust). No direct evidence is provided on the effect of punishment or on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as punishment is not present in the experimental design. However, it provides indirect support for the idea that, in the absence of punishment, even interventions that increase cooperation (such as exposure to successful out-groups) do not lead to sustained or spreading cooperation or higher group payoffs. The discussion and literature review suggest that punishment may be necessary for stable, high-efficiency outcomes in repeated PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, without punishment, efficiency gains from social learning or imitation are likely to be transient. The experimental design and results can help calibrate the expected baseline (control) efficiency in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the treatment effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Guo, J. D. R. (2025). Doing good in the digital world. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that both identification and attribution of individual contributions increase average contributions in a finitely repeated public goods game. The effect is strongest when both are present, but even imprecise identifiers (numbers, avatars) and partial attribution (not perfectly linking actions to identifiers) increase contributions relative to baseline. The effect is primarily behavioral: more subjects contribute their full endowment when individual contributions are revealed. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the game, and the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Theoretical analysis considers the possibility of punishment outside the game, but the experimental data do not support a punishment-based explanation for the observed effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how identification and attribution affect contribution behavior in public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Since no punishment or reward mechanism is present, the findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with punishment enabled. However, the results do suggest that increasing transparency and reducing anonymity can increase contributions, which may interact with the effects of punishment in other designs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence on the effects of information and identification, but not on punishment or efficiency.

- source: Liu, X. L., Li, J. B., Wang, G. R., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., & Fu, X. G. (2020). Transcranial direct current stimulation of the rLPFC shifts normative judgments in voluntary cooperation. *Neuroscience Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that modulating rLPFC activity via tDCS causally affects both participants' normative judgments about appropriate contributions and their actual contributions in a one-shot, linear, asymmetric public goods game. Anodal stimulation (increasing rLPFC excitability) leads to higher normative judgments and higher contributions, while cathodal stimulation (decreasing rLPFC excitability) leads to lower judgments and contributions, relative to sham. The effect is robust across both 'rich' and 'poor' player types. No punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms are present in the game, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant to understanding the neural basis of normative judgment and voluntary cooperation, but not to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, number of rounds) may be useful for context, but the absence of punishment means this study should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Grayson, S., Feinberg, M., Willer, R., & Zaki, J. (2025). Ironic effects of prosocial gossip in driving inaccurate social perceptions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game, participants overwhelmingly use gossip to highlight free-riders rather than cooperators, even when most group members are cooperative. When a new set of participants reads these gossip notes, they systematically underestimate the cooperativeness of the original group, becoming more cynical and less accurate in their perceptions. However, this increased cynicism does not translate into lower expectations for their own group's cooperation or reduced contributions in their own public goods game. There is some evidence that exposure to negative gossip polarizes behavior, increasing the likelihood of both full cooperation and full defection, but the average contribution is not significantly affected.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or gossip on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Instead, it provides evidence that gossip (as a reputational mechanism) is negatively skewed and can distort outsiders' perceptions of group cooperativeness, but does not affect actual contribution behavior in subsequent games. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that reputational mechanisms like gossip may increase cooperation (as shown in the referenced Feinberg et al., 2014 study), but the current paper's main contribution is to highlight the informational and perceptual side effects of such mechanisms, not their impact on efficiency.

- source: Coutts, A. (2022). Identifying communication spillovers in lab-in-the-field experiments&x2729;. *Journal of Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that communication spillovers between villages (i.e., when participants in one village can talk to those in a neighboring village who have already played) lead to substantial increases in contributions to the public good. The effect is robust to different matching strategies and distance thresholds. In the second round, when some villages played alternative versions of the public goods game (including punishment, reward, or risk), the positive effect of these treatments on contributions was only observed in villages not exposed to communication spillovers. The main implication is that communication between sessions can significantly increase cooperation, potentially biasing estimates of treatment effects in field experiments. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that communication (even unstructured, informal communication between sessions) can increase cooperation in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that allowing or enabling communication (or failing to prevent spillovers) may increase contributions, which could translate into higher efficiency if all other game parameters are held constant. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, and only analyzes contribution rates, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a main treatment, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight the importance of controlling for communication and spillovers when interpreting the effects of any treatment (including punishment) in field experiments.

- source: Samu, F., Piras, S., Righi, S., Setti, M., & Takács, K. (2025). Cooperation is not rewarded by friendship, but generous and selfish students repel each other in social networks. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a sample of 10-year-old students, measured cooperation and prosociality in economic games (including a PGG) do not predict receiving more friendship nominations or being avoided via negative ties. There is no evidence that prosocial students are more popular or that selfish students are more disliked. However, there is a significant tendency for students with dissimilar levels of generosity (especially in the Dictator Game) to form negative ties with each other, suggesting that social network repulsion is structured by differences in prosociality. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. While it uses a standard PGG to measure individual cooperation, the outcomes are social network nominations (friendship and negative ties), not payoffs or efficiency. The only adjacent relevance is the interpretation of negative ties as a form of informal social punishment, but this is not a formal or incentivized punishment mechanism as in PGG experiments with punishment. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Peng, H. C. (2022). Effects of majority-vote reward mechanism on cooperation: a public good experimental study. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The majority-vote reward mechanism significantly increases group efficiency and cooperation compared to both peer reward and no reward, in both homogeneous and heterogeneous MPCR settings. Peer reward increases efficiency and cooperation only in homogeneous groups. In heterogeneous groups, only the majority-vote reward mechanism leads to a significant increase in efficiency. The efficiency gains are due to higher contributions and more willingness to pay reward costs under the majority-vote mechanism. The paper provides direct evidence on efficiency outcomes (Table 6), showing that the majority-vote reward mechanism achieves the highest efficiency among the tested mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with 4 players, 6 rounds per condition, continuous contribution, no chat, and full information, adding a majority-vote reward mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-reward ratio) increases efficiency relative to the control (no reward) and peer reward conditions. Peer reward also increases efficiency in homogeneous groups, but not in heterogeneous groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar design dimensions, especially regarding the presence and type of reward mechanism, MPCR homogeneity, and the absence of punishment. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, majority-vote reward is a robust way to increase efficiency, especially when group members have heterogeneous returns from the public good.

- source: Colasante, A., Morone, A., Nemore, F., & Tiranzoni, P. (2024). Self-love, growth, and competition in a public good game. *Kyklos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing intragroup competition for higher MPCRs in a public goods game, especially when combined with dynamic endowments (growth), leads to significantly higher contributions and much greater group wealth compared to growth alone. The CG treatment (competition + growth) more than doubles average group wealth compared to growth-only (G) treatment. Inequality (Gini coefficient) is lower in the short term in the CG treatment than in G, but converges over time. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or tested in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games. It is not informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, as no punishment mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the effects of dynamic endowments and intragroup competition, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Yang, Q. X., & Yang, Y. L. (2024). A social monitoring mechanism for third-party judges promotes cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in repeated PGGs without punishment or reward, cooperation and group payoff decline over time, consistent with previous literature. The authors reconstruct a micro-dynamic model based on proportional changes in expected payoff, showing that players' willingness to cooperate depends on their current strategy and local cooperation levels. The model predicts the observed decline in cooperation and group payoff. The paper also documents a 'restart effect' in two-stage games, where cooperation temporarily increases at the transition point. The findings provide a framework for predicting the evolution of cooperation and group payoff in repeated PGGs without external interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on baseline efficiency and group payoff dynamics in repeated PGGs without punishment or reward. It is directly relevant for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency as a function of game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, MPCR, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related variables. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it is highly informative for modeling the baseline (control) case.

- source: Oechssler, J., Reischmann, A., & Sofianos, A. (2022). The conditional contribution mechanism for repeated public goods-The general case. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The CCM leads to much higher efficiency (as measured by average group contributions and the share of Pareto optimal outcomes) than the standard VCM in repeated public goods games with complete information. The CCM achieves about 78% Pareto optimal outcomes, compared to 24% for the VCM. The SCCM (one-message version) and VCMC (VCM with non-binding communication) perform between the two, with SCCM catching up to CCM in later periods. Under incomplete information, both CCM and VCM achieve similar, moderate efficiency, and the difference is not significant. The risk of being a 'sucker' (earning less than the endowment) is much lower in CCM than in VCM. The study provides strong evidence that the CCM can sustain high efficiency in repeated public goods games without requiring knowledge of others' preferences, but does not test any punishment or sanctioning treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated public goods games with mechanisms that allow conditional contributions, but it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions. The experimental design and outcome measures are directly relevant to efficiency prediction, but since no punishment is present in any treatment, the paper cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. It does, however, provide a strong benchmark for efficiency in control games without punishment, especially for games with 5 players, 60 rounds, continuous contributions, and no communication or punishment. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper can only inform the baseline (control) efficiency, not the treatment effect of punishment.

- source: Gürdal, M. Y., Gürerk, Kaçamak, Y., & Kart, E. (2024). How to increase and sustain cooperation in public goods games: Conditional commitments via a mediator. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The conditional commitment mechanism, when it enables full contribution (FULL treatment), leads to almost universal agreement and very high efficiency: average group payoffs are close to the social optimum. In the HALF treatment (commitment to half contribution), agreement rates and efficiency are lower, and some subjects attempt to lead by contributing more than the default, but this does not fully overcome the free-rider problem. There is no punishment or sanctioning in any treatment. The study demonstrates that a well-designed commitment device can substitute for punishment in achieving high efficiency in public goods games, but only if the commitment outcome is itself efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. However, it is highly informative about the baseline efficiency of a standard linear public goods game (VCM) with and without a strong commitment device. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is best used as a control/baseline for efficiency in the absence of punishment, and as evidence for the potential of alternative mechanisms (commitment) to achieve high efficiency. It does not inform the marginal effect of punishment relative to control, but does provide precise efficiency data for a 4-player, 10-round, MPCR=0.4, no-punishment, no-reward, no-chat, continuous contribution VCM.

- source: English, W. (2012). Demystifying trust: Experimental evidence from Cambodia and Thailand. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in one-shot, four-player public goods games in rural Thailand and Cambodia, standard survey measures of trust (from the World Values Survey) do not predict contributions or group efficiency. Instead, expectations about others' contributions are the key driver of cooperative behavior and group payoff. When expectations are experimentally lowered (by revealing low contributions from a previous group), both contributions and group efficiency drop sharply. The distribution of player types (reciprocators, free riders, altruists) is similar to previous studies, with reciprocators being the largest group. The results suggest that interventions that shift expectations can rapidly change group efficiency, while survey-based trust is not a reliable predictor of economic cooperation in this context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, group efficiency in public goods games is highly sensitive to participants' expectations about others' behavior, but not to standard survey measures of trust. For the downstream prediction task, this means that, for games with similar design (one-shot, no punishment, no communication, fixed group size, known MPCR), efficiency is likely to be determined by expectation management rather than by trust survey scores. The absence of punishment means the paper does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of expectation-shaping interventions. The findings are most relevant for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in PGGs without punishment, and for understanding that expectation manipulation (e.g., via feedback or communication) can have large effects on efficiency.

- source: Hajikhameneh, A., & Iannaccone, L. R. (2023). God games: An experimental study of uncertainty, superstition, and cooperation. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that adding random or pseudo-supernatural earnings shocks to a standard public goods game (without punishment or reward) substantially increases group contributions and group earnings, relative to the baseline. Communication (chat) further boosts contributions and efficiency in most conditions, but in the 'watchful' (quasi-monotheistic) condition, chat can foster counterproductive superstitions, leading to suboptimal group strategies and lower efficiency. The study provides strong evidence that even irrelevant risk or uncertainty can increase cooperation and group welfare in PGGs, but does not test or report on punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. However, it does show that introducing random or pseudo-supernatural shocks (even when not contingent on behavior) can increase efficiency, and that communication can further enhance or sometimes reduce efficiency depending on framing. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight that other forms of uncertainty or narrative framing can have large effects on group efficiency. The game design dimensions are well-documented, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot be used to estimate the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Nax, H. H., Balietti, S., Murphy, R. O., & Helbing, D. (2018). Adding noise to the institution: an experimental welfare investigation of the contribution-based grouping mechanism. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that, in a repeated public goods game with 16 players (4 groups of 4), introducing a contribution-based grouping mechanism (meritocratic matching) with low or moderate noise leads to much higher efficiency (average total payoff) than random matching (no meritocracy). The efficiency gains are robust across different levels of noise, with the highest efficiency observed under perfect meritocracy (no noise), but even substantial gains at moderate noise levels. The control (no meritocracy, i.e., random matching) produces the lowest efficiency, consistent with standard PGG decay. The study also finds that increased meritocracy reduces payoff inequality, contrary to theoretical expectations. The results are robust to different measures of inequality and are observed both in lab and online settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with grouping based on contributions, increasing the precision of the grouping mechanism (i.e., reducing noise in meritocratic matching) leads to large increases in efficiency relative to random matching. For prediction tasks, the presence of a contribution-based grouping mechanism (even if noisy) should be expected to substantially increase efficiency compared to standard PGG controls. The effect is robust across a range of noise levels, and the efficiency gains are not offset by increased inequality. However, the study does not involve punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so its direct applicability to punishment-enabled games is limited. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of sorting/grouping mechanisms, not punishment.

- source: Ball, S., Sarangi, S., & Upadhyay, S. (2025). Coalitions Improve the Coordination and Provision of Public Goods: Theory and Experimental Evidence. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to form coalitions before making contribution decisions in a binary public goods game leads to higher contributions and higher group payoffs. Coalition members are much more likely to contribute than in the standard VCM, and the decline in contributions over rounds is mitigated. Higher MPCR increases both coalition size and the likelihood of contribution, resulting in higher payoffs for all group members, including the least well-off. The coalition mechanism works by sorting players with stronger social preferences into the coalition, who then coordinate to contribute. The experiment directly compares the standard VCM (control) to the coalition treatment, showing clear increases in group payoff and efficiency when coalitions are allowed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in binary (all-or-nothing) public goods games with induced social preferences, adding a coalition formation stage (where coalition members vote and are bound by majority rule) increases group payoff and efficiency relative to the standard VCM. The effect is stronger at higher MPCR. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without coalition mechanisms, but not for punishment-based interventions, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. The findings suggest that coordination mechanisms (like coalitions) can substitute for punishment in increasing efficiency, especially when MPCR is high and social preferences are present.

- source: Koessler, A. K., Müller, J., & Zitzelsberger, S. (2023). Asymmetric heterogeneities and the role of transfers in a public goods experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with three players (one high-endowment/low-productivity, two low-endowment/high-productivity), enabling voluntary, unconditional transfers before contribution decisions leads to higher group efficiency and average payoffs compared to a baseline with no transfers and a condition with exogenous (non-voluntary) transfers. Most high-endowment players transfer all or half of their endowment, and recipients reciprocate with high contributions, resulting in larger public good provision and higher group profits. The effect is not observed when transfers are exogenously imposed, highlighting the importance of intention-based reciprocity. If the transfer option is available but not used, cooperation and efficiency are lower than in the baseline. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctions, introducing a voluntary transfer mechanism in a PGG with asymmetric heterogeneity can substantially increase efficiency and group payoffs, provided that transfers are used and reciprocated. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar heterogeneity and transfer options, but do not inform about the effects of punishment or sanctions, as these are not present in the design. The results suggest that mechanisms enabling trust and reciprocity (such as voluntary transfers) can substitute for punishment in boosting efficiency, but the effect is contingent on the use and perception of the transfer. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is highly informative for understanding the efficiency effects of alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms in heterogeneous PGGs.

- source: He, S. M., & Zhu, X. (2023). Real-time monitoring in a public-goods game. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a four-player linear public-goods game, mechanisms that allow for incremental, irreversible commitments—especially when combined with a real-time clock—lead to much higher and more stable contributions than baseline, announcement, or final commitment mechanisms. Contributions in the main (clock) treatment reach about 75% of the maximum possible by the end, while in the baseline and announcement treatments, contributions decay to near zero. The paper provides detailed behavioral analysis of strategy adoption (trigger and near-dominant strategies) but does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, nor does it directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public-goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. Its findings are not directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, given game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it does provide evidence that certain commitment mechanisms (incremental, clock-based) can substantially increase contributions in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not informative.

- source: Chakraborti, R., Maloney, M., Roberts, G., & Shogren, J. F. (2018). Social capital and the voluntary provision of public goods. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: Both structured (team-building) and unstructured (free-form) communication interventions increase contributions in a linear public goods game. Free-form communication leads to higher and more persistent contributions than team-building when group composition is stable. When groups are reallocated after the intervention, team-building's effect on contributions persists, but the effect of free-form communication is reduced, though still positive. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. Its findings are relevant for understanding the impact of communication and social capital interventions on contribution behavior, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency.

- source: Wang, G. R., Li, J. B., Wang, W. H., Wang, Y., & Wang, J. F. (2024). Variance, norms and cooperative behavior in public goods games. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that, in a standard linear public goods game (4 players, one-shot, strategy method), increasing the variance of others' contributions (while holding the mean constant) reduces individual contributions, empirical expectations, and normative expectations. The effect is robust across free riders and cooperators, though their expectations differ. Social norms (empirical and normative expectations) are positively associated with contribution, but do not significantly mediate the effect of variance on contribution. The study does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in others' contributions (variance) affects contribution behavior and social norms in public goods games without punishment or reward. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it manipulate or discuss any punishment-related game design dimensions. Thus, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment. It is useful for understanding baseline (control) behavior in classic PGGs, especially regarding the impact of variance, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Vermeer, A. B. L., Heerema, R. L., & Sanfey, A. G. (2016). Third-Party Cooperation: How Reducing Material Involvement Enhances Contributions to the Public Good. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that reducing personal material involvement in a public goods game increases cooperation rates: participants contribute more when making decisions for a third party than for themselves. This effect is further enhanced when the group consists of human players rather than computer players, suggesting that social motives drive third-party cooperation. The main outcome is the proportion of tokens contributed, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how personal involvement and social context affect cooperation rates, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. The design and results may be contextually informative for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Martinsson, P., Medhin, H., & Persson, E. (2019). MINIMUM LEVELS AND FRAMING IN PUBLIC GOOD PROVISION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a standard linear public goods game, contributions are higher in the give frame than in the take frame. When a minimum contribution level (20% of endowment) is introduced, contributions decrease in the give frame (crowding out) but increase in the take frame (crowding in). The effect is driven by more low contributors being forced to increase in the take frame, while in the give frame, some higher contributors reduce their contributions to the minimum. The distribution of contributor types is stable across frames and minimum level conditions. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it provides informative evidence about the effects of minimum contribution requirements and framing on contribution behavior. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For tasks involving minimum contribution levels or framing, the results suggest that minimum levels can have opposite effects depending on the frame: crowding out in give, crowding in in take. Since efficiency is not reported, the findings should not be used to predict efficiency outcomes of punishment-enabled games.

- source: Johnson, T. (2024). Empirically testing a relationship between cooperation and the prime numbers. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, in a 27-round strangers public goods game (no punishment, no reputation), contributions generally decline over time, but a minority of participants continue to contribute even in late rounds. There is modest evidence that contributions are slightly higher at prime-numbered rounds, but this effect is driven by early rounds (2 and 3) and disappears when they are excluded. Placebo tests suggest the observed effect is unlikely to be spurious, but exploratory analysis casts doubt on the substantive importance of the prime-number effect. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and the experimental condition does not include punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as the analyzed condition lacks any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide a clear description of a standard PGG baseline (strangers, 4 players, 27 rounds, no punishment, no chat, continuous contributions, MPCR=0.1), which can be used as a control condition in comparative studies. The findings on contribution decline and the absence of efficiency or payoff analysis mean this paper is only useful for establishing baseline behavior in the absence of punishment.

- source: Griessmair, M., & Hippmann, P. (2022). Anger, Guilt, and Repeated Cooperation in Social Dilemmas. *Emotion*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in repeated public goods games, experienced anger (arising when others contribute less than expected) leads to a greater decline in contributions, while experienced guilt (arising when one contributes less than others or less than expected) cushions this decline. These effects are robust to controlling for cognitive-economic variables (beliefs about others' contributions). The effects of anger and guilt differ by cooperative type: free riders act on anger but not guilt, while cooperators and reciprocators act on both. The emotional effects are present in both partner and stranger matching, with some evidence for emotional carryover. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the baseline dynamics of cooperation in repeated linear PGGs without punishment or reward. It shows that emotional responses (anger, guilt) are important drivers of contribution decline, but does not address the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding control (no-punishment) efficiency and the psychological mechanisms underlying contribution decline. It does not provide evidence on how punishment would affect efficiency, nor does it manipulate any of the key game design dimensions related to punishment or reward.

- source: Dorner, Z., Tucker, S., & Hassan, G. M. (2024). Heterogeneous productivity stabilizes public good contributions under certainty, uncertainty and ambiguity. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous productivity (certain, uncertain, or ambiguous) into a repeated public goods game leads to more stable contributions over time compared to the standard homogeneous productivity baseline, where contributions typically decline. Under certain heterogeneity, contributions remain stable and end higher than in the homogeneous case. Under uncertainty or ambiguity, contributions start lower but are also stable. The effect is driven by high-productivity types contributing more, which supports higher group contributions. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, and all outcome measures are behavioral (contribution levels), not payoff-based (efficiency, group earnings, or welfare).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in productivity affects contribution dynamics, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Bazart, C., Lefebvre, M., & Rosaz, J. (2022). Promoting socially desirable behaviors through persuasion and commitment: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing anonymous, open-ended communication (exhortations) in a public goods game leads to substantially higher contributions compared to a baseline with no communication. The effect is attributed to cultural norms and prior experience with exhortations, not to reputation-building, as anonymity precludes reputational effects. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on contribution rates and the content/effect of messages.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling anonymous communication (chat/exhortation) increases cooperation (contributions) in public goods games, even in the absence of reputation effects. However, it does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is informative about the effect of the 'chat' dimension (present/absent) on contribution rates, but does not inform about the effect of punishment or about efficiency per se. It is not directly useful for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but supports the importance of communication as a moderator of cooperation.

- source: Kok, L., Oosterbaan, V., Stoker, H., & Vyrastekova, J. (2020). In-group favouritism and social norms: Public goods experiments in Tanzania. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that making sub-village identity salient (In-group treatment) does not increase public goods contributions compared to mixed sub-village groups (Out-group treatment). In fact, contributions are significantly higher in the Out-group treatment. This is explained by the activation of a village-level social norm of cooperation in the Out-group context, while the In-group context triggers more selfish, conditional cooperation. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide evidence that social context and group identity salience can affect contribution behavior, which may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as not providing direct evidence on punishment or efficiency effects.

- source: Yeo, J., & Zhuo, S. (2024). The usage of apologies and group cooperation. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing participants to send apologies (either public or private) after observing contributions in a repeated public goods game leads to significantly higher individual contributions compared to a control with no communication. The effect is stronger for public apologies. The increase in cooperation is mediated by more optimistic beliefs about others and group-level adherence to sincere apology norms. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings. All outcome measures are behavioral (contributions, beliefs, apology usage).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that simple, norm-laden communication (apologies) can increase cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially when made public. However, it does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its findings may be useful for understanding the role of communication and social norms as alternative or complementary mechanisms to punishment, but it cannot be used to predict efficiency changes from enabling punishment.

- source: Liu, J., Sonntag, A., & Zizzo, D. J. (2022). Information defaults in repeated public good provision. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated linear public goods game (n=4, 10 rounds), the default for seeing group contribution information (opt-in vs. opt-out) does not affect the proportion of subjects who actually obtain the information. However, when the default is no information, the decline in contributions over time is slower, suggesting that cooperation unravels more slowly. The price of information (cost to see group contribution info) has a strong effect: when information is free or incentivized, almost all subjects get it; when costly, about a third still pay for it. Subjects adjust their contributions toward the group average, using beliefs when information is not available. There is no direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of information architecture (defaults and cost of information) on contribution dynamics. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For tasks involving the effect of information availability or defaults on contribution (not efficiency), the finding that a default of no information slows the decline of cooperation may be useful. However, since efficiency is not measured or analyzed, and punishment is not present, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the core prediction task.

- source: Chang, D., Chen, R., Krupka, E. L., & Song, Z. W. (2024). Do policy instruments that restrict social identity expression increase economic cooperation?. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Restricting identity expression (banning pin display) increases contributions to the mixed-group public good when Leavers are the majority, with no effect on in-group contributions. The increase in mixed-group contributions is explained by more optimistic beliefs about others' contributions, not by changes in social norms or pinning behavior. The effect is context-specific and may not generalize to other identities, repeated games, or settings with different group compositions. No punishment or sanction mechanism is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how identity expression restrictions can affect contribution behavior via beliefs, in a one-shot, simultaneous, multi-group PGG without punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment or efficiency, this paper is not informative.

- source: Chen, Y. Y., Lu, X. B., Li, Y. Z., Zeng, L. L., Yu, P., Luo, J., Ye, H., & Zheng, W. J. (2021). The Role of the Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex in Public Good and Public Bad Games: Evidence From a tDCS Study. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that modulating VMPFC activity via tDCS can causally affect cooperative behavior in public bad games (loss domain), specifically decreasing cooperation at the start of the game, but has no effect in public good games (gain domain). The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution/loss contribution, beliefs, attitudes), not efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanction mechanism in the design. The findings are relevant for understanding neural mechanisms of cooperation, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as there is no punishment or sanction mechanism in the experimental design, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported. The findings are limited to neural modulation of cooperation and beliefs in standard and loss-framed public goods games. It may provide some contextual background on behavioral outcomes in PGGs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Bose, N., & Sgroi, D. (2022). The role of personality beliefs and small talk in strategic behaviour. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing 4 minutes of pre-game chat (small talk) between paired subjects leads to higher contributions in a two-person public goods game compared to a control group with no chat. This effect is mediated by beliefs about the partner's extraversion, which are formed during the chat. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the effect of pre-game communication and personality beliefs on cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It does provide strong evidence that pre-game chat (communication) increases cooperation in a two-person public goods game, mediated by personality beliefs, but does not report group payoff or efficiency. For the downstream prediction task (punishment effect on efficiency), this paper is not directly informative, but it does support the importance of communication as a moderator of cooperation. It cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Cheo, R., Huang, K. A., & Li, J. P. (2023). Group cooperation of village officials in Chinese rural resettlement: A lab in the field. *Contemporary Economic Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that higher average contributions by village officials in a public goods game are associated with higher self-reported support for a resettlement project among villagers. This relationship is robust to controls for demographic and village characteristics. Mechanism analysis suggests that more cooperative leadership teams inform villagers earlier about the resettlement, and that higher PGG contributions are correlated with more altruistic behavior in a dictator game. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes from the PGG. Actual sign-up rates for resettlement contracts are reported as supplementary evidence, showing a correlation with officials' PGG contributions, but these are not the main focus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a standard PGG without punishment or reward, and the main outcomes are non-payoff behavioral measures (contribution, support, information flow). There is no manipulation or analysis of punishment, nor any efficiency or group payoff outcome reported. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, J. B., Liu, X. L., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., Wang, P. C., Niu, X. F., & Zhu, C. K. (2018). Transcranial Direct Current Stimulation of the Right Lateral Prefrontal Cortex Changes a priori Normative Beliefs in Voluntary Cooperation. *Frontiers in Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that modulating rLPFC activity via tDCS causally shifts participants' a priori normative beliefs about cooperation in a public goods game. Anodal stimulation increases, and cathodal stimulation decreases, both the perceived normative standard (norm.belief) and the expected contributions of others (pg.belief). These effects are robust across player types (high and low endowment) and are statistically significant. However, the study does not measure or report actual contribution behavior, group payoffs, or efficiency, nor does it include any punishment or sanction mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a standard linear PGG design but does not include punishment, nor does it report any efficiency, payoff, or contribution outcomes. The only outcomes are beliefs about norms and others' contributions. Therefore, it does not inform the downstream prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency, given game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Ranehill, E., Weber, R. A., & Wu, K. Y. (2024). Does redistribution affect cooperation and trust?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that neither progressive nor regressive redistribution policies have significant effects on cooperation, trust, or trustworthiness among group members, as measured in a public good game and a trust game. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes for the public good game. Exploratory analyses suggest some effects of redistribution on productivity in a real-effort task, but these are not the main focus and do not pertain to public good game efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes for the public good game. The design and findings are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Luo, J., & Wang, X. X. (2023). Leave behind or migrate? Evidence from a field experiment in China concerning the impact of being left behind/migrating status on children's behaviour and preferences. *China Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study provides evidence that being left behind or migrating is associated with lower prosocial behavior (including cooperation in a public goods game) among children, with migrant children generally more negatively affected than left-behind children. The negative effects of migration dissipate over time and are less severe with shorter migration distances. Living with at least one parent mitigates some negative effects for left-behind children. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism in the public goods game or other tasks, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. While it uses a public goods game to measure cooperation, it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Del Ponte, A., Masiliunas, A., & Lim, N. (2025). Decentralized voluntary agreements do not reduce emissions in a climate change experiment. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: In a lab experiment with a public bads game (3 players, 18 rounds), the introduction of voluntary pledge-and-review agreements, automatic financial penalties for exceeding pledges, and/or peer evaluation (reputation contest) did not reduce average production or disaster risk compared to baseline. Most participants joined agreements and complied with their pledges, but pledges were not ambitious enough to reduce emissions. Financial penalties increased compliance but did not improve group efficiency, as participants could avoid penalties by not joining or by making unambitious pledges. Peer evaluation did not reduce production and even reduced the ambition of pledges. The results suggest that, in decentralized and voluntary settings, punishment mechanisms do not improve efficiency unless participation and ambition can be enforced.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a voluntary, decentralized public bads game with 3 players and 18 rounds, adding automatic financial penalties for exceeding pledges does not improve group efficiency or reduce emissions compared to baseline, because participants can avoid penalties by not joining or by making unambitious pledges. Peer evaluation (reputation-based non-monetary punishment) also does not improve efficiency and may reduce ambition. For prediction, this suggests that in similar voluntary, opt-in, pledge-based games, the presence of punishment mechanisms (even if automatic and with high penalty rates) will not increase efficiency unless there are mechanisms to ensure ambitious pledges and broad participation. Control efficiency is a good predictor of treatment efficiency when punishment is voluntary and avoidable.

- source: Heine, F., & Strobel, M. (2020). Reward and punishment in a team contest. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares reward and punishment mechanisms in a team contest game (with and without between-group competition). Groups of four play 15 rounds, deciding how much to contribute to a group lottery (contest) and then can reward or punish teammates at a cost. The main findings are: (1) Both punishment and reward mechanisms lead to over-contribution relative to Nash equilibrium, causing significant efficiency losses (rent dissipation). (2) Punishment does not increase efficiency; it reduces it, as punishing is costly and does not significantly increase contributions. (3) Rewarding increases contributions more than punishment, but is welfare-neutral (cost to giver equals benefit to receiver). (4) The contest environment (with an opponent group) leads to even greater inefficiency and lower average payoffs than the non-contest environment. (5) The R&P treatment (both reward and punishment available) in the contest environment is the least efficient, with the highest overspending and lowest mean income. (6) Receiving reward increases subsequent contributions, but punishment does not. (7) Overall, the presence of punishment does not solve the efficiency problem in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a team contest environment with public-good-like features, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may worsen it, especially when both reward and punishment are available. The effect of punishment is less positive (more negative) on efficiency compared to reward or baseline. The contest structure (between-group competition) amplifies inefficiency. For prediction, if the control game (no punishment) is already inefficient due to over-contribution, enabling punishment is likely to further reduce efficiency, not improve it. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, known MPCR, punishment cost and tech, no identification of punishers, no communication) are all specified and match standard lab contest/PGG designs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled in similar environments.

- source: Kanitsar, G. (2021). Self-Governance in Generalized Exchange. A Laboratory Experiment on the Structural Embeddedness of Peer Punishment. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment increases cooperation and group earnings (efficiency) in public good structures only when punishment is costless; costly punishment does not improve efficiency. In generalized exchange, peer punishment (both costly and costless) does not increase cooperation or group earnings. The effectiveness of punishment is thus highly contingent on the network structure: dense sanctioning networks (public good) allow punishment to deter defection and improve efficiency, while sparse networks (generalized exchange) do not. The cost-impact ratio of punishment is important, but its effect is moderated by the structural environment. The study provides direct evidence that the efficiency gains from peer punishment in public goods do not transfer to generalized exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the network structure. In standard public good games, costless punishment can substantially increase efficiency, but costly punishment may not. In generalized exchange (circular, sparse punishment network), enabling punishment—even if costless—does not improve efficiency. Thus, when predicting the efficiency effect of punishment, it is crucial to account for the density of the punishment network and whether the game is a standard public good or a generalized exchange. Control efficiency is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency when moving from public good to generalized exchange structures.

- source: Casari, M., & Tavoni, A. (2024). Climate clubs in the laboratory. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares several treatments, including a baseline public goods game (PGG), a version with punishment (PGGwP), and a 'climate club' treatment that combines conditional cooperation and mandatory punishment. The key finding is that adding punishment opportunities (and counter-punishment) does not significantly increase cooperation rates and leads to a statistically significant decrease in net earnings (efficiency) compared to the baseline. The climate club treatment increases cooperation rates but still results in lower net earnings than the baseline, due to the costs associated with punishment. The results are robust across different group compositions and treatments. The paper provides direct evidence that, in this design, punishment reduces efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a three-player, 30-round, binary-contribution public goods game with costly punishment and counter-punishment, enabling punishment reduces group efficiency (net earnings) compared to the control without punishment. The effect is robust and statistically significant. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, under similar design parameters (binary choice, no chat, known rounds, punishment cost per unit = 1, punishment effectiveness varies by target, no rewards), enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The paper also shows that the presence of counter-punishment does not mitigate this effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG environments.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Kingsley, D. C. (2021). Inequality and Peer Punishment in a Common-Pool Resource Experiment. *Strategic Behavior and the Environment*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Peer punishment in a nonlinear CPR game reduces extractions in both equal and unequal endowment settings, but its effect on efficiency (group earnings relative to the social optimum) depends on the distribution of endowments. In the equal endowment treatment, punishment leads to lower earnings due to high punishment costs and lack of coordination. In the unequal endowment treatment, punishment does not reduce average earnings and increases earnings for low-endowment participants, as groups coordinate around the Equal Earnings norm. Punishment is more targeted and less frequent in the unequal treatment, leading to higher efficiency. The effect of punishment is thus moderated by the presence of endowment inequality, which creates a focal point for coordination and more effective norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in CPR/public goods-like games depends on the distribution of endowments (inequality). When endowments are equal, punishment can reduce efficiency due to high punishment costs and poor coordination. When endowments are unequal, punishment is more effective, leading to higher efficiency, especially for low-endowment participants, as groups coordinate around a salient norm. For prediction, the presence of endowment heterogeneity should be considered a key moderator: punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when inequality is present and salient. Other design features (e.g., punishment technology, group size, number of rounds) are held constant in this study, so the main actionable insight is about the interaction between punishment and endowment inequality.

- source: DeCaro, D. A., DeCaro, M. S., Janssen, M. A., Lee, A., Graci, A., & Flener, D. (2024). Learning from regulatory failure: How Ostrom's restorative justice design principle helps naïve groups create wiser enforcement systems to overcome the tragedy of the commons. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Facilitated groups (those guided to discuss enforcement using restorative justice principles) achieved higher group efficiency (net tokens harvested) than unfacilitated groups. The highest efficiency was observed in groups that first experienced regulatory abuse (punishment without communication) and then received facilitation (PFN treatment). Groups that experienced regulatory failure but did not receive facilitation often failed to develop effective enforcement systems and had lower efficiency. The presence of coordinated punishment systems (as opposed to independent or no punishment) was associated with the highest group efficiency. The study demonstrates that both the structure of punishment opportunities and the process of group learning and facilitation are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like games depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the context in which it is introduced (e.g., prior experience with regulatory failure/abuse) and whether groups receive facilitation to discuss enforcement principles. In prediction tasks, the presence of facilitated discussion (restorative justice), the opportunity for communication, and the structure of the punishment system (cost, effectiveness, coordination) should be considered as moderators. The control efficiency (without punishment) is lower than the treatment efficiency (with punishment and facilitation), especially when groups experience regulatory abuse and are then guided to reflect and coordinate.
