The findings suggest that punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain institutional and learning conditions.

- source: Webb, J., & Foddy, M. (2004). Vested interests in the decision to resolve social dilemma conflicts. *Small Group Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The punishment rule (targeted sanctioning) leads to greater resource preservation (more trees left at the end of the game) compared to the equality rule (shared sanctioning), but the equality rule results in higher total group profit. Individual profit outcomes depend on access: high-access participants profit more under the equality rule, while low-access participants profit more under the punishment rule. Voting for structural change (switching sanctioning systems) is predicted by individual profit and fines, not by group-level resource preservation. The study highlights that the impact of sanctioning systems on efficiency and payoff is heterogeneous and depends on both the structure of the sanction and the distribution of resource access.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency and payoff in public-goods-like resource dilemmas depends on the design of the punishment system and the distribution of resource access. If efficiency is defined as resource preservation, targeted punishment (punishment rule) is more effective. If efficiency is defined as total group profit, shared punishment (equality rule) can yield higher payoffs. The results suggest that the effect of enabling punishment is not universally positive or negative for efficiency; it depends on the specific sanctioning mechanism and group heterogeneity. For prediction, the design of the punishment system (targeted vs. shared), the distribution of access, and the definition of efficiency are critical moderators.

- source: van Klingeren, F., & Buskens, V. (2024). Graduated sanctioning, endogenous institutions and sustainable cooperation in common-pool resources: An experimental test. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a dynamic common-pool resource (CPR) game with four players and repeated rounds, the introduction of sanctioning (punishment) mechanisms—either strict or graduated—leads to higher cooperation and resource sustainability compared to no sanctioning. Strict sanctioning (fixed, high-cost punishment) initially results in higher resource size and profit, but graduated sanctioning (punishment severity increases with repeated defection) leads to greater cooperation and lower appropriation over time, eventually closing the gap in resource size and profit. When groups can endogenously choose their sanctioning mechanism (via majority vote every five rounds), cooperation and resource outcomes improve further, especially in the long run. However, the differences in group and individual profit between sanctioning types are small and diminish over time. The study provides partial support for the effectiveness of graduated and endogenously chosen sanctioning, especially for long-term cooperation, but strict sanctioning may be more effective in short games or early rounds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated, dynamic CPR games with endogenous resource dynamics and mutual monitoring. The results suggest that both strict and graduated punishment mechanisms increase efficiency (resource sustainability and group profit) relative to no punishment. Strict punishment is more effective in the short term, but graduated punishment becomes equally or more effective over longer time horizons. Endogenously chosen punishment mechanisms (via group voting) further enhance cooperation and resource outcomes, though the effect on profit is small. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency, with the magnitude and time course depending on the punishment regime and whether the institution is endogenously chosen. The findings are most applicable to settings with 4 players, 40 rounds, continuous contribution/appropriation, no chat, and mutual monitoring.

- source: Milinski, M., & Marotzke, J. (2022). Economic experiments support Ostrom's polycentric approach to mitigating climate change. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a lab experiment with groups of 9 players, subdivided into 3 subgroups of 3, the opportunity for subgroups to impose costly sanctions on other subgroups (punishment treatment) did not increase the probability of reaching the collective target or improve group payoffs compared to a control without sanctions. Sanctioning was used frequently, but its costs reduced overall payoffs, and it failed to discipline free riders effectively because fair contributors who were also sanctioned reduced their own contributions. Thus, punishment in this polycentric, subgroup-based setting did not improve efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-goods-like collective-risk game with subgroup-level costly punishment, enabling punishment does not increase group efficiency or the probability of reaching the collective target, and may even reduce efficiency due to the costs of punishment and negative effects on fair contributors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar designs (polycentric, subgroup-based, punishment between subgroups, costly punishment), enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency over the control condition without punishment, and may even be detrimental.

- source: Robbett, A. (2019). Just ask? Preference revelation and lying in a public goods experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares four conditions in a repeated public goods game with private, randomly assigned types: (1) baseline voluntary contribution (VCM), (2) Mechanism (report type to a mechanism that enforces the socially optimal contribution for the reported type), (3) Revelation (report type to group members, then contribute voluntarily), and (4) Punishment (as Revelation, but with a costly peer punishment stage). The main findings are: (a) Mechanism and Revelation both increase cooperation and group efficiency relative to VCM, but Mechanism's effect fades over time; (b) Punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency compared to VCM, and group profits are actually lower in the Punishment condition; (c) Punishment is mainly used to sanction obviously dishonest reports, not under-contribution, and is too weak to incentivize higher contributions; (d) The presence of punishment undermines the positive effect of truthful reporting on cooperation seen in the Revelation condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with private, heterogeneous incentives and observable reports/contributions, adding a peer punishment mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio) does not increase efficiency or group payoffs compared to a baseline without punishment. In fact, efficiency is lower with punishment, likely due to underutilization and mis-targeting of sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar environments (3 players, 10 rounds, no chat, private types, punishment available after observing reports/contributions, punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency relative to control. The results also highlight that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the clarity of norms and the ability to identify free-riders; when these are ambiguous due to private information, punishment may backfire or be ineffective.

- source: Kamei, K., Putterman, L., & Tyran, J. R. (2023). Civic engagement, the leverage effect and the accountable state. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a large-group lab experiment, participants first face a public goods allocation problem (main stage) and can only enable a central punishment institution (which enforces optimal contributions) if enough group members voluntarily complete costly 'civic tasks' in a pre-stage (second-order dilemma). When the punishment institution is absent, group efficiency is low (public sector allocations rapidly decline to ~13% of the optimum). When the institution is present, allocations and group earnings approach the social optimum. The probability of achieving the institution is much higher when the cost of civic engagement is low and when social interaction is allowed. The study demonstrates that the presence of a punishment institution, conditional on sufficient civic engagement, robustly increases efficiency in public goods provision. The effect is strongest when the cost of civic engagement is low and when social feedback is available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a central punishment institution in a public-goods-like environment dramatically increases efficiency, moving group payoffs close to the social optimum. The effect is conditional on the institution being present, which depends on a prior (second-order) cooperation problem. The efficiency gain from punishment is robust across treatments, but the likelihood of achieving the institution (and thus the efficiency gain) is higher when the cost of enabling punishment is low and when social interaction is present. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, given a design where punishment is enabled (i.e., the institution is present), efficiency will be much higher than in the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is moderated by the cost of enabling punishment and by the presence of social interaction, but the direction is consistently positive for efficiency.

- source: Nikias, A., & Sy, A. (2021). Mutual monitoring in multi-period teams: an experimental investigation. *Team Performance Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that lowering the cost of punishment in a two-period team production game (with payoffs analogous to a prisoner's dilemma) increases both the frequency of punishment and the rate of working (cooperation) in both periods. When punishment is costless, participants are more likely to punish non-cooperators, and this threat leads to higher cooperation. The design ensures that higher working rates directly translate to higher group output and expected utility, so efficiency is higher when punishment is less costly. The effect is statistically significant for both periods. The study isolates the effect of punishment cost by holding other factors constant and using a one-shot horizon to rule out future gains from cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player, two-period team production game with mutual monitoring and contingent punishment, reducing the cost of punishment increases cooperation and thus group efficiency. The design is closely related to public goods and prisoner's dilemma games, and the mapping from working to group payoff is explicit. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that lower punishment cost (punishment_cost dimension) will lead to higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, holding other design features constant. The findings are most directly applicable to small, short-horizon, mutual monitoring settings without communication or rewards.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., Cherki, B., & Israel, S. (2024). Cooperation, punishment, and group change in multilevel public goods experiments. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment increases cooperation in standard (single-group) public goods games, sustaining high contribution levels and preventing decay. In multilevel public goods games (where participants can contribute to private, local, or global accounts), punishment only increases total and global contributions if participants previously played a single-group PGG. If the multilevel PGG is played first, punishment does not significantly increase cooperation or efficiency. The effect of punishment is thus moderated by the order in which group structures are experienced. Normative expectations and punishment patterns suggest that prior experience with single-group PGGs helps groups coordinate on efficient (global) cooperation norms in multilevel settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the structure of the game (single-group vs. multilevel) and the order in which participants experience these structures. For prediction, the presence of a multilevel structure and whether participants have prior experience with single-group PGGs are key moderators. In standard PGGs, punishment reliably increases efficiency. In multilevel PGGs, punishment only increases efficiency if participants have previously played a single-group PGG. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are clearly specified, and the absence of communication and anonymity of punishment are also relevant. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment will increase efficiency in single-group PGGs and in multilevel PGGs only if there is prior single-group experience.

- source: Amirova, I., Petrick, M., & Djanibekov, N. (2022). Investment traps and resilience to shocks: An experimental study of Central Asian collective water governance. *Irrigation and Drainage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat
  findings: The experiment found multiple stable equilibria (lock-ins) in collective investment in irrigation infrastructure. Communication (chat) treatments increased collective investment and made high-investment equilibria more likely, but did not guarantee them. Penalty (punishment) treatments, both low and high, reduced collective investment compared to baseline, contrary to theoretical expectations. The penalty treatments crowded out intrinsic motivation to cooperate, resulting in lower investment levels and less resilience to shocks. The main outcome is collective investment, which is a behavioral proxy for group efficiency, but the paper does not report explicit group payoffs or efficiency ratios.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public-good-like field experiment with real farmers, introducing punishment (penalties) can reduce collective investment and thus lower efficiency-related outcomes, compared to a baseline with no punishment. Communication (chat) increases investment, but does not guarantee high efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar field settings may decrease efficiency, especially when intrinsic motivation is important. The main outcome is collective investment, not explicit group payoff, but the relationship is close. The findings are most relevant for field settings with real-world participants and repeated games with investment as the public good.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., Lelieveld, G. J., De Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & Van Dijk, E. (2022). Applying a logic of appropriateness to understand behavioral differences between common resource dilemmas and public good dilemmas. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, holding the underlying payoff structure constant, participants are more cooperative in CRDs than in PGDs. Punishment is used more frequently in CRDs, while reward is used more frequently in PGDs. These effects are robust to changes in the framing of the action (taking/leaving/giving/keeping). However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how the willingness to punish and cooperate differs between CRDs and PGDs, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper does not provide direct evidence, but it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used in CRDs and that cooperation is higher in CRDs. The game design dimensions are well described, so the paper is useful for understanding behavioral responses to punishment opportunities, but not for predicting efficiency effects.

- source: Maeda, K., Kumai, Y., & Hashimoto, H. (2022). Potential influence of decision time on punishment behavior and its evaluation. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that participants assign more punishment under time pressure (intuition) than after deliberation, especially in one-shot PGG and second-party punishment games. However, punishers are evaluated more positively when their punishment is deliberative rather than intuitive, and when they are third-party (not direct victims). The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it does not directly inform how punishment affects efficiency in PGG-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in PGG or related games. It does, however, provide informative context about how punishment behavior and its social evaluation depend on decision time and the role of the punisher. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that the psychological and reputational consequences of punishment may depend on whether punishment is seen as deliberative or intuitive, and whether it is administered by a direct victim or a third party. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not measured, the paper cannot be used to calibrate the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions.

- source: Chen, S., Shi, J. C., Yang, X. L., Ye, H., & Luo, J. (2019). Modulating Activity in the Dorsolateral Prefrontal Cortex Changes Punishment in the 3-Player Prisoner's Dilemma: A Transcranial Direct Current Stimulation Study. *Frontiers in Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that modulating DLPFC activity via tDCS can significantly decrease the amount of punishment assigned by cooperators to defectors (both in second-party and third-party punishment), depending on the stimulation mode. One bilateral stimulation (left anodal/right cathodal) increases cooperation rates. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding neural mechanisms of punishment and cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or prisoner's dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, as it does not report these outcomes. It does, however, provide detailed evidence on how punishment behavior and cooperation rates are affected by neural modulation in a 3-player prisoner's dilemma with punishment. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment behavior can be modulated by factors unrelated to game design (e.g., neural stimulation), and that cooperation can be increased by certain interventions. However, without efficiency or payoff data, the paper's direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited.

- source: Liao, Y. L., Zhang, L., Lei, S. Y., Song, M. Z., Deng, W. K., & Hu, D. F. (2021). RETRACTED: Third-Party Punishment Mechanism and Corporate Cooperation in Environmental Investment: Experiments on Public Goods Game (Retracted Article). *Discrete Dynamics in Nature and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally compares a threshold public goods game with and without third-party punishment (TPP), using groups of three participants (framed as enterprises) making resource investment decisions toward a group threshold. The TPP mechanism automatically punishes group members whose investment rate is below the group mean. The presence of TPP leads to higher mean resource investment amounts, higher individual and group investment rates, and a substantially higher success rate of cooperation (from 55.57% to 76.85%). Regression analysis confirms that TPP has a significant positive effect on investment behavior, even after controlling for individual characteristics and measured preferences for interaction and equity (IEP).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a third-party punishment mechanism in a threshold public goods game increases group investment and the probability of achieving the public good. For prediction tasks, the presence of TPP (punishment enabled) should be expected to yield higher efficiency or group payoff than the control (punishment disabled), especially in small groups (n=3) with a threshold structure and automatic, impersonal punishment. The results are most directly applicable to threshold PGGs with automatic third-party punishment and no communication. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency-related outcomes under these design conditions.

- source: Angelsen, A., & Naime, J. (2024). The mixed impacts of peer punishments on common-pool resources: Multi-country experimental evidence. *World Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment in a CPR extraction game increases cooperation (reduces deforestation) when it is prosocial (targeting above-average extractors), but reduces cooperation when it is antisocial (targeting cooperators). The frequency and impact of punishment vary by country and by group endowment inequality, with Indonesia showing much higher punishment rates and stronger norm enforcement. The experiment does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but shows that punishment changes the marginal incentives for extraction and that prosocial punishment can make deviation from the group norm unprofitable. Antisocial punishment, however, undermines cooperation. The findings highlight the importance of cultural and institutional context for the effectiveness of peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of peer punishment on cooperation in CPR-like games depends on the type of punishment (prosocial vs. antisocial), the cultural context, and group heterogeneity. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that prosocial punishment increases cooperation and reduces extraction, which would be expected to increase efficiency in a standard PGG/CPR context. However, antisocial punishment can reduce cooperation and thus efficiency. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment for increasing efficiency is context-dependent and can be undermined by antisocial punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment can improve efficiency when prosocial, but that the presence of antisocial punishment and cultural context are important moderators. The lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Volk, S., Nguyen, H., & Thöni, C. (2019). Punishment under threat: The role of personality in costly punishment. *Journal of Research in Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals high in Honesty-Humility engage in costly punishment for prosocial reasons, independent of threat, while those high in Emotionality engage in costly punishment in response to perceived punishment threat. Agreeableness does not predict punishment behavior. The experiment uses a two-person PGG with a punishment stage where each participant can punish a member of another team and can be punished by a different member of a third team, ruling out direct reciprocity. The main outcomes are assigned and expected punishment, both measured as deduction points. The paper does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, but discusses that punishment under threat is likely inefficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and personality drivers of costly punishment in a PGG-like environment with punishment threat, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The design is a two-person PGG with continuous contributions, no communication, and a punishment stage where punishment is costly (1 token cost to punisher, 3 token loss to punished). The findings suggest that punishment can be driven by threat rather than prosocial motives, and the authors argue that such punishment is likely inefficient, but this is not empirically demonstrated. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that punishment under threat may not improve efficiency and may even reduce it, especially when driven by non-prosocial motives, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: McEvoy, D. M. (2012). Paying to punish on a free ride: member and nonmember punishments in voluntary coalitions. *Applied Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a public good game with voluntary coalition formation, allowing voluntary punishment (where any player can punish any other) does not increase public good provision relative to no punishment. However, when coalition members can credibly commit to punishing noncompliance (quasi-voluntary punishment), public good provision increases significantly. Both coalition members and nonmembers are equally likely to punish noncompliance, and the extent of nonmember punishment does not change when members can credibly enforce compliance. The main outcomes are contribution levels and punishment frequencies; efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that simply enabling voluntary punishment (without credible commitment) is not sufficient to increase efficiency or public good provision in coalition-based public good games. Only when punishment is structured so that coalition members can credibly commit to punishing noncompliance does cooperation (and likely efficiency) increase. For prediction, the presence of a credible, committed punishment mechanism is a key moderator; voluntary, non-committed punishment may have little or no effect on efficiency. The paper does not provide direct efficiency data, so predictions must be inferred from contribution outcomes.

- source: Chavez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2025). Social context, framing, and compliance with the law: experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that higher monitoring probabilities increase compliance in all treatments. Law framing increases compliance only in the strategic (public good) environment, and actually decreases compliance in the non-strategic environment where the law does not benefit others. Compliance is not higher when noncompliance imposes an externality on third parties. Compliance is higher in public good (strategic) environments than in non-strategic ones, especially when monitoring is weak. The main outcome is compliance, not efficiency or group payoff, so the results are most directly informative about behavioral responses to punishment rather than efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how punishment (monitoring and sanctions) affects compliance rates in public goods game-like environments, especially under different social framings. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and results are highly relevant for predicting compliance behavior, and the strategic (public good) treatments are close analogs to standard PGGs with punishment. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective at increasing compliance (and thus likely efficiency) when the public good benefits group members, especially under weak enforcement. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Shuvo, M. F. R., & Kabir, K. M. A. (2024). Exploring cyclic dominance in rock-paper-scissor strategies within the Optional Prisoner's Dilemma: A quantitative analysis with environmental feedback. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment increases cooperation in one-shot, three-person PD games, both when imposed exogenously (costless) and endogenously (costly to the punisher). The effect of punishment is decomposed into a direct effect (altering incentives for the punished individual) and an indirect effect (raising expectations that others will cooperate, thus encouraging conditional cooperators). Both effects are of similar magnitude. In the endogenous punishment condition, the cost of punishment is small relative to the benefit from increased cooperation, resulting in a net positive benefit. However, the main outcome is contribution, not group efficiency or total payoff, though some calculations of net benefit are provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (even when costly to the punisher) increases cooperation in public-goods-like environments, and that the effect is due to both direct and indirect mechanisms. While efficiency (as defined by group payoff relative to maximum possible) is not directly reported, the paper's calculations of net benefit and cost-effectiveness suggest that punishment increases group welfare. The design is a one-shot, three-player, continuous-contribution PD with and without punishment, and with both exogenous and endogenous punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when both direct and indirect effects are present, and when punishment is not prohibitively costly. However, the lack of direct efficiency reporting means that predictions must be inferred from contribution and net benefit data.

- source: Selles, J., Bonhommeau, S., Guillotreau, P., & Vallee, T. (2020). Can the Threat of Economic Sanctions Ensure the Sustainability of International Fisheries? An Experiment of a Dynamic Non-cooperative CPR Game with Uncertain Tipping Point. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Introducing an endogenous economic sanction (a fixed cost triggered by resource depletion below a threshold) in a dynamic CPR game leads to more cooperative behavior, less overexploitation, and higher average resource stocks compared to a baseline with no sanction. The effect is even stronger when the location of the threshold is uncertain. However, while sanctions reduce the frequency of myopic (selfish) strategies, many groups still fail to avoid the threshold, and only a few achieve near-optimal resource management. Average group profit is lower and more variable in sanction treatments due to the high penalty for crossing the threshold, but overall, the threat of sanctions shifts behavior toward greater cooperation and less rent dissipation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (here, an economic sanction triggered by overexploitation) in a dynamic, non-cooperative CPR game increases cooperative behavior and reduces overexploitation, which should translate into higher efficiency relative to the baseline. However, the effect on group profit is nuanced: while sanctions reduce overexploitation, groups that cross the threshold incur large losses, and only a minority achieve near-optimal outcomes. For prediction, the presence of a punishment/sanction mechanism is likely to increase efficiency on average, but the effect may be heterogeneous depending on group behavior and the risk of triggering the sanction. The game design here is a dynamic CPR with 3 players, no chat, continuous contribution (harvest), and a punishment that is endogenous and collective (not peer-to-peer). The findings are most informative for similar dynamic CPR or threshold public goods settings.

- source: Dave, C., Hamre, S., Kephart, C., & Reuben, A. (2020). Subjects in the lab, activists in the field: public goods and punishment. *Eurasian Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that both lab and field (activist) subjects respond to incentives in a public goods context with punishment. Activists are more likely to punish non-cooperative behavior, even when it is costly and probabilistic, and are more likely to settle at the Nash equilibrium when firms increase their public good contributions. Higher MPCR leads to higher contributions and more frequent punishment. The main behavioral drivers are inequity aversion and negative reciprocity. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome, so the effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how punishment is used in a public-goods-like environment, especially with a costly, probabilistic punishment mechanism. It shows that punishment is frequently used and is responsive to MPCR and fairness concerns. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and the likelihood of punishment under different design parameters (e.g., MPCR, subject pool), but not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Samid, Y., & Suleiman, R. (2008). Effectiveness of Coercive and Voluntary Institutional Solutions to Social Dilemmas. *New Issues and Paradigms in Research on Social Dilemmas*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that introducing a moderately coercive authority (with moderate fines for non-participation and moderate fees for participation) increases the rate of authority support and contributions compared to non-coercive or exploitative authority. However, when coercion becomes excessive (high fees and/or fines), groups may still converge to high authority support, but this does not necessarily improve efficiency, as the cost of coercion can outweigh the benefits. The non-coercive authority is ineffective unless social comparison is reduced (partial feedback), in which case cooperation increases. The main findings are about behavioral convergence to equilibria (all-defect, partial authority, all-authority) rather than direct efficiency or group payoff. The results highlight the importance of balancing the cost and effectiveness of coercive mechanisms to avoid undermining the benefits of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for predicting the effect of punishment (coercive authority) on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that moderate punishment (moderate fines and fees) can increase cooperation and authority support, which are likely to improve efficiency, but excessive punishment (high fees/fines) can lead to high compliance at the cost of efficiency, as the costs may outweigh the benefits. The paper does not report direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so predictions must be inferred from behavioral outcomes (contribution rates, authority support) and the structure of the payoff matrices. The findings also indicate that feedback about others' payoffs (social comparison) can reduce the effectiveness of non-coercive mechanisms. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that moderate, mutually agreed punishment is most likely to improve efficiency, while excessive punishment may not.

- source: Selterman, D. (2019). Altruistic Punishment in the Classroom: An Update on the Tragedy of the Commons Extra Credit Question. *Teaching of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that adding an altruistic punishment option (students can sacrifice their own points to remove points from a random over-consumer) increases the rate at which groups successfully avoid the 'tragedy of the commons' (i.e., more classes stay under the 10% threshold and receive points) compared to prior years without punishment. However, the study does not directly measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The main findings are that the punishment option increases cooperation (fewer students choose the selfish option), and that students report positive attitudes and increased understanding of social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a threshold public goods dilemma can increase group cooperation and the likelihood of group success (i.e., receiving the reward). However, because the outcome is binary (group gets points or not) and not a continuous efficiency or payoff measure, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The evidence suggests that punishment can improve group outcomes in similar all-or-nothing, threshold-based social dilemmas, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency change. The design is also classroom-based and not a standard repeated PGG, so caution is warranted in generalizing to other settings.

- source: Yang, H. B., Zhang, Y. K., Lyu, Y. R., & Tang, C. M. (2023). Group bias under uncertain environment: A perspective of third-party punishment. *Acta Psychologica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is harsher under environmental uncertainty than under certainty. Out-group members are punished more than in-group members (out-group derogation), and in-group favoritism is observed when violations are clear. The amount of punishment increases with the severity of the harvest (i.e., the more a player takes, the more they are punished). Gender of the third-party also moderates punishment patterns. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how punishment behavior is affected by environmental uncertainty, group affiliation, and gender in a CRD setting similar to a public goods game. However, because it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., & Singer, T. (2021). Cooperation across multiple game theoretical paradigms is increased by fear more than anger in selfish individuals. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: Induced fear, compared to anger, increases cooperation (as measured by a latent factor across multiple games, including the public good game) in individuals with selfish value orientations, bringing their cooperation levels up to those of prosocial individuals. No significant effect of emotion induction is found on punishment behavior. The effect is robust to controls for mood, risk attitudes, and other confounds. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that emotional context (fear vs. anger) can moderate cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially among selfish individuals. However, because the outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment) rather than efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment (i.e., enabling/disabling punishment), but rather includes punishment games as part of a behavioral factor. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that context and individual differences can moderate cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Zhosan, D., & Gardner, R. (2013). Problems of the commons: group behavior, cooperation and sanctioning in a two-harbor experiment. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment simulating a two-commons (two-harbor) resource dilemma, groups of 8 participants (students and professional fishermen) repeatedly chose how to allocate effort between two commons and a safe alternative. The experiment varied institutional features across blocks: baseline (no restrictions), enforcement (costly movement between commons), communication, and communication plus sanctioning. Efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) was the primary outcome. Communication led to large, significant increases in efficiency for all groups, with average net efficiency rising from ~0.8 to ~0.98. The addition of a sanctioning mechanism (warnings, then monetary penalties for repeated defection) further increased efficiency in most groups, sometimes reaching 1.0. The effect of sanctioning was generally positive but smaller than the effect of communication, and in one group, sanctioning led to a breakdown in cooperation. The results show that communication and sanctioning can both be effective in increasing efficiency in commons dilemmas, with communication having the largest and most consistent effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication among group members in a commons dilemma can dramatically increase efficiency, even before any formal or monetary sanctioning is introduced. The addition of a sanctioning mechanism (warnings and monetary penalties) yields further, though more modest, efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, the presence of communication and/or sanctioning should be expected to increase efficiency substantially relative to a control with neither. The effect of communication is robust and large; the effect of sanctioning is positive but may be context-dependent and sometimes less pronounced. The experiment's design and reporting allow for direct mapping to game design dimensions relevant for prediction.

- source: Hsu, L. C. (2013). TAX AUDITING AS A PUBLIC GOOD GAME: AN EXPERIMENTAL STUDY ON PUNISHMENT AND COMPLIANCE. *Pacific Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The endogenous-audit (collective punishment) treatment produces much higher compliance rates than both the no-audit and random-audit treatments. In the final 10 rounds, compliance rates are 0.297 (no audit), 0.758 (random audit), and 0.921 (endogenous audit). However, average earnings are lower in the endogenous-audit treatment (85.2 points) than in the random-audit treatment (92.1 points), due to the distribution of fines and discovered taxes. Audit productivity (e.g., reported tax per audit) is higher in the endogenous-audit treatment in the latter half of the game. The main effect of punishment is to increase compliance, but the effect on efficiency (group payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported and may be ambiguous due to the cost of monitoring and the way fines are pooled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a collective punishment mechanism (endogenous audit) in a public-goods-like tax game increases compliance rates compared to both no-punishment and exogenous punishment (random audit) conditions. However, the effect on efficiency (group payoff relative to the full cooperation benchmark) is not directly reported and may be ambiguous, as average earnings are lower in the endogenous-audit treatment than in the random-audit treatment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment can increase compliance but may not always increase efficiency, depending on the cost structure and how punishment/fines are redistributed. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, audit cost structure, etc.) are well specified and can be mapped to the prediction task, but the lack of direct efficiency analysis is a limitation.

- source: Littman, R., Estrada, S., Stagnaro, M. N., Dunham, Y., Rand, D., & Baskin-Sommers, A. (2020). Community Violence and Prosociality: Experiencing and Committing Violence Predicts Norm-Enforcing Punishment but Not Cooperation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a high-violence urban US sample, exposure to and commission of violence are not associated with increased cooperation in economic games (including a Public Goods Game), but are positively associated with norm-enforcing punishment (i.e., willingness to pay to punish selfish others in economic games). The association is specific to norm-enforcing punishment and not to antisocial punishment or general willingness to punish. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it does provide indirect evidence that, in a US urban context, individuals with higher exposure to or commission of violence are more likely to engage in norm-enforcing punishment, but not more likely to cooperate. This suggests that the presence of punishment may not increase cooperation or efficiency in similar populations, but the study does not test the causal effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The design and results are most relevant for understanding individual differences in punishment and cooperation, not for predicting treatment effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Loukopoulos, P., Eek, D., Gärling, T., & Fujii, S. (2006). Palatable punishment in real-world social dilemmas? Punishing others to increase cooperation among the unpunished. *Journal of Applied Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that intermittent punishment (where only some group members are punished for defection in a given round) increases cooperation rates among those not directly punished, compared to a no-punishment baseline. This 'spillover effect' is robust to changes in the framing of the decision (individual vs. group focus) and to information about prior cooperation by others. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the payoff structure is such that increased cooperation would increase group earnings. The findings suggest that intermittent punishment can promote cooperation even among those not directly targeted by sanctions, and that this effect is not simply due to general factors that increase cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing intermittent punishment in a public-goods-like sequential social dilemma increases cooperation rates among unpunished group members, compared to no punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The findings suggest that even partial or probabilistic punishment can have positive behavioral effects on cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if the payoff structure rewards cooperation. The robustness of the spillover effect to framing and prior cooperation manipulations suggests that the effect of punishment on cooperation is not easily moderated by these factors. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that introducing punishment (even if not universal) can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Pedrazzini, S., Gurtner, L. M., Aggrey, V., & Moser, S. (2025). Exploring individual and organizational factors influencing cooperation in commons: a scoping review. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The review finds that both individual (e.g., social identification, trust, values) and organizational (e.g., incentives, communication, social norms, anonymity) factors significantly influence cooperation in commons and social dilemma settings. Regarding punishment, meta-analyses and experimental studies included in the review show that punishment systems reliably increase cooperation rates, but may reduce group earnings or efficiency due to the cost of punishment, especially in early rounds. Reward systems also increase cooperation and tend to result in higher group payoffs than punishment. The review notes that the effectiveness of punishment depends on factors such as cost, group homogeneity, information accuracy, and cultural context. However, the review does not provide quantitative estimates or systematic synthesis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation as the main dependent variable.
  prediction_guidance: This review is useful for identifying which game design dimensions are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation, such as the presence and cost of punishment, group size, communication, and information structure. It provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation, but only indirect and mixed evidence about its effect on efficiency or group payoff, with some included studies noting that punishment can reduce group earnings due to its cost. For the downstream prediction task, this review suggests that enabling punishment will likely increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency will depend on the costliness of punishment, the possibility of antisocial punishment, and whether rewards are also available. The review does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Billinger, S., & Rosenbaum, S. M. (2019). Discretionary mechanisms and cooperation in hierarchies: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment compares a baseline PGG (no hierarchy) to four HPGG treatments varying in whether managers can contribute (discretionary contribution: present/absent) and whether managers can allocate returns (discretionary return: static/dynamic). All HPGG treatments yield higher average contributions and accumulated earnings than the baseline, indicating that hierarchy and managerial discretion can sustain cooperation and improve efficiency. However, when managers have full discretion over returns but cannot contribute (AD treatment), both contributions and worker earnings are lowest among HPGG treatments, showing that unchecked managerial appropriation undermines efficiency. When managers can contribute, both group earnings and cooperation are higher and more stable over time. Managers consistently earn more than workers, especially in dynamic return treatments, due to 'strategic luring'—managers initially reward workers to elicit contributions, then appropriate more in later rounds. The design of discretionary mechanisms thus crucially shapes both efficiency and the distribution of surplus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing hierarchical mechanisms (managerial discretion over inputs and/or outputs) in a PGG-like environment increases group efficiency relative to a standard PGG baseline. However, the efficiency gains depend on the specific design: allowing managers to contribute and/or restricting their ability to appropriate returns leads to higher and more sustained efficiency. If managers can appropriate returns without contributing, efficiency and worker payoffs suffer. For prediction, the presence of hierarchy and the structure of managerial discretion are key moderators: enabling managerial contribution and limiting appropriation both support higher efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially in settings with hierarchical or leader-follower structures.

- source: Cox, C. A., & Stoddard, B. (2024). Inequality and the allocation of collective goods. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment compares standard linear public goods games (equal-share) to treatments where a third-party allocator divides the collective good among contributors. The allocator mechanism increases efficiency in all cases, but the effect is strongest in homogeneous groups. When group members differ in endowments or productivity, the allocator's ability to boost efficiency is significantly diminished, though still positive relative to the equal-share baseline. Allocators generally reward contributors in proportion to their relative effort (percentage of endowment contributed), but in heterogeneous groups, conflicting fairness ideals make it harder to sustain high efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that flexible allocation mechanisms can substitute for punishment/reward institutions in promoting efficiency, but their effectiveness is moderated by group heterogeneity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for flexible allocation of the collective good (by a third-party allocator) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when groups are homogeneous. However, the efficiency boost is reduced in the presence of heterogeneity in endowments or productivity. For prediction tasks, the presence of an allocator (as a form of centralized sanctioning/reward) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to equal-share baselines, but the magnitude of the effect will be smaller in heterogeneous groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with allocation mechanisms, and indirectly relevant for understanding the limits of punishment/reward institutions in heterogeneous environments.

- source: Gaudeul, A., Crosetto, P., & Riener, G. (2017). Better stuck together or free to go? Of the stability of cooperation when individuals have outside options. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment varied the ease of exit from a public project (barriers to exit) in a repeated, two-player, stochastic public-good-like game. When exit was easier (lower barriers), participants exited more frequently, but this did not increase efficiency or average payoffs. In fact, average individual welfare was lower in treatments with lower barriers to exit. The main reason was excessive exit, driven by pessimistic beliefs about the peer's likelihood of leaving, rather than accurate cost-benefit analysis. The study finds that exit is used both as a response to perceived exploitation and as a punishment for failure, but these mechanisms do not improve group efficiency. The findings suggest that making exit easier undermines group stability and reduces welfare, contrary to some theoretical expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-good-like environments with imperfect monitoring and stochastic outcomes, making exit easier (i.e., lowering barriers to exit) reduces efficiency and average payoffs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of exit options (a form of sanction or punishment via exclusion/self-exclusion) on efficiency. The results suggest that, unless exit is tightly controlled or costly, its availability will likely decrease group welfare due to excessive and inefficient use. For prediction tasks, the presence and ease of exit should be treated as a negative moderator of efficiency, even when the control (no-exit) game already has moderate efficiency. The study does not test classic costly punishment (e.g., deduction points), but its findings on exit as a sanction are highly informative for settings where exit or exclusion is a possible response to non-cooperation.

- source: Kusterer, D. J., & Schmitz, P. W. (2020). Public goods, property rights, and investment incentives: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that ownership structure affects investment incentives and efficiency in public-good provision, but not always as predicted by theory. When the investor values the public good more, giving them ownership increases investment and group surplus. When the non-investor values the public good more, giving them ownership does not increase investment or surplus, because the investor-owner sometimes withholds the public good to punish the other party, reducing efficiency. When the possibility of such punishment is removed (by exogenously enforcing provision), investment and efficiency fall in line with theoretical predictions. Thus, the efficiency effect of ownership and the potential for punishment are intertwined, and the ability to punish by withholding provision can reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency effect of institutional design (ownership allocation) in public-good games depends on whether players can punish by withholding provision. In environments where the owner can withhold the public good as punishment, efficiency may be lower than predicted by standard theory, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is high. If the design disables such punishment (e.g., by enforcing provision), efficiency aligns with theoretical predictions. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of punishment opportunities (even if not explicit costly punishment) is a key moderator. The findings are most relevant for two-player, sequential, bargaining-based public-good games with binary investment and provision decisions.

- source: Herne, K., Kuyper, J., & Lappalainen, O. (2023). The Mode of Communication as a Driver of Sustainable and Equitable Asymmetric Common Pool Resource Use. *Environmental Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both unstructured and structured (deliberative) communication robustly increase group contributions and the efficiency of resource generation in an asymmetric common pool resource game, compared to a no-communication baseline. Deliberation (structured communication) reduces the impact of player position on contributions, leading to more equitable participation, but does not significantly increase total efficiency or equality of extraction compared to unstructured communication. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that communication—both unstructured and structured—substantially increases efficiency in asymmetric common pool resource games relative to a no-communication baseline. However, it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The design and results are highly relevant for understanding the effects of communication and deliberation, but not for punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, though it does establish a high-efficiency baseline for games with communication.

- source: Cagala, T., Glogowsky, U., Grimm, V., & Rincke, J. (2019). PUBLIC GOODS PROVISION WITH RENT-EXTRACTING ADMINISTRATORS. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study introduces a 'public trust game' (PTG), a repeated public goods game with an administrator who can extract rents from the contribution pool. The main finding is that the presence of a rent-extracting administrator reduces contributions and efficiency, but this is entirely explained by the reduction in MPCR (i.e., the efficiency of the public good). When comparing the PTG to a standard PGG with the same (lowered) MPCR, there is no significant difference in contributions or efficiency. The dynamics show that rent-extraction shocks and cooperation shocks only have short-run effects; the system returns to its previous level of cooperation and efficiency. The volatility of contributions is mainly driven by contributors' own behavior, not by the administrator. Groups with higher trust and cooperative attitudes experience less short-run volatility in cooperation after shocks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments where an administrator can extract rents, the negative effect on efficiency is fully mediated by the reduction in MPCR. If the control game (standard PGG) and the treatment game (with administrator) have the same MPCR, the presence of the administrator does not further reduce efficiency. Thus, for prediction, the key moderator is the effective MPCR: punishment or rent extraction that reduces MPCR will reduce efficiency accordingly, but the mere presence of an administrator or rent-extraction opportunity does not further depress efficiency beyond this. The paper does not study punishment in the standard sense (peer or centralized costly punishment), but its findings are highly informative for cases where punishment or corruption reduces the effective return from cooperation.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., Puerto, S., & Velez, M. A. (2021). Regrouping to Reduce Overfishing: Evidence from a Series of Lab-in-the-Field Experiments in Mexico. *Marine Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a framed CPR lab-in-the-field experiment with real fishermen, regrouping participants by their past extraction levels (publicly ranking and then reassigning groups) leads to a significant reduction in individual and group extraction, both in the observation period (after announcement) and after regrouping. The effect is strongest for the most sustainable group but is present across all groups. Compliance with the quota increases, especially in the most sustainable group. Other interventions (social disapproval, venting, moral priming) do not reduce extraction. The control group shows no reduction in extraction. The results suggest that symbolic recognition and group reassignment can reduce overexploitation and improve compliance, indirectly improving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that regrouping (a form of symbolic reward and group reassignment based on past behavior) can reduce overexploitation in CPR games, even in field-relevant settings. However, it does not test costly punishment or direct sanctioning mechanisms. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of non-costly, symbolic, or reputational interventions rather than monetary punishment. The game design is close to a public goods game but is a CPR extraction game. Efficiency is not directly measured, but reductions in extraction and increased compliance imply improved group welfare. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that interventions leveraging social recognition and group identity can have positive effects on efficiency, especially when punishment is weak or absent.

- source: Buso, I. M., Di Cagno, D., Gueth, W., & Spadoni, L. (2025). Voluntary Partnerships for Equally Sharing Contribution Costs. *B E Journal of Theoretical Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Voluntary cost-sharing partnerships in a PGG-like environment significantly increase average contributions and prevent the typical decay of cooperation over repeated rounds. Partners contribute more than outsiders, and the presence of the partnership mechanism (whether endogenous or exogenous) leads to higher group contributions and payoffs than the standard PGG. The effect is strongest for larger partnerships. The mechanism does not involve punishment or reward, but rather a self-commitment to cost-sharing. Efficiency (in terms of group payoff relative to the social optimum) is not directly reported, but the data show that average contributions are about 50% of the endowment with partnerships, compared to about 32% in the standard PGG. The voluntary nature of partnership formation has a modest additional effect, especially for small partnerships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional mechanisms allowing voluntary cost-sharing partnerships can substantially increase group contributions and payoffs in PGG-like settings, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. For prediction tasks, the presence of such a partnership mechanism should be expected to yield higher efficiency than standard PGGs with similar MPCR and group size. The effect is robust to whether partnership formation is endogenous or exogenous, except for very small partnerships. The findings are most directly relevant for settings where cost-sharing or coalition formation is possible, but do not directly inform about punishment effects. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, other institutional mechanisms can also enhance efficiency, and that the design dimensions (player count, MPCR, endogenous/exogenous group formation) interact with the mechanism's effectiveness.

- source: Chen, J., Fonseca, M. A., & Grimshaw, S. B. (2021). When a nudge is (not) enough: Experiments on social information and incentives. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In the large-N binary prisoners' dilemma, both financial incentives (reward or fine) and positive social information (majority invest) increase cooperation rates among unmotivated types. Negative social information (majority keep) reduces cooperation among motivated types. The combination of incentives and positive social information does not produce additional gains for unmotivated types, and may even crowd out intrinsic motivation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results imply that punishment/reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates, especially among those not intrinsically motivated to cooperate. The effect of social information is context-dependent: it is more effective when the externality of cooperation is salient and financially quantifiable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of punishment/reward mechanisms and social information on cooperation rates in a large-N binary prisoners' dilemma with MPCR = 0.05. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the increase in cooperation rates from punishment/reward treatments among unmotivated types suggests that enabling punishment/reward would likely increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the externality is salient. The design dimensions (large group, binary choice, no chat, no explicit payoff feedback beyond own outcome, known group size, no identification of punishers) are all specified. The absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction, but the qualitative direction is clear: punishment/reward increases cooperation and likely efficiency, especially among defectors.

- source: Harrell, A., & Traeger, M. L. (2025). Reputation-based reciprocity in human-bot and human-human networks. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The presence of bots in indirect reciprocity networks reduces human generosity, even when bots are programmed to be strong reciprocators. This reduction is driven by humans' reluctance to help bots, due to weaker norms about bots deserving help, not by a lack of reputational concern. Both 'reputational giving' (giving when observed by a third party) and 'rewarding reputations' (helping those with a reputation for helping) are less effective when bots are present. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the reduction in giving implies lower group welfare in hybrid networks compared to human-only networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of non-human agents (bots) in indirect reciprocity games reduces generosity and, by implication, group welfare. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that social context (e.g., presence of bots) can moderate the effectiveness of reciprocity-based mechanisms, but does not provide direct evidence about punishment or efficiency. The design and findings may be informative for understanding how social norms and recipient identity affect cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Stoddard, B. V., & Walker, J. M. (2019). The market for talent: Competition for resources and self-governance in teams. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Competition for a common-member's contributions creates 'winners' and 'losers': groups that start with higher contributions retain the common-member's loyalty and sustain higher contributions, while low-contribution groups decline further.
