Providing dedicated-members with more information about the common-member's contributions increases the common-member's total contributions and reduces discrimination between groups, but does not significantly raise dedicated-members' contributions in low-performing groups. Allowing ostracism (costless exclusion by majority vote) leads to higher and more equal contributions across groups, as both common- and dedicated-members increase contributions and the threat of exclusion disciplines low contributors. Ostracism is targeted at low contributors and rarely used against high contributors. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (ostracism) and information on contribution behavior in public goods games with divided loyalties. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that ostracism (a form of endogenous, costless group-level punishment) can increase and equalize contributions across groups, especially in settings where initial disparities would otherwise persist. The results also show that information about others' contributions can increase contributions by pivotal members. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling ostracism or increasing transparency may improve group outcomes in public goods games, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Gomez-Ruiz, L., & Sánchez-Expósito, M. J. (2020). The Impact of Team Identity and Gender on Free-Riding Responses to Fear and Cooperation Sustainability. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that strong team identity significantly reduces free-riding responses to fear and increases cooperation rates among men, but not among women. The effect of team identity on cooperation sustainability (maintenance of cooperation over time) is marginally significant for men and not significant for women. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, free-riding responses), not efficiency or group payoff. No formal punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present; the study focuses on informal control (team identity) and gender differences. The findings suggest that team identity can be an effective informal mechanism to sustain cooperation among men in repeated social dilemmas with binary contribution choices and feedback, but not among women.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It studies a repeated binary-choice social dilemma (close to a public goods game) without any punishment or reward mechanism. The main manipulations are team identity and gender. The outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, free-riding responses), not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does provide contextual evidence about the role of informal mechanisms (team identity) and gender in sustaining cooperation. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in binary-choice, feedback-rich, no-punishment settings, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Tan, J. H. W., & Bolle, F. (2023). Intragroup punishment and intergroup conflict aversion weaken intragroup cooperation in finitely repeated games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that intragroup cooperation is sensitive to the presence of intergroup rivalry and the payoff structure. Cooperation is lower when it inflicts losses on rivals, even when socially efficient, and higher when synergy within the group is increased. Participants use dynamic strategies, such as punishing partner defection with unyielding defection, but do not respond similarly to rival actions. Outgroup altruism (conflict aversion) further reduces cooperation that would harm rivals. Cooperation rates decline over time due to these mechanisms. The study does not include a costly punishment stage or analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The 'punishment' discussed is behavioral (trigger strategies, unyielding defection) rather than a costly punishment mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding how dynamic strategies and group-specific preferences affect cooperation, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to the introduction of a punishment option. The game structure is close to a PGG, but the absence of a costly punishment treatment and lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: De Luca, G., Sekeris, P. G., & Spengler, D. E. (2018). Can violence harm cooperation? Experimental evidence. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments: a 'conflict' treatment where violent appropriation (chance) is a viable option when resources are low, and a 'control' treatment where the cost of appropriation is so high that it is never optimal. In the conflict treatment, participants are significantly less likely to choose the cooperative extraction rate ('low') and more likely to choose the non-cooperative rate ('high') in early rounds, anticipating future conflict. The expectation of future conflict thus undermines cooperation even before conflict occurs. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral shift from cooperation to non-cooperation implies lower efficiency in the conflict treatment. Additional analyses show that experiencing conflict in previous games further reduces cooperation in subsequent games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a credible, destructive appropriation/conflict option in a dynamic CPR game reduces cooperative behavior and likely reduces efficiency, even before conflict is actually chosen. For prediction tasks, the presence of a credible destructive sanction (even if not a standard punishment mechanism) should be expected to reduce efficiency relative to a control with no such option. However, the paper does not study standard punishment (costly peer punishment for norm violations) but rather a destructive appropriation/conflict option, so the results are most relevant for settings where punishment is destructive and ends the game, not for standard public goods games with ongoing punishment. The findings suggest that the threat of destructive sanctions can unravel cooperation and efficiency, especially when the sanction is credible and not prohibitively costly.

- source: Gürdal, M. Y., Torul, O., & Vostroknutov, A. (2020). Norm compliance, enforcement, and the survival of redistributive institutions. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in the absence of enforcement, contributions to redistribution decay rapidly due to free riding, even among individuals with a high propensity to follow rules. Exclusion (random checks with exclusion for non-compliance) slows the decay but does not prevent it. Only full enforcement (computer-enforced contributions) sustains high contributions and reduces post-tax income inequality. Rule-following individuals initially try to follow the rules but eventually reduce contributions in response to free riding. Rule-followers prefer institutions with strong rules when enforcement is present, but switch to unregulated institutions when enforcement is absent and free riding is prevalent. The paper reports post-tax income and Gini coefficients, but does not directly analyze efficiency as group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum. The findings suggest that some form of enforcement is necessary to sustain redistribution and prevent the collapse of cooperative institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of enforcement (a form of exogenous punishment) on group outcomes in public-goods-like environments. While it does not implement a classic costly punishment mechanism, it shows that enforcement (computer-enforced contributions) is necessary to sustain high contributions and reduce inequality; exclusion as a sanction slows but does not prevent decay. In the absence of enforcement, contributions and redistribution collapse. The results suggest that, in similar designs, enabling effective enforcement or punishment is likely to increase group welfare and reduce inequality, but the paper does not report efficiency in the sense of group payoff relative to the full cooperation benchmark. The findings are most informative for settings where enforcement is exogenous and not costly to the enforcer. The evidence is less directly applicable to classic peer punishment with explicit costs. Control efficiency (without enforcement) is low due to free riding; treatment efficiency (with enforcement) is likely much higher, but the exact ratio is not reported.

- source: Mitterbacher, K., Palan, S., & Fleiss, J. (2024). Intergroup cooperation in the lab: asymmetric power relations and redistributive policies. *Empirica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in an asymmetric intergroup setting, the threat or implementation of discriminatory redistribution (a form of institutionalized sanction) reduces cooperation by the disadvantaged group (migrants). When citizens choose equal sharing, migrants are more likely to contribute, and when migrants contribute, citizens are less likely to vote for discrimination. This reciprocity can lead to mutually beneficial cooperation, but discrimination or lack of cooperation can trigger downward spirals. The mere possibility of discrimination reduces cooperation compared to a baseline where only equal sharing is possible. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution and voting rates), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, discriminatory redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like games with asymmetric power. The findings suggest that institutionalized sanctions (discriminatory redistribution) can reduce cooperation and thus would likely reduce efficiency, especially when the disadvantaged group perceives the system as unfair. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. The design and behavioral results are informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment or sanctions may backfire in asymmetric settings, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Cavalcanti, C. (2020). On the Determinants of Denouncing Illegal Fishing: A Field Study in Artisanal Fishing Communities. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that fishermen who are more impatient (prefer immediate rewards in a lab task) and those who are more pro-social (contribute more in a public goods game) are more likely to report a willingness to denounce others for illegal fishing. The willingness to denounce is interpreted as a form of adaptive altruistic punishment, but is measured via self-report rather than observed behavior. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare are reported. The public goods game is used only as a measure of pro-sociality, not as a treatment or outcome. The study provides correlational evidence on individual differences in punishment propensity, not on the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate punishment as a game feature, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. Instead, it provides correlational evidence that individual differences in impatience and pro-sociality predict willingness to denounce norm violations in a real-world resource dilemma. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that individual differences may moderate punishment behavior, but not providing evidence on the efficiency consequences of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Tambunlertchai, K., & Pongkijvorasin, S. (2020). The impacts of collective threshold requirements for rewards in a CPR experiment. *Environmental Economics and Policy Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Introducing collective-threshold-based rewards in a CPR game with villagers in Thailand leads to lower resource extraction and higher payoffs compared to the control (no reward) condition. The strict and moderate threshold treatments are most effective at reducing extraction and increasing payoffs, but differ in cost efficiency and equity: strict thresholds are most cost-efficient but induce free-riding and high payoff variance, while moderate thresholds are nearly as effective but yield more equitable payoff distributions. Relaxed thresholds are less effective. Increasing the reward size does not affect long-run extraction or payoffs, but accelerates the achievement of group targets. The study provides direct evidence on how reward design (threshold and size) affects efficiency and payoff outcomes in CPR settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in CPR games with collective-threshold-based rewards (but no punishment), efficiency and group payoffs increase relative to control. The effect is moderated by the threshold level: strict and moderate thresholds are most effective, with strict thresholds being most cost-efficient but less equitable. Reward size mainly affects the speed of cooperation, not long-run efficiency. For prediction, the absence of punishment and the presence of collective-threshold rewards should be expected to yield higher efficiency than control, especially under strict or moderate thresholds. The findings are directly relevant for settings with group-based positive incentives but do not inform about punishment effects.

- source: Catola, M., D'Alessandro, S., Guarnieri, P., & Pizziol, V. (2025). Norms and Efficiency in a Multi-Group Society: An Online Experiment. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, online multilevel public goods game, personal normative beliefs are the main driver of contribution decisions, more so than empirical or normative expectations (social norms). Both personal and social norms are responsive to changes in the relative efficiency of the local and global public goods: as the efficiency of the global public good increases, contributions to it rise and personal norms become more aligned with contribution levels. There are also cross-norm effects, where empirical expectations about one public good negatively correlate with contributions to the other. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is informative about how changes in the marginal per capita return (mpcr) affect contribution behavior and the role of personal and social norms in such settings. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may provide useful context for understanding baseline behavior in multilevel public goods games without punishment.

- source: Lo Iacono, S., Sonmez, B., & Fairbrother, M. (2024). The effect of trusting contexts in social dilemmas with collective and individual solutions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Groups in high-trust contexts are more likely to coordinate on the collective solution, resulting in higher efficiency and less resource waste. In low-trust contexts, participants invest more in individual solutions, leading to lower efficiency and more waste. Free-riding rates are similar across trust contexts. The presence of both individual and collective solutions introduces coordination challenges and inefficiency, especially in low-trust settings. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is informative about how trust context affects efficiency and coordination in a threshold public goods game with individual fallback, but since no punishment or sanctioning is present, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. The design and efficiency results may be useful as a control or baseline for similar games, but not for estimating the marginal effect of punishment.

- source: Mori, R., Hanaki, N., & Kameda, T. (2024). An outside individual option increases optimism and facilitates collaboration when groups form flexibly. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing an outside individual option (voluntary participation) in a threshold public goods game increases the efficiency (average payoff) and group success rate compared to mandatory participation, particularly when group formation is flexible and loners do not affect group outcomes. The mechanisms are (1) self-selection of optimistic individuals into groups and (2) belief updating that increases cooperation among those who stay. The positive effect on efficiency is strongest when the baseline (mandatory) group success rate is not already near ceiling. The effect reverses if loners' externality is high (i.e., if loners still affect group outcomes, as in fixed group boundaries). No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in threshold public goods games with flexible group formation and no punishment or reward mechanisms, introducing an outside individual option (voluntary participation) increases efficiency and group success, except when the baseline success rate is already very high. The effect is mediated by self-selection and belief updating. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without voluntary participation, but not for games with punishment. The absence of punishment or reward means the results are not directly transferable to settings where those mechanisms are present, but the paper is highly informative for the effect of voluntary participation and group boundary flexibility.

- source: Brown, C. L. (2024). Team production in endogenous networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a public-goods-like team production environment with endogenous network (team) formation, most efficiency loss is due to failures in the team formation stage, not the contribution stage. When both synergy heterogeneity and high link costs are present, teams are smaller, less productive, and network efficiency loss is highest. Contribution levels within teams are generally close to equilibrium predictions, and contribution inefficiency is relatively minor compared to network inefficiency. The absence of punishment or sanctioning means the results speak to the baseline efficiency of endogenous team formation, not to the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on efficiency outcomes in a public-goods-like environment with endogenous team formation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide a strong baseline for efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in settings where team formation is endogenous and costly. For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency of the control (no-punishment) condition in similar networked public goods games, but cannot inform the treatment effect of punishment.

- source: Becchetti, L., Conzo, P., & Degli Antoni, G. (2015). Public disclosure of players' conduct and common resources harvesting: experimental evidence from a Nairobi slum. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a repeated CPRG with fixed groups and no punishment, providing public feedback on individual withdrawals and payoffs (vs. private feedback only) increases average withdrawal rates by about 12 percentage points (from 62.7% to 74.3%), significantly reducing group efficiency and total payoff. The divergence between conditions grows over rounds. The effect is robust to controls for individual and group characteristics. The authors attribute the effect to conditional cooperation/conformity: when information is public, individuals tend to match the group's average behavior, but this leads to convergence at higher withdrawal (lower cooperation) levels. The results support Ostrom's hypothesis that, without formal sanctions, monitoring alone can undermine cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public goods or common pool resource games without punishment, increasing transparency or monitoring (public feedback on individual actions and payoffs) can reduce efficiency and group payoff, especially in low social capital environments. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, when punishment is not available, enabling public information about individual behavior is likely to decrease efficiency relative to a private information baseline. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without public feedback, but do not inform the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in either condition. The results highlight that monitoring alone is not a substitute for punishment in sustaining cooperation.

- source: Hackel, J., Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., Goto, A., & Taudes, A. (2021). Asymmetric effects of social and economic incentives on cooperation in real effort based public goods games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that social incentives (charity donations) significantly increase the proportion of participants who fully cooperate (complete 90+ tasks), while economic incentives (personal monetary reward) significantly reduce the proportion of full defectors (complete 10 or fewer tasks). The effects are asymmetric: social incentives promote high cooperation, economic incentives reduce non-cooperation. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, focusing instead on positive incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The design is a close variant of a PGG, using real effort instead of resource allocation, and focuses on the effects of positive (economic and social) incentives on cooperation and defection rates. For prediction tasks about punishment and efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it does suggest that incentive structures (other than punishment) can have asymmetric effects on cooperation and defection. The absence of punishment and the focus on non-payoff behavioral outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under punishment.

- source: Gallier, C., Langbein, J., & Vance, C. (2018). Non-binding Restrictions, Cooperation, and Coral Reef Protection: Experimental Evidence from Indonesian Fishing Communities. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that non-binding recommendations, especially those from an external source (e.g., NGO or scientist), can reduce extraction effort in a CPR game, but this effect is highly context-dependent and only significant in the most diverse community (Bunaken). Democratic recommendations have a weaker effect, and leader-based recommendations have no effect. There is no punishment or sanctioning in any treatment. The main outcome is extraction effort, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that, in the absence of enforcement, non-binding recommendations alone are often insufficient to promote cooperation, except in certain socio-cultural contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or CPR games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the limits of non-binding recommendations in the absence of enforcement, but do not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment means this study is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Szekely, A., Bruner, D., Steinmo, S., Todor, A., Volintiru, C., & Andrighetto, G. (2023). Preferences for honesty can support cooperation. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individual honesty preferences, measured via incentivized and self-report tasks, are positively associated with higher contributions in a public goods game with asymmetric information. This association is stronger and more consistent in the Honesty Frame (where the language cues honesty) than in the standard Contribution Frame. However, the framing manipulation does not lead to a significant difference in average contributions between treatments. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present and efficiency is not measured. However, it provides informative context about the role of honesty preferences and framing in influencing contribution behavior in asymmetric information public goods games. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior in the absence of punishment.

- source: Ando, H., Nishi, A., & Handcock, M. S. (2025). Statistical modelling of networked evolutionary public goods games. *Journal of the Royal Statistical Society Series A-statistics in Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that participants preferentially form and maintain network ties with others who also cooperate, and avoid forming ties that would close triangles (i.e., transitive closure is disfavored in tie formation). Participants also prefer to connect with others of similar wealth. The modeling approach (STERGM) is validated for small, repeated networked public goods games. However, the experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It does, however, provide detailed information about a networked PGG variant with dynamic partner selection, which may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation and network effects in the absence of punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency outcomes, this paper offers only contextual or indirect value.

- source: Rosenbaum, S. M., Billinger, S., & Stieglitz, N. (2012). Safeguarding Common-Pool Resources in Transition Economies: Experimental Evidence from Central Asia. *Journal of Development Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that average contributions to the public good are high and nearly identical in both Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan, despite differences in market integration and transition stage. The experiment is a one-shot, anonymous, five-player VCM with no punishment or reward options. The results suggest that, in the absence of sanctioning mechanisms, group size, anonymity, and market integration do not significantly affect contribution rates in this context. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism was present or analyzed. It does, however, provide contextual evidence about baseline contribution rates in a one-shot, five-player, anonymous VCM with MPCR=0.4 and no communication or sanctions. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative only for estimating control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar game designs, but provides no evidence on the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kirkland, K., Van Lange, P. A. M., Charalambous, N., Strauss, C., & Bastian, B. (2024). Beauty Builds Cooperation by Increasing Moral Concern for the Environment. *Environment and Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that when participants believe their extraction from a common pool will have negative consequences for the environment (e.g., less money to a tree preservation charity), they take fewer points, indicating more cooperative/conservation behavior. This effect is stronger when the environmental consequence is paired with a beautiful image of a tree. The effect is mediated by increased concern for tree preservation and reduced concern for personal financial gain. Manipulating the anthropomorphic qualities of trees (e.g., emphasizing mind-like traits) increases perceived mind but does not further increase cooperation. The studies do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It does, however, provide informative evidence that non-monetary moral value and aesthetic framing can increase cooperative behavior in resource dilemma games similar to PGGs. Since no punishment or sanctioning is present, and no efficiency or group payoff is reported, the paper is not directly useful for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding how non-monetary framing or environmental consequences can shift extraction behavior, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment.

- source: Zong, J. C., de Jong, E., Qiu, J. Y., & Li, J. (2025). Socially appropriate intervention: A cross-country investigation of third-party norm enforcement. *China Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This cross-country lab experiment uses a modified trust game with third-party punishment and reward. Third parties can spend up to 5 ECU per round to punish or reward the receiver, with a 1:1 cost-impact ratio. The experiment manipulates norm uncertainty by varying whether the third party is from the same country as the sender and receiver (same-country) or from a different country (cross-country). Third parties observe the sender's expectation about the receiver's return and use this as norm-relevant information. The main findings are: (1) third-party intervention (punishment/reward) is common and contingent on whether the receiver meets the sender's expectation; (2) both cooperation (transfers, returns) and efficiency (sender-receiver welfare, social welfare) are higher in the cross-country treatment; (3) third-party intervention is more responsive to norm-relevant information under higher norm uncertainty (cross-country); (4) the norm enforced by third parties shifts depending on context (trustworthiness in same-country, equal-sharing in cross-country). The presence and responsiveness of third-party punishment/reward increases group efficiency, especially when norm uncertainty is high.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment and reward in a trust-game-like environment increases efficiency (group welfare), especially when norm uncertainty is high (e.g., cross-group or cross-country settings). The effect is mediated by the responsiveness of third parties to norm-relevant information. The punishment mechanism here is costly (1:1 cost-impact), and the game is not a standard PGG but a trust game with continuous transfer and return choices. The findings suggest that, in environments with higher norm uncertainty or social salience, enabling punishment/reward can yield larger efficiency gains. The control (no punishment) condition is not directly present, but the same-country treatment with less responsive third-party intervention can serve as a lower bound. The results are most transferable to settings where third-party punishment is possible, norm cues are available, and the punishment/reward is not highly leveraged (1:1). For prediction, the presence of third-party punishment/reward, higher norm uncertainty, and norm-relevant information all increase the expected efficiency relative to control.

- source: Li, Y. Z., Luo, J., Niu, H., & Ye, H. (2023). When punishers might be loved: fourth-party choices and third-party punishment in a delegation game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Across three lab experiments, the paper shows: (1) Helpers are more trusted and preferred as partners in trust games, but punishers are more likely to be offered higher amounts in ultimatum games due to their deterrence signal, resulting in higher actual payoffs for punishers. (2) In a delegation game, when the proposer's knowledge of the delegate's punishment history is made explicit, fourth-party players prefer to select punishers as their delegates, and this choice leads to higher expected payoffs for both the delegate and the principal. (3) The effect is contingent on the visibility of the punishment signal; when the proposer's information is limited, helpers are preferred. Thus, punishment increases efficiency/payoff in environments where its deterrence is visible and relevant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in games adjacent to public goods games (third-party punishment, ultimatum, and delegation games), enabling punishment increases the efficiency (payoff) of punishers and those who select them as delegates, especially when the punishment signal is visible to others. The effect is context-dependent: punishment is less effective in trust-based settings but more effective in settings where deterrence is valuable and observable. For prediction tasks, the presence of a visible punishment mechanism, the structure of the game (ultimatum/delegation vs. trust), and the information available to other players are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is lower than with punishment in deterrence-relevant settings.

- source: Herne, K., Lappalainen, O., Setälä, M., & Ylisalo, J. (2022). Accountability as a Warrant for Trust: An Experiment on Sanctions and Justifications in a Trust Game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment used a 2x2 design (punishment/no punishment x justification/no justification) in a three-player trust game (two senders, one responder, repeated stranger matching, 6 rounds). The opportunity for senders to punish responders (at a cost: 1 point spent reduces responder's payoff by 3) did not significantly increase the amounts sent or returned, nor did it improve group efficiency. In fact, net earnings for responders were lower in punishment treatments due to the cost of punishment, and group efficiency was not improved. Justification requirements (responders must explain their choices) increased contributions and returns, but this effect was not robust when controlling for the amount received. The combination of punishment and justification did not outperform justification alone in terms of efficiency. The main effect of punishment was to reduce responder payoffs via costly sanctions, not to increase group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a trust-game-like environment with costly punishment (punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment does not increase group efficiency and may reduce it due to the cost of punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games (not a standard PGG, but a trust game with two senders and one responder, continuous contributions, no communication, 6 rounds, known horizon, no rewards). The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than or similar to the control (no punishment), unless the punishment is costless or much more effective. The paper also shows that justification requirements can increase contributions, but this does not translate into higher efficiency when punishment is present. Thus, for prediction, enabling costly punishment in similar trust or PGG-adjacent games is likely to reduce or not improve efficiency relative to control.

- source: dos Santos, M., Braithwaite, V. A., & Wedekind, C. (2014). Exposure to superfluous information reduces cooperation and increases antisocial punishment in reputation-based interactions. *Frontiers in Ecology and Evolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In reputation-based helping games (without punishment), cognitive disturbance (superfluous words) reduces cooperation and lowers group payoffs, indicating that efficiency is sensitive to cognitive constraints. In reputation-based punishment games, disturbance increases antisocial punishment and reduces punishment of defectors, but does not significantly change overall cooperation or mean final payoffs. The link between reputation and payoff is weakened under disturbance. The study highlights that experimental outcomes in reputation and punishment games are highly sensitive to environmental and cognitive factors, and that the expected efficiency gains from punishment or reputation mechanisms may not materialize under cognitive load.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency effects of punishment and reputation mechanisms in indirect reciprocity games are sensitive to cognitive context. For prediction tasks, it suggests that efficiency gains from punishment (or reputation) may be undermined or nullified if cognitive load is high, even when game design parameters are otherwise favorable. The results also show that the presence of punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency: the effect depends on how punishment is used (e.g., antisocial vs. prosocial punishment) and on whether players can process reputational information. When using control efficiency to predict treatment efficiency, one should consider that cognitive or informational disturbances can reduce or eliminate the positive effects of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Pevnitskaya, S., & Ryvkin, D. (2022). The effect of options to reward and punish on behavior in bargaining. *Journal of Economics & Management Strategy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that adding costly punishment and/or reward options for responders in ultimatum bargaining changes both proposer and responder behavior. When only reward is available, proposers make lower offers and efficiency drops. When only punishment is available, acceptance of low offers increases, and efficiency for low offers is higher than baseline. When both reward and punishment are available, both offers and efficiency are highest. The use of reward and punishment is rare and mostly symbolic, so efficiency losses from their direct use are minimal. The main efficiency effects come from changes in offer and acceptance behavior, not from the direct cost of sanctions. The results suggest that punishment options can increase efficiency in settings with low offers, while reward options alone may reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on how the availability and cost/effectiveness of punishment and reward options affect efficiency in a two-player bargaining environment. While not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in asymmetric, bilateral settings with costly sanctions. The results show that costly punishment (with 1:1 cost-impact ratio) can increase efficiency by increasing acceptance of low offers, especially when offers are low, but that reward alone can reduce efficiency. The combination of both instruments yields the highest efficiency. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the presence of reward, the cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the baseline offer/acceptance norms. The findings are most transferable to settings with costly, direct, one-to-one punishment and reward, and less so to multiplayer or public goods environments.

- source: Hajikhameneh, A., & Rubin, J. (2019). Exchange in the Absence of Legal Enforcement: Reputation and Multilateral Punishment under Uncertainty. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated trust game with 12 principals and 12 agents per session, a multilateral punishment institution allows principals to assign a permanent bad reputation to agents at a cost (C=0 or C=5). The experiment varies the probability that the principal's return is destroyed by nature (θ=0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.30). Total welfare (sum of principal and agent earnings) is the main outcome. When punishment is costless, welfare decreases monotonically with uncertainty. When punishment is costly, welfare is not significantly different between no uncertainty and modest uncertainty (θ=0.05), but falls at higher uncertainty. The cost of punishment reduces the frequency of punishment and can lead to more 'benefit of the doubt' given to agents with bad reputations, which can offset welfare losses from uncertainty at low levels. However, at high uncertainty, welfare declines regardless of punishment cost. The design does not allow for endogenous institution formation or communication between players.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on how the cost of punishment and the presence of outcome uncertainty moderate the effect of multilateral punishment on efficiency in a trust-game-like environment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment can sustain high efficiency when uncertainty is low and punishment is not too costly, but that the effect is attenuated or reversed as uncertainty increases or punishment becomes more costly. The results are most relevant for trust games with binary reputation and costly, permanent punishment, and less directly transferable to standard PGGs. The findings support the importance of including punishment cost and environmental uncertainty as moderators when predicting the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: De Geest, L. R., Kidwai, A. H., & Portillo, J. E. (2022). Ours, not yours: Property rights, poaching and deterrence in common-pool resources. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing insiders to punish outsiders in a CPR setting increases efficiency by reducing poaching and aligning insider harvests with the social optimum. The effect of punishment is more pronounced when insiders have earned their property rights, as they coordinate more effectively to deter poaching through higher and more frequent punishment. However, even with punishment, insiders do not fully deter poaching, and some poaching persists. The presence of chat among insiders likely facilitates coordination. The study provides direct evidence on how punishment and the method of property rights allocation interact to affect group payoffs and efficiency in a CPR context with intergroup conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a CPR game with communication among insiders increases efficiency, especially when property rights are earned. The efficiency gain is due to reduced poaching and better coordination among insiders. The design is adjacent to a public goods game (PGG) but differs in the presence of outsiders and the poaching stage. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), with a larger effect if group identity is salient (e.g., earned rights). The effect is not absolute—some inefficiency remains due to incomplete deterrence. The results are most applicable to CPR games with insider/outsider structure, chat among insiders, and a punishment technology with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio.

- source: Kamei, K., Sharma, S., & Walker, M. J. (2025). Collective sanction enforcement: New experimental evidence from two societies. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that third-party punishment of defectors is common in both the UK and India, but is much stronger in the UK. In the UK, adding multiple third parties increases aggregate punishment, and enabling higher-order punishment (punishment of other third parties for failing to punish or for anti-social punishment) further increases aggregate punishment. This transforms the game from a prisoner's dilemma to a stag-hunt, increasing the potential for efficient cooperation. In India, punishment levels are lower, and neither group size nor higher-order punishment opportunities are sufficient to change the incentive structure; defection remains the dominant strategy. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus positive and substantial in the UK (especially with higher-order punishment), but weak in India. The results highlight the importance of cultural context and prevailing norms in determining the efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency impact of punishment in social dilemma games depends on the number of punishers, the availability of higher-order punishment, and the cultural context. In environments similar to the UK, enabling higher-order punishment among multiple third parties can transform the incentive structure to support efficient cooperation, while in environments like India, the same institutional changes have little effect on efficiency due to weaker punishment norms. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by group size, higher-order punishment mechanisms, and societal norms. The control efficiency (without punishment) is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency; the presence and effectiveness of punishment institutions and cultural context must be considered.

- source: Praxmarer, M., Rockenbach, B., & Sutter, M. (2024). Cooperation and norm enforcement differ strongly across adult generations. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that seniors are both more cooperative and more willing to punish defectors than juniors. However, the presence or generation of the third-party punisher does not significantly increase cooperation rates for either generation. As a result, the efficiency (measured by per capita earnings or welfare) is higher in groups of seniors due to their higher baseline cooperation, not due to the effect of punishment. Punishment is costly and does not lead to higher cooperation, so its net effect on efficiency is limited or even negative within each generation. The main moderator of efficiency is the generation of the players, not the punishment treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in repeated two-player prisoner's dilemma games with third-party punishment, enabling punishment does not reliably increase efficiency, as cooperation rates are largely unaffected by the presence or type of punishment. Efficiency is higher among seniors due to their higher baseline cooperation, not because punishment increases cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of third-party punishment (with the parameters used here) should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially among juniors. The main design dimension affecting efficiency is the generation (age) of the players, not the punishment mechanism. The findings are most directly relevant to two-player PDs with binary third-party punishment, and less so to standard public goods games.

- source: Lec, F. L., Matthey, A., & Rydval, O. (2023). Punishing the weakest link - Voluntary sanctions and efficient coordination in the minimum effort game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that voluntary costly punishment opportunities in a repeated minimum effort game (with 8 players per group, 16 rounds, and a two-stage design) significantly increase both effort levels and group efficiency compared to a baseline with no punishment and a treatment with costless disapproval (communication). In the punishment treatment, after an initial drop, average group payoff (net of punishment costs) rises and eventually exceeds the control, with about a third of groups reaching or approaching the Pareto-optimal equilibrium. Punishment is mainly directed at minimum-effort players, and its use is frequent. Disapproval/communication alone produces only a temporary or weak improvement. The results show that decentralized sanctions can serve as an effective coordination device in Pareto-ranked coordination games, with a net positive effect on efficiency in the longer run.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated weakest-link (minimum effort) games with large groups and a history of inefficient coordination, enabling voluntary costly punishment leads to a robust and lasting increase in group efficiency, even after accounting for the direct costs of punishment. The effect is stronger and more persistent than that of costless communication. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, holding control efficiency and other design dimensions constant, enabling punishment (with a 2:1 fine-to-cost ratio and anonymous assignment) will increase average efficiency, especially in settings with multiple rounds, large groups, and no prior communication. The initial rounds after introducing punishment may see a drop in net efficiency, but this reverses as groups coordinate on higher efforts. The results are most directly applicable to coordination games with similar structure, but provide adjacent evidence for public goods games.

- source: Gueth, W., & Otsubo, H. (2023). When Efficiency Requires Arbitrary Discrimination: Theoretical and Experimental Analysis of Equilibrium Selection. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that third-party discriminatory sanctioning (especially when preannounced and when agents differ in wealth) increases the likelihood that the two agents coordinate to avoid mutual harm, resulting in higher group efficiency. When the third party sanctions equally, agents are less likely to coordinate efficiently. The effect is robust to the order protocol (sequential vs. simultaneous), but is strongest when the sanctioning schedule is observable. Asymmetric wealth increases the tendency for the third party to sanction the richer agent more, confirming the 'deep-pocket' hypothesis. Overall, the ability to use discriminatory punishment, and to preannounce it, enables higher efficiency in this coordination dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a coordination game adjacent to the public goods game, enabling a third party to use discriminatory punishment (especially when the punishment schedule is observable) increases group efficiency by facilitating coordination and avoiding mutual harm. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, the design dimensions of punishment observability and the ability to discriminate in punishment can be leveraged to improve efficiency. The results are most relevant for settings where a third party can precommit to a punishment schedule and where agents may differ in wealth or status. For prediction tasks, the presence of observable, flexible, and potentially discriminatory punishment is likely to increase efficiency relative to control conditions without such punishment.

- source: Schaefer, A. (2023). Polycentricity and adaptation: A multilevel selectionist approach. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated CPR lab experiment with three-person groups, the introduction of collective (pool) punishment (10P) does not significantly improve efficiency or payoffs compared to the baseline without punishment. Only a minority of groups in the 10P treatment maintain higher resource levels and payoffs. When second-order punishment (20P) is added (punishing both over-harvesters and tax evaders), the sanctioning institution persists, but efficiency and payoffs decline: subjects extract more to compensate for tax costs, and resource exhaustion becomes more frequent. The effect of punishment is thus context-dependent: collective punishment can promote conservation in some groups, but second-order punishment undermines efficiency overall. The paper provides direct evidence on group payoffs, resource exhaustion, and the mechanisms by which punishment affects behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in CPR-like environments is not uniformly positive and depends on the institutional design. Pool punishment (collective, voluntary tax-funded) does not reliably increase efficiency compared to no punishment, and second-order punishment (punishing tax evaders) can reduce efficiency by increasing extraction and resource collapse. For prediction, the presence of second-order punishment is a strong negative moderator for efficiency, while voluntary collective punishment has a mixed or null effect. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient to guarantee higher efficiency; the details of the punishment institution and group responses are critical. Control efficiency is a strong predictor, but the effect of punishment depends on whether it is voluntary, enforced, and how costs are distributed.

- source: Nigus, H. Y., Nillesen, E., Mohnen, P., & Di Falco, S. (2023). Markets and socially responsible behavior: do punishment and religion matter?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study uses a lab-in-the-field market experiment in Ethiopia to test the effects of punishment and religious priming on socially responsible behavior and welfare. In the market baseline (no punishment), only about 21% of purchases are fair (no negative externality), and average earnings are about 56 Birr. When punishment is introduced (observers can punish at a cost of 1 Birr to reduce others' earnings by 5 Birr), the proportion of fair purchases rises to 95%, and average earnings increase to 66 Birr. Punishment also reduces anti-social behavior in a subsequent joy-of-destruction game (money burning). Religious priming increases fair purchases to 32% but does not significantly improve welfare compared to the baseline. The results show that punishment mechanisms are highly effective at increasing efficiency (group welfare) in this market context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a market game with negative externalities, adding a punishment mechanism (with a cost of 1 to reduce another's payoff by 5) substantially increases efficiency (average group earnings) compared to a baseline with no punishment. The effect is large and robust. The design is not a standard PGG but is adjacent: it is a market/trade game with externalities and third parties, and the efficiency measure is directly comparable (average earnings relative to maximum possible). The punishment mechanism is costly and effective. Religious priming has a much weaker effect. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar market or externality games, enabling punishment with a moderate cost-to-impact ratio will lead to a large increase in efficiency over the control. The effect is much larger than that of non-monetary priming.

- source: Mitzkewitz, M., & Neugebauer, T. (2020). Can intermediaries assure contracts? Experimental evidence. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment compares five treatments: a baseline (no intermediary), two ENFO treatments (punishment fines of 10 or 20 tokens), and two INFO treatments (reputation-based, with or without a small service cost). In the baseline, average payoffs are low (mean 0.64 tokens/round). INFO treatments increase payoffs modestly (mean 1.75 and 1.19 tokens/round for INFO(0) and INFO(.5)), while ENFO treatments yield much higher payoffs (mean 2.38 and 5.18 tokens/round for ENFO(10) and ENFO(20)). The ENFO institution, which enforces monetary fines for cheating, is much more effective at increasing efficiency than the INFO institution, which only provides reputation information. Increasing the fine in ENFO from 10 to 20 tokens further increases efficiency. The INFO institution's effect is limited, in part because reputation can be quickly repaired and there is no centralized punishment. The study also finds that the cost to use INFO reduces its adoption and thus its effectiveness. Over time, ENFO treatments move toward higher efficiency, while INFO treatments tend to regress toward less efficient outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player prisoner's dilemma games with optional participation, introducing a punishment mechanism (ENFO) that imposes a monetary fine for cheating substantially increases efficiency compared to a control with no punishment. The effect is stronger with higher fines. An information-based reputation system (INFO) yields only modest efficiency gains, especially if reputation can be easily repaired and there is no centralized enforcement. For prediction, the presence and strength of punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech), as well as the absence of reward, are key moderators. The control efficiency (baseline payoff) is low, and the treatment efficiency (with ENFO) is much higher, especially with higher fines. The design features of the punishment mechanism (cost, effectiveness, centralization) are critical for predicting efficiency gains.

- source: Calabuig, V., Jiménez-Jiménez, N., Olcina, G., & Rodriguez-Lara, I. (2024). Coordinated and uncoordinated punishment in a team investment game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares coordinated and uncoordinated punishment in a team investment game with two investors and one allocator. Coordinated punishment (where both investors must punish to inflict a penalty) increases joint investment and allocator return relative to uncoordinated punishment. However, this does not translate into higher efficiency, as the increased frequency of joint punishment in the coordinated condition destroys the potential efficiency gains. The highest efficiency (sum of total payoffs) is observed in the no-punishment condition. There are no significant efficiency differences between coordinated and uncoordinated punishment treatments after accounting for punishment costs. Thus, punishment—regardless of its form—does not improve efficiency in this setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a team investment game with binary investment and an allocator, enabling punishment (either coordinated or uncoordinated) reduces efficiency compared to a no-punishment baseline. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are: binary (all-or-nothing) investment, two investors and one allocator (asymmetric roles), fixed punishment cost, and varying punishment effectiveness. The findings suggest that, even if punishment increases cooperation or returns, the cost and frequency of punishment can offset these gains, leading to lower or unchanged efficiency. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency, and the specific form of punishment (coordinated vs uncoordinated) does not significantly alter this outcome in this design. The results are most directly applicable to asymmetric, binary-contribution, repeated games with fixed matching and no communication.

- source: Deng, X. Y., Wang, T., Xue, L., & Yang, S. (2025). Norm enforcement on minorities: Evidence from traffic violations. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The laboratory experiment shows that the availability of peer punishment (especially when participants can choose whom to observe and punish) increases rule compliance, particularly among minority (outgroup) members who are more likely to be monitored. However, the introduction of punishment reduces total group payoffs compared to the no-punishment baseline, due to the direct costs of punishing and being punished. The highest compliance (lowest violation rate) occurs when both observability and punishment are present, but this comes with increased inequality: minorities earn significantly less than majorities or locals. The frequency of fully compliant (high-norm) groups is higher with punishment, but overall efficiency (average payoff relative to the social optimum) is lower in punishment treatments than in control. Punishment is applied non-discriminatorily, but minorities are more likely to be observed and thus punished, leading to a 'compliance minority effect' and a 'local premium' in payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with negative externalities and peer punishment, enabling punishment increases compliance but reduces efficiency (total group payoff) compared to the no-punishment baseline. The reduction in efficiency is due to the costs of punishment and increased inequality, especially for minorities who are more likely to be monitored and punished. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in similar designs will likely decrease average efficiency, even if compliance increases. The effect is strongest when observability is endogenous (players choose whom to monitor) and when group composition allows for minority/majority distinctions. The findings are most directly relevant to settings with peer punishment, endogenous monitoring, and negative externalities from non-cooperation.

- source: Safarzynska, K. (2020). Collective punishment promotes resource conservation if it is not enforced. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when participants can voluntarily contribute to a centralized sanctioning institution (which probabilistically punishes untrustworthy behavior in a dyadic trust game), they frequently do so, but the resulting institutions are typically inefficient. The cost of forming the institution is not offset by the gains in trust and trustworthiness, so overall group earnings (efficiency) are lower than in the control condition where no sanctioning institution can be formed. This result holds for both partner and stranger matching protocols. The paper also finds that higher sanction probabilities increase trust and trustworthiness, but the aggregate welfare is still lower due to the cost of institution formation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a trust-game environment with voluntary centralized sanctioning, enabling punishment can reduce efficiency compared to a no-punishment baseline, even though trust and trustworthiness increase. For prediction, this suggests that the presence of costly, voluntarily funded centralized punishment may decrease efficiency unless the punishment mechanism is highly cost-effective. The findings are most relevant for trust-game-like settings with endogenous institution formation, and less directly for standard PGGs, but the negative efficiency effect of costly punishment is a cautionary signal for similar environments.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2021). Co-enforcement of Common Pool Resources to Deter Encroachment: Evidence from a Field Experiment in Chile. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The field experiment with Chilean fishers shows that co-enforcement (insider monitoring plus government sanctions) reduces poaching and increases insiders' harvests and earnings relative to a no-enforcement baseline. However, the costs of monitoring largely offset these gains, so net efficiency improvements are modest. Voting on monitoring investments and higher fines both help insiders coordinate to invest enough in monitoring to eliminate average poaching gains, but poaching persists and is not highly sensitive to the level of monitoring or fines. The results suggest that while co-enforcement can improve outcomes, the ongoing cost of monitoring is a major constraint on efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing punishment (in the form of exogenous government sanctions for poaching, combined with group monitoring) increases group efficiency/payoff relative to a no-enforcement baseline, but the net gains are limited by the costliness of monitoring. The design is not a standard public goods game but is a dynamic common pool resource game with poaching by outsiders. The findings suggest that, in similar CPR settings, enabling punishment/sanctions will likely increase efficiency, but the magnitude of the effect depends on the cost structure of monitoring and the ability of insiders to coordinate. Voting and higher fines can help achieve deterrence, but unless monitoring costs are low, efficiency gains may be small. For prediction, the control (no-punishment) efficiency is lower than with punishment enabled, but the treatment effect is muted by monitoring costs.

- source: Macleod, W. B., Lara, V. V., & Zehnder, C. (2025). Worker Empowerment and Subjective Evaluation: On Building an Effective Conflict Culture. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of enabling punishment (conflict) in a principal-agent game with subjective evaluation is highly context-dependent. In the baseline, communication, and agreement (code of conduct) experiments, allowing workers to punish managers for not paying bonuses does not increase efficiency: the costs of conflict outweigh any small increases in effort, and total surplus is lower or unchanged compared to the no-punishment condition. Workers and managers fail to coordinate on an efficiency-enhancing norm, and punishment is often misapplied. However, when conflicts are institutionalized through a formal grievance process (grievance experiment), workers and managers coordinate more on a pay-for-output norm, effort increases significantly, and total surplus is higher in the punishment-enabled condition. Thus, punishment can enhance efficiency, but only when supported by strong institutional framing and norm coordination.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is not uniform and is highly sensitive to the institutional environment, especially the presence of mechanisms that coordinate norms (such as a grievance procedure). In environments lacking such coordination, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency due to the direct costs of conflict. In contrast, when a formal grievance process is present, punishment can increase both effort and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of institutionalized conflict resolution and norm-coordination mechanisms should be considered a key moderator. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment) unless the institutional context is specified.

- source: Dasgupta, N., & Musolesi, M. (2025). Investigating the impact of direct punishment on the emergence of cooperation in multi-agent reinforcement learning systems. *Autonomous Agents and Multi-agent Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Direct punishment, when just punishment is net-rewarding, is effective at promoting cooperation and maximizing group efficiency in MARL populations facing social dilemmas.
