Populations with direct punishment (especially with partner selection) achieve the highest total group reward at equilibrium, outperforming third-party punishment and combined punishment mechanisms in terms of efficiency, despite achieving slightly lower cooperation rates. Third-party punishment and combined punishment mechanisms lead to higher cooperation but incur greater punishment costs, reducing overall efficiency. The presence of partner selection and reputation further enhances the effectiveness of direct punishment. If just punishment is costly (i.e., not net-rewarding), neither cooperation nor punishment emerges, and group efficiency collapses. The findings are robust to changes in population size.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in Iterated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with MARL agents, enabling direct punishment (with net-rewarding just punishment) increases group efficiency compared to no punishment. The effect is strongest when combined with partner selection and reputation. Third-party punishment and combined punishment mechanisms further increase cooperation but reduce efficiency due to higher punishment costs. The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of punishment, the reward structure for just punishment, and the availability of partner selection and reputation are key moderators of the efficiency effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in adjacent social dilemma games (e.g., IPD) with similar design features, especially in artificial agent populations.

- source: Zhao, Y. K., Zhao, X. B., & Shen, Z. J. M. (2018). The hot-versus-cold effect in a punishment game: a multi-round experimental study. *Annals of Operations Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two elicitation methods (strategy vs. direct-response) in a multi-round, two-player punishment game. The strategy method leads to significantly higher punishment rates (84% vs. 62%) and more frequent cooperative sender actions (S1: 68% vs. 44%). Combined payoffs (group welfare) are higher for S1 actions, and the higher frequency of S1 under the strategy method results in higher average group payoffs. Regression and learning model analyses show that the difference in punishment rates is due to mental accounting: the strategy method allows receivers to integrate the cost and expected benefit of punishment, increasing its perceived utility. Over repeated rounds, senders learn to avoid punishment by choosing S1 more often, further increasing group welfare. Reward rates are low and do not differ between methods. The findings suggest that, in repeated games, the strategic value of punishment (deterrence) is more important than the immediate emotional satisfaction of punishing, and that the design of the punishment mechanism (including decision timing) can significantly affect cooperation and group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player punishment games, higher punishment rates (enabled by the strategy method) lead to more cooperative sender behavior and higher group payoffs. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the structure is adjacent: one player can take a selfish or cooperative action, and the other can punish. The results suggest that, in repeated settings, enabling effective punishment (with high perceived strategic value) increases efficiency by deterring selfish behavior. For prediction tasks, this implies that, all else equal, punishment mechanisms that increase the perceived strategic value of punishment (e.g., via decision timing or repeated interaction) will lead to higher efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is low due to frequent selfish actions. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, punishment-enabled games, but the mechanism may generalize to other repeated social dilemmas with punishment.

- source: Zhang, M., Ma, Y. J., Tao, Y., Wang, Z., Shi, L., & Wang, R. W. (2020). Awareness of wealth inequalities breeds animosity. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in repeated PD games with costly punishment, the visibility of wealth information interacts with initial wealth inequality to affect both cooperation rates and average final wealth. When initial wealth is unequal, making wealth visible reduces cooperation and leads to lower average final wealth compared to when wealth is hidden. When initial wealth is equal, visibility has little effect on cooperation or final wealth. Punishment is available but rarely used, and its frequency does not significantly differ across treatments. Thus, in environments with inequality, hiding wealth information improves both cooperation and group payoff (final wealth), while visibility exacerbates inequality and reduces efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving efficiency in repeated PD or PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides evidence that the visibility of wealth information is a strong moderator when initial endowments are unequal: making wealth visible reduces efficiency (average final wealth), while hiding it increases efficiency. The effect is not present when initial endowments are equal. The presence of punishment does not appear to moderate this effect, as punishment is infrequent and not significantly different across treatments. Thus, when modeling efficiency outcomes, consider both initial wealth inequality and information visibility as key moderators, especially in games with available but rarely used punishment.

- source: Li, L. Y., Zhao, X. B., Xie, D., & Xiao, X. (2024). On difference between direct-response method and strategy method in decision-making: behavioural and neural evidence in a reward-punishment game. *Journal of the Operational Research Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that the elicitation method (direct-response vs. strategy) significantly affects the rates of reward and punishment in a repeated two-player reward-punishment game. Specifically, the strategy method leads to higher rates of both reward and punishment. Behavioral modeling and neural evidence suggest that the ownership effect (overweighting of cost) suppresses reward in the direct-response method, while counterfactual thinking (comparison of possible outcomes) increases punishment in the strategy method. Strategic thinking is present in both methods but does not explain the difference between them. The study does not report efficiency or total payoff outcomes, and the game structure is not a public goods game or a direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the method of eliciting punishment and reward decisions (direct-response vs. strategy) can substantially affect the frequency of punishment and reward, with the strategy method increasing both. However, it does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The game is a two-player repeated reward-punishment game, not a public goods game or close variant, and the main outcomes are behavioral rates, not payoffs. The findings may be relevant for understanding how elicitation methods could moderate punishment effects, but the lack of efficiency data and the different game structure limit its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2024). Time pressure and deliberation affect moral punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main findings are that (1) time pressure reduces the probability of moral punishment, (2) deliberation increases the probability of moral punishment, and (3) the content of deliberation matters: deliberating fairness increases, while deliberating self-interest decreases, moral punishment. These effects are specific to moral punishment and do not consistently affect other types of punishment or cooperation. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct impact of these manipulations on group efficiency is not assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how cognitive constraints and deliberation affect the likelihood of moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that manipulations increasing deliberation (e.g., removing time pressure, encouraging fairness-based reflection) are likely to increase the use of moral punishment, which in other literature is often associated with higher cooperation and potentially higher efficiency. However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper's findings should be used as indirect evidence about the behavioral mechanisms (punishment rates) rather than direct evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2024). People punish defection, not failures to conform to the majority. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that people are more likely to cooperate and to punish defectors (moral punishment) when the majority of partners are cooperative. Antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators) is much less common and does not increase when defection is the majority behavior, contradicting a pure conformity account. The presence of partner punishment (Experiment 1) increases cooperation rates above the base rate, while its absence (Experiment 2) leads to cooperation rates more closely matching the environment. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but the final account balances suggest that punishment and cooperation patterns affect individual earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how punishment is used in dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma games, showing that punishment is primarily directed at defectors and is sensitive to the prevalence of cooperation. However, it does not directly report or analyze efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency in public goods games is limited. The design is adjacent to PGGs: it is a repeated, one-shot, dyadic PD with a costly punishment option, not a multi-player PGG. The punishment mechanism is well specified (costly, with a fixed cost-to-impact ratio). The findings suggest that enabling punishment increases cooperation, especially when the environment is cooperative, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction, this paper supports the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and likely efficiency in similar dyadic games, but caution is needed in extrapolating to multi-player PGGs.

- source: Gallo, E., Barak, D., & Langtry, A. (2023). Social distancing in networks: A web-based interactive experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Fines (punishment) for not practicing social distancing significantly increase the probability that participants choose to distance, compared to a baseline with no fine. Nudges (informational videos) have a smaller and less robust effect. The effect of fines is immediate and persistent, while the nudge effect is weaker and context-dependent. Network structure (star vs. complete) and position (superspreader vs. peripheral) influence distancing: superspreaders distance more. High disease contagiousness increases distancing. Political ideology, risk preferences, and social preferences also moderate distancing behavior. However, the experiment does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing fines (punishment) increases compliance with a costly pro-social action (distancing) in a networked environment structurally similar to a public goods game. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (distancing) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, binary action, fixed cost/benefit, punishment cost and impact, no reward, no feedback) are well specified, so the paper is informative about the likely direction of punishment effects on cooperation, but not directly on efficiency. Use this paper as supportive evidence that punishment increases pro-social behavior in similar environments, but do not use it for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: Guo, Y. C., Zhao, X. R., Liu, Y. B., & Ma, J. H. (2024). The effect of third-party intervention on normative behavior of bystanders: An explanation of the social norm cueing effect. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both third-party punishment and third-party compensation, when observed by bystanders, increase the bystanders' fairness in subsequent dictator game allocations. This effect is mediated by changes in descriptive norm perceptions. The effect is present when the intervention is presented as group summary information (Study 1) or as an individual action perceived as representative of group behavior (Study 2). The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a dictator game, not a public goods game or its direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that observing third-party punishment or compensation can increase norm-compliant behavior (fairness in allocation) among bystanders, mediated by changes in perceived descriptive norms. However, because the study uses a dictator game (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding norm transmission and the psychological mechanisms by which punishment or compensation can affect behavior, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Liu, J., & Riyanto, Y. E. (2017). Information transparency and equilibrium selection in coordination games: an experimental study. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Revealing information about the existence and structure of centralized punishment or reward mechanisms in a stag hunt game increases the rate of mutual cooperation (the payoff-dominant equilibrium) compared to a control where only final payoffs are shown. The effect is stronger for punishment than for reward. The mechanism works both by increasing beliefs in others' cooperation and, for punishment, by directly increasing cooperative choices. In contrast, when the underlying game remains a prisoner's dilemma (i.e., the punishment/reward is too small to change the equilibrium), revealing the mechanism does not increase cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in mutual cooperation implies higher group welfare in the context of the stag hunt game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that, in two-player, repeated stag hunt games with centralized punishment or reward, making the institutional mechanism transparent increases cooperation rates, especially when punishment is highlighted. While efficiency is not directly measured, the increase in mutual cooperation (the payoff-dominant outcome) suggests that efficiency would also increase. The results are most relevant for coordination games adjacent to public goods games, rather than standard PGGs. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment and making its mechanism salient is likely to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such information, especially when the punishment is strong enough to change the equilibrium structure.

- source: Arai, S., Tooby, J., & Cosmides, L. (2022). Motivations to reciprocate cooperation and punish defection are calibrated by estimates of how easily others can switch partners. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that participants who perceive higher relational mobility (i.e., that others can easily switch partners) reciprocate more and punish less in a dyadic trust game with punishment. The opportunity to switch partners (experimental manipulation) did not affect punishment, but did interact with relational mobility to affect reciprocation in American participants. Punishment did not increase subsequent cooperation and made punished partners more likely to leave. The main outcomes are behavioral (reciprocation, punishment, switching), not efficiency or group payoff. Payoff tables are provided, but efficiency is not a dependent variable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in punishment-enabled games: higher perceived partner choice (relational mobility) leads to more reciprocation and less punishment, which could plausibly increase efficiency, but efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured or analyzed. The game is a dyadic trust game with punishment, not a standard public goods game, so findings are adjacent but not directly transferable. The paper is most informative about how punishment and partner choice cues affect individual behavior, not aggregate efficiency.

- source: Ferguson, E. (2021). Inequality averse and compassionate blood donor: implication for interventions. *Vox Sanguinis*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Blood donors are more likely to choose compensation (directly helping the victim) over punishment (sanctioning the perpetrator) in a one-shot 3PPC game, while organ donors are more likely to choose punishment. The compensation and punishment options are both costly to the third party, and the game is designed so that each £1 spent on compensation or punishment has a 2x effect. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, but describes how different choices affect the distribution of payoffs. A follow-up experiment finds that an intervention message based on advantageous inequality aversion increases non-donors' willingness to take information about blood donation, with some evidence that guilt-based appeals may have negative effects on current donors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about individual preferences for compensation versus punishment in a third-party context, which is adjacent to public goods games with punishment. The design is a one-shot, three-player game with both punishment and compensation options, and the main outcomes are behavioral choices, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of a compensation option may reduce the use of punishment, and that individuals' philanthropic identities (e.g., blood donor vs. organ donor) influence their preference for direct help versus norm enforcement. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in repeated public goods games, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited and indirect.

- source: Loustau, T., Glassman, J., Martin, J. W., Young, L., & Mcauliffe, K. (2024). The impact of group membership on punishment versus partner rejection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two preregistered online experiments, participants responded to resource allocation decisions by either punishing (subtracting from the allocator's bonus) or rejecting (choosing not to interact again). In both a minimal group paradigm and a real-world political group context, participants punished outgroup members more than ingroup members, especially when the allocator's intent was selfish and the outcome was unequal. However, group membership did not affect the likelihood of partner rejection. The main outcomes are punishment and rejection rates; no efficiency or group payoff measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games with and without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs but focuses on dyadic allocation games with punishment and partner rejection as responses. The main contribution is to show that group membership increases punishment of outgroup members, but this is not linked to efficiency or welfare outcomes. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, but it does provide evidence that group membership can moderate punishment behavior.

- source: Barriga, A., Ferguson, N. T. N., Fiala, N., & Leroch, M. A. (2023). Ethnic cooperation and conflict in Kenya. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing participants to switch partners (partner choice) increases cooperation rates compared to when switching is not possible. When switching is not possible, participants are more likely to punish defectors than to withdraw, but when switching is possible, punishment and withdrawal are used equally. Unexpectedly, punishment rates are higher when switching is possible. Importantly, punishment does not increase the likelihood that defectors will cooperate in the next round. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that partner choice is more effective than punishment at sustaining cooperation in this dyadic context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: in two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency as much as enabling partner choice (switching). Punishment does not increase subsequent cooperation, and its use is not associated with higher group welfare. The results suggest that, in dyadic games, punishment may not improve efficiency, and its effect may be limited or even negative if not paired with partner choice. However, because the study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games is limited and should be interpreted with caution, especially for multi-player settings.

- source: Boonmanunt, S., & Meier, S. (2023). The effect of financial constraints on in-group bias: Evidence from rice farmers in Thailand. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that being more financially constrained (before harvest) does not increase in-group bias in cooperation or norm enforcement. In-group bias in cooperation and punishment is observed only after harvest (less constrained), but these effects are not statistically significant in regression analyses. Sharing behavior is stable across financial conditions. The main outcomes are cooperation rates, punishment assigned, and sharing amounts, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that financial constraints do not inherently increase parochialism in cooperation or punishment among low-income rice farmers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it provides evidence that, in a prisoner's dilemma with third-party punishment (an adjacent but not exact PGG variant), the presence of punishment allows for the study of norm enforcement, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, cooperation rate) rather than efficiency. The design details (player count, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, but the lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's direct value for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Deutchman, P., Aellen, M., Bogese, M., Bshary, R., Drayton, L., Gil, D., Martin, J., Pretot, L., Raihani, N., Santos, L. R., & Mcauliffe, K. (2023). Punishment is sensitive to outside options in humans but not in cleaner fish (Labroides dimidiatus). *Animal Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that human participants are sensitive to the intent behind cheating when deciding to punish: they punish more when a partner chooses to cheat despite having a cooperative option. Cleaner fish, in contrast, do not show this sensitivity; male fish punish female partners at similar rates regardless of whether the female had a choice to cooperate or not. The main outcome is the likelihood or amount of punishment, not efficiency or group payoff. The human experiment is a novel economic game inspired by cleaner fish mutualism, not a standard public goods game, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that human punishment is sensitive to intent (alternative options), while cleaner fish punishment is not. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the psychological basis for punishment in humans includes sensitivity to intent, which may affect when and how punishment is used, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Wang, X. M., Zhu, H. J., Zhao, Q. Y., & Wang, Y. R. (2024). The influence of social comparison on third-party altruistic behavior. *Current Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the direction of social comparison strongly influences whether third parties choose to punish violators or compensate victims in dictator-game scenarios. Upward comparison (feeling worse off) increases punishment, mediated by anger, while downward comparison (feeling better off) increases compensation, mediated by empathy. Public contexts increase the amount invested in compensation (especially for downward comparison), but do not change the basic choice pattern. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about psychological and contextual moderators of third-party punishment and compensation in dictator-game settings, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The findings are relevant for predicting when punishment or compensation will be chosen and how much will be invested, depending on social comparison and context. However, because the design is a third-party dictator game (not a public goods game) and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly support prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. The evidence is most useful for understanding behavioral and emotional drivers of punishment/compensation, not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Ünal-Kocaslan, O., & Akgün, S. (2024). Altruistic punishment in intergroup context. *Current Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants are willing to engage in costly punishment of unfair resource allocations, supporting the concept of altruistic punishment. The amount spent on punishment is higher for unfair than fair allocations. Ingroup favoritism is observed in that participants punish more harshly when the victim is an ingroup member, but there is no effect of the perpetrator's group identity. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on punishment behavior and emotional responses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment is robustly observed in third-party punishment games, especially when unfairness is directed at ingroup members. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as it uses a third-party punishment game with a dictator-like allocation, not a standard PGG. The findings are most useful for understanding when and how punishment is deployed, not its effect on group efficiency.

- source: Klempt, C., & Pull, K. (2018). The hidden costs of control revisited: Should a sanctioning policy be announced in advance?. *Managerial and Decision Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the way sanctions are implemented and communicated matters: when the principal preannounces a sanctioning policy, agents interpret this as a signal of distrust, which can reduce their voluntary effort (crowding out). When the sanctioning decision is not announced in advance or is made by a computer, agents do not interpret it as a negative signal, and effort is higher. The study provides evidence for both incentive and signaling effects of sanctions, but all results are in terms of individual effort, not group payoff or efficiency. There is no direct measurement or reporting of efficiency or total surplus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding the importance of how punishment is announced and who imposes it. If the prediction task involves settings where punishment is preannounced and personalized, one should expect possible crowding out of intrinsic motivation, potentially reducing the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. If punishment is not preannounced or is impersonal (e.g., random or computer-imposed), the negative signaling effect is absent, and the incentive effect dominates, likely leading to higher effort (and by analogy, higher efficiency). However, since the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, these implications are indirect and should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Windrich, I., Kierspel, S., Neumann, T., Berger, R., & Vogt, B. (2024). Enforcement of Fairness Norms by Punishment: A Comparison of Gains and Losses. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Introducing a third-party punishment mechanism in the dictator game reduces selfishness (i.e., increases fairness) only when the punishment is strong enough, and this effect is more pronounced in the loss domain than in the gains domain. Weak punishment can have no effect or even reduce fairness in the gains domain. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, but provides detailed analysis of behavioral responses (dictator's demand, fair/equal splits, punishment frequency) to punishment strength and domain (gains vs. losses).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how the strength of the punishment mechanism (punishment impact per unit cost) moderates the behavioral effect of punishment in a dictator game context, especially in the loss domain. However, because the main outcome is dictator's demand (a measure of fairness/selfishness) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results suggest that strong punishment is necessary to reduce selfishness, and that punishment may be more effective in loss contexts. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect support for the importance of punishment strength, but does not provide direct efficiency estimates.

- source: Wiederhold, E., & Bühren, C. (2025). Letting off Steam! Experimental Evidence on Displaced Aggression. *Review of Behavioral Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment studies how participants in the role of 'Player C' choose to punish others after allocations that may be unfair (intentional or by chance) or fair. Punishment is costly: for each point given up, 5 points are deducted from the target. The main findings (from the main paper, not this appendix) concern when and whom participants punish, and how this varies by the intentionality of the unfairness and by subject pool. Theoretical predictions using the Fehr-Schmidt model suggest punishment should only occur in unfair allocations, and only against those with higher payoffs. The appendix does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on punishment behavior in a non-PGG allocation game, with a strong manipulation of intentionality and target of punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The punishment technology (cost and impact) is clearly specified, which may be useful for mapping to other studies. The design is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant.

- source: Doering, L., & Ody-Brasier, A. (2021). Time and Punishment: How Individuals Respond to Being Sanctioned in Voluntary Associations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effect of being sanctioned (fined) for absence in voluntary microsavings groups changes over time. Early in group tenure, sanctioned individuals reduce their prosocial contributions to the collective fund, but as they become more dependent on the group (measured by time in the group or by having received emergency funds), their response to sanctions becomes increasingly prosocial, with higher allocations to the collective fund after being fined. The effect is robust to various controls and alternative specifications. The study does not measure group efficiency or total group payoff, but the findings suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting prosocial behavior is temporally and relationally dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on prosocial behavior (here, contributions to a collective fund) is not static but depends on group tenure and individual dependence on the group. For prediction tasks involving efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like environments, this suggests that the impact of punishment may be weaker or even negative early in group formation, but can become more positive as group members become more dependent on each other. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather individual prosocial allocation, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The study is most informative about the temporal dynamics and social context moderating punishment effects, rather than about the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Lois, G., & Wessa, M. (2019). Creating sanctioning norms in the lab: the influence of descriptive norms in third-party punishment. *Social Influence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants' third-party punishment behavior in a repeated dictator game was strongly shaped by social feedback about peer punishment. Exposure to high or low average peer punishment led to persistent differences in participants' own punishment levels, even after feedback was removed. The presence of a single free rider (a peer who never punished) in a group with otherwise high punishment reduced average punishment and increased the frequency of free riding, despite the same average peer punishment as the high group. These effects persisted over time, indicating the formation of stable sanctioning norms. The study demonstrates the importance of descriptive norms and the 'bad apple' effect in shaping costly punishment behavior, but does not directly measure or report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that descriptive norms and the presence of free riders can substantially moderate the level of costly punishment in repeated norm enforcement games. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, free riding) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like environments. The study suggests that the presence of free riders can undermine the effectiveness of punishment as a norm enforcement tool, which could plausibly reduce efficiency gains from punishment, but this is not directly tested. The game is a repeated third-party punishment dictator game, not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency should be made with caution.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2023). Communicating emotions, but not expressing them privately, reduces moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two experiments, the authors find that giving participants the opportunity to communicate their emotions to their partner after each round of a Prisoner's Dilemma game with costly punishment reduces the rate of moral punishment compared to a control condition. However, this reduction is small, and moral punishment remains common. Privately expressing emotions (without communication) does not reduce moral punishment. Both emotion communication and private expression can reduce cooperation and increase other forms of punishment (hypocritical punishment, punishment bias). The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how emotion communication and private expression affect punishment behavior in a repeated one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as a function of punishment, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment rates (especially moral punishment) can be reduced by allowing emotion communication, but the effect is small. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage. The results are most useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Migheli, M., & Saraceno, M. (2023). On the propensity to settle or litigate in laboratory disputes. *Economia Politica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that the threat of costly, uncertain punishment (litigation) is a strong motivator for offenders to restore what they took, more so than fairness concerns about the victim's effort. Offenders are more likely to restore when they learn the victim worked for their endowment, but this effect is mainly present among female offenders. Higher litigation fees increase the probability that offenders restore to avoid litigation and decrease the probability that victims initiate litigation, with gender moderating these effects. The study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral responses to punishment threats and fairness cues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral effects of costly, uncertain punishment threats (litigation) in a two-player dispute context, not a public goods game. It shows that punishment threats (here, litigation) increase the likelihood of restoration (settlement), especially when the cost of punishment is high. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as it involves second-party costly punishment with the possibility of redress, but lacks the multi-player, repeated, or contribution structure of PGGs. The main relevance is for understanding how punishment cost and fairness cues affect compliance and dispute resolution, not efficiency.

- source: Güney, S., & Newell, B. R. (2013). Fairness overrides reputation: the importance of fairness considerations in altruistic cooperation. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that rejection of unfair offers (costly punishment) in the Mini Ultimatum Game is primarily driven by fairness considerations, particularly the perceived intentions of the proposer. When intentions are not assessable, outcome fairness still motivates some punishment, but to a lesser degree. The opportunity for reputation building and future interaction (i.e., repeated, non-anonymous play) does not significantly increase costly punishment when fairness considerations are strong, but can increase punishment when intentions are ambiguous. The study does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the psychological drivers of costly punishment in ultimatum-like bargaining games are primarily fairness-based, not reputation-based, and that the presence of repeated interaction or reputation opportunities does not necessarily increase punishment or change behavioral outcomes when fairness motives are strong. However, because the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment occurs, not its aggregate payoff consequences.

- source: Cabrales, A., Kendall, R., & Sánchez, A. (2022). The effectiveness of prosocial policies: Gender differences arising from social norms. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that policy interventions (framing, exogenous punishment, endogenous punishment) do not reduce the overall average speed (a proxy for collective safety) compared to control. However, there are strong gender differences: policies reduce risky (Fast) choices and average speed among female participants, but increase them among males. This results in no net improvement in group-level outcomes and increases gender inequality in payoffs. The effect is mediated by differences in social norms and normative expectations. The most effective policy for changing female behavior is endogenous punishment, but male participants respond by increasing risky behavior, offsetting the gains. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, and the main outcomes are behavioral and norm-related.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment mechanisms (both exogenous and endogenous) on group-level efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like environments may be highly heterogeneous and context-dependent, especially along gender lines. In this specific driving game, punishment does not improve group-level efficiency (as proxied by average speed or safety) because positive effects for one subgroup (females) are offset by negative effects for another (males). The design is not a standard PGG, and efficiency is not directly measured, so the findings should be transferred with caution. The main implication for prediction is that punishment may not always increase efficiency, especially if there are strong heterogeneities in norm compliance or behavioral response. The study highlights the importance of considering subgroup responses and social norms when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Myers, C. D. (2016). Participation and punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that when punishment is possible, participation (turnout) increases dramatically (from 60% to 88%). Punishment is overwhelmingly directed at abstainers, and abstainers incur a net loss from being punished. Egoist subjects are more responsive to punishment than pro-social subjects, leading to a more egoist composition among participants when punishment is available. However, because participation is costly and does not increase the value of the prize, increased participation does not improve group efficiency or welfare. The study does not report treatment effects on efficiency or group payoff, and the design makes clear that increased participation is not efficiency-enhancing.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases participation in a binary-choice, team-based contest game where participation is individually costly and does not increase group welfare. However, it does not provide evidence that punishment increases efficiency or group payoff; in fact, the design ensures that increased participation is efficiency-reducing. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment depends critically on the structure of the game: in games where participation is not efficiency-enhancing, punishment may increase costly participation without improving (and possibly reducing) efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral responses (participation/cooperation) to punishment, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in standard public goods games.

- source: Cheung, G., & Siegel, D. A. (2025). The Costs of Collective Action: An Experimental Study of Risk, Punishment, and Immunity. *Journal of Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that higher punishment risk and cost reduce participation and group success among nonimmune individuals. Immune individuals are more likely to participate, but their participation is not affected by punishment risk or cost. As the number of immune individuals increases, nonimmune individuals participate less, consistent with free riding. Group size has no significant effect on nonimmune participation or group success, but immune individuals participate more in larger groups. The main outcomes are participation and group success, not efficiency or total group payoff. Average payoffs are reported, but not analyzed as a function of the experimental treatments. The findings support the theoretical model's predictions about the effects of punishment risk, cost, immunity, and group size on participation and collective action success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how external punishment risk and cost reduce participation and group success in a collective action setting, and how immunity and group size moderate these effects. However, the game is not a standard public goods game: the group benefit is all-or-nothing and depends on a random pivotal actor, and only participants can be punished by an external agent. There is no within-group punishment or contribution to a divisible public good. Efficiency as defined in public goods games (total group payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly analyzed, though average payoffs and group success rates are reported. The results are most relevant for predicting how external punishment risk and cost affect participation and group-level success in threshold-like or all-or-nothing collective action settings, especially when some individuals are immune to punishment. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGG efficiency predictions, but may inform models where external sanctions or risk of punishment for cooperation are present.

- source: Yin, X. L., Li, J. B., Li, D. H., & Chen, S. Y. (2023). When emotional responses conflict with self-interested impulses: A transcranial direct current stimulation study of cognitive control in cooperative norm compliance. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) is higher when punishment is costless and right DLPFC activity is inhibited (cathodal tDCS), driven by stronger negative emotional responses to norm violations. When punishment is costly, neither tDCS nor emotional response significantly affects TPP. The results suggest that the cognitive control process for norm compliance involves a conflict between emotional impulses to punish and self-interested motives to avoid cost. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological mechanisms of third-party punishment in a prisoner's dilemma variant, especially the role of cost in moderating punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that costly punishment may be less frequent and less sensitive to emotional impulses, which could imply a limited effect on efficiency, but this is not measured. The design and findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is used, not its impact on group welfare.

- source: Behnk, S., Barreda-Tarrazona, I., & García-Gallego, A. (2018). Punishing liars-How monitoring affects honesty and trust. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a severe, cost-free punishment mechanism for dishonesty in a sender-receiver game leads to a large increase in honest messages and trust by receivers, compared to a baseline with no punishment. This effect persists even when the probability of detecting dishonesty is reduced to 10%, except at a moderate detection probability (25%), where honesty is lower. The deterrence effect of punishment is less pronounced when the sender's potential gain from deception is high. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the behavioral results indicate that punishment can sustain high levels of honesty and trust with minimal monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a principal-agent (sender-receiver) environment with asymmetric information, the availability of severe, cost-free punishment for dishonesty can dramatically increase honest behavior and trust, even with low monitoring (detection) probabilities. However, the game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, and efficiency or group payoff is not measured. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like environments, the results suggest that punishment mechanisms with high severity and low cost may have strong positive effects on pro-social behavior, but the transfer to efficiency outcomes in PGGs is indirect. The findings are most relevant for settings where the main concern is honesty/truth-telling under asymmetric information, rather than contribution to a public good.

- source: Rostovtseva, V. V., Puurtinen, M., Salinas, E. M., Cox, R. F. A., Groothuis, A. G. G., Butovskaya, M. L., & Weissing, F. J. (2023). Unravelling the many facets of human cooperation in an experimental study. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that individuals are consistent in their prosocial and punishment-related decisions across a variety of economic games, but that punishment is not correlated with other prosocial tendencies. The appearance of interaction partners (as judged by facial attributes) systematically affects prosocial behavior in most games, but not in the punishment game. There is no evidence that facial appearance predicts actual prosocial behavior. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a suite of economic games including a punishment game, the tendency to punish is not associated with other prosocial behaviors and is not influenced by partner appearance. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in showing that punishment behavior is psychologically and behaviorally distinct from other forms of cooperation, and that its drivers may differ from those of prosociality. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always align with higher prosociality, but provides no direct evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Im, C., & Lee, J. (2022). On the fragility of third-party punishment: The context effect of a dominated risky investment option. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when a third party in a dictator game is offered a dominated risky investment option in addition to the punishment and safe options, both the frequency and amount of punishment decrease significantly compared to when only punishment and safe options are available. The demand for investment increases when all options are available, indicating a substitution effect. The results are robust to risk attitudes and to making the investment option strictly dominated (negative expected return). The findings support the hypothesis that context effects (specifically, the compromise effect) can undermine third-party punishment, even when the alternative is unattractive in both material and psychological terms. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of additional, even irrelevant, choice options can reduce the use of third-party punishment in dictator-game-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design of the choice set (e.g., presence of unrelated alternatives) can moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation or norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral mechanisms and the fragility of punishment as an enforcement tool in the presence of context effects.

- source: Li, M. P., Shangguan, C. Y., Shi, H. Q., & Lu, J. M. (2021). Watching Eyes Triggers Third-Party Punishment: The Role of Emotion Within the Eyes. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that displaying images of eyes (vs. control images) increases the frequency of third-party punishment in a modified dictator game, and that negative (angry) eyes further increase punishment compared to positive (happy) eyes. The effect is robust across varying degrees of unfairness in the dictator's offer. The outcome measured is the rate of punishment decisions, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that social cues of observation and negative evaluation can increase norm enforcement via punishment, but do not address the impact on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that social cues (watching eyes) and their emotional valence can increase the frequency of third-party punishment in a dictator game context. However, it does not provide data on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a third-party dictator game with costly punishment, but the outcome is behavioral (punishment frequency) rather than payoff-based. The findings may be useful for understanding psychological moderators of punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Alam, R., & Rai, T. S. (2025). Profitable third- party punishment destabilizes cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that profitable third-party punishment (i.e., paying punishers to punish) destabilizes cooperation: dictators are less likely to share when punishers are paid, both before and after any punishment is experienced. This effect is robust across multiple experimental designs, including repeated games and conditions optimized for reinforcement learning. Even when receivers (who benefit from cooperation) can choose the punishment regime, they systematically select profitable punishment, reducing their own expected earnings by about 20%. The negative effect of profitable punishment on cooperation is only eliminated when the game structure makes antisocial punishment impossible and this is clearly signaled. The main outcome is cooperation rate, but expected receiver earnings are also reported, showing a negative welfare effect. The study highlights the importance of the communicative function of punishment and the dangers of introducing profit motives into punishment systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing profit motives for punishment (i.e., making punishment profitable for the punisher) can reduce cooperation and lower welfare, even when punishment is otherwise available. While the experiments use dictator game variants rather than standard public goods games, the mechanisms are closely related: the presence and structure of punishment, and especially the motives signaled by punishment, are critical moderators of its effect on cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment is not always beneficial for efficiency—if punishment is profitable for the punisher, it may reduce efficiency compared to both unpaid punishment and no punishment. The effect is robust to repeated play and persists even when learning is optimized. The results also show that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether antisocial punishment is possible) is a key moderator. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than group efficiency, and the game is a dictator game variant, direct quantitative predictions for public goods games should be made with caution, but the qualitative direction is clear: profitable punishment can backfire.

- source: Koenig, B. L., & Riley, C. M. (2017). To what reference point do people calibrate cost-free, third-party punishment?. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned preferred punishments (fines) to a perpetrator in a theft scenario, with victim loss and perpetrator gain manipulated independently. Both victim loss and perpetrator gain influenced punishment preferences, with participants tending to select the larger of the two as a reference point. On average, preferred punishments exceeded the sum of victim loss and perpetrator gain. A substantial minority of participants preferred punishments much larger than this outcome differential, while a small minority preferred no punishment. When asked about compensation for the victim (rather than punishment for the perpetrator), participants' preferred amounts were lower and rarely exceeded the outcome differential. The study demonstrates that third-party punishment preferences are not simply proportional to harm or gain, but often exceed both, and that there is considerable heterogeneity in punitive preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is informative about the psychological calibration of punishment preferences in third-party contexts, showing that people often prefer punishments that more than offset both victim loss and perpetrator gain. This suggests that, in environments where third-party punishment is possible and costless, punishment levels may be set high, potentially deterring transgressions more strongly than would be predicted by models based solely on harm or gain. The findings are most relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punishment, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Chen, M. L., Zhu, X. L., Zhang, J., Ma, G. P., & Wu, Y. H. (2021). Neural correlates of proposers' fairness perception in punishment and non-punishment economic games. *Current Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Proposers overwhelmingly choose absolutely unfair offers in the dictator game (non-punishment), but shift to more relatively unfair or fair offers in the ultimatum game (punishment). The presence of punishment leads to more fair behavior by proposers, consistent with the idea that punishment deters unfairness. Neural data (MFN amplitude) and self-reported guilt indicate that proposers experience cognitive conflict and guilt when making unfair offers, even when punishment is not possible. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (ultimatum game) leads to more fair offers by proposers compared to the absence of punishment (dictator game). However, because the study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. It is informative for predicting behavioral changes (offer distributions) under punishment, but not for the downstream efficiency prediction task. The design is adjacent to public goods games, but not a direct variant.

- source: Yang, S. S., Chen, S. J., & Liu, Y. F. (2025). Perceptions of Economic Fairness Positively Affect Altruistic Punishment. *European Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Across five studies (including lab experiments with TPP games), the paper finds that individuals with higher perceptions of economic fairness are more likely to engage in costly punishment of norm violators. This effect is causal and robust to different manipulations of fairness perceptions. However, the effect is moderated by outcome efficacy: the positive effect of fairness perceptions on punishment only appears when punishment is expected to be effective at changing behavior or upholding norms. The main dependent variable is the amount of punishment assigned (tokens spent or likelihood of sending a disapproval message), not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about psychological moderators of punishment behavior (specifically, perceptions of economic fairness and outcome efficacy), but does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes in public-goods-like games. The experimental paradigm is adjacent to PGGs (third-party punishment of unfair dictator allocations), but the findings cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The results may inform models of when punishment is more likely to be used, but not its effect on group efficiency. Game design dimensions such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost are not systematically manipulated or reported, and efficiency is not measured.

- source: Qirko, H. (2020). Altruistic punishment in modern intentional communities. *Interaction Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that, in modern intentional communities, free-riding is often acknowledged as a problem and most communities have rules or norms to address it. However, only about half of the communities report actually enforcing these rules through third-party punishment, and even then, enforcement is context-dependent and often flexible. The data do not support the existence of a strong, inherited predisposition to engage in costly third-party punishment. Instead, punishment behavior appears to be shaped by contextual, self-maximizing considerations. No direct measures of group efficiency, welfare, or payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It suggests that, in real-world cooperative groups with explicit rules and opportunities for punishment, actual use of costly third-party punishment is infrequent and context-dependent. This implies that the presence of punishment mechanisms does not guarantee their use or an increase in efficiency, especially outside of laboratory or game-like settings. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding the limits of external validity for lab-based punishment effects and for highlighting the importance of contextual moderators.

- source: Klempt, C. (2012). Fairness, spite, and intentions: Testing different motives behind punishment in a prisoners' dilemma game. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that cooperators and defectors differ in their motives for punishment. Cooperators punish defectors more when defection is intentional, suggesting a role for retaliation, but still punish unintentional defectors, indicating some concern for payoff inequality. Defectors punish both cooperators and defectors equally, regardless of intentions, consistent with spiteful motives. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or prisoners' dilemma games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the motives behind punishment and how they may depend on the intentionality of actions, but it does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except to suggest that punishment behavior may depend on player type and perceived intentions, which could indirectly affect efficiency in other designs.

- source: Kupfer, T. R., & Tybur, J. M. (2023). Third-party punishers who express emotions are trusted more. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishers who express moral emotions (anger or disgust) are trusted more by others than those who punish financially or do not express emotions. Expressing emotions in addition to financial punishment further increases trust. The main outcome is the amount of money entrusted in a trust game, which is a behavioral measure of trust and reputation, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that emotion expression can enhance the reputational benefits of third-party punishment, potentially facilitating cooperation, but the study does not measure or report on efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment and emotion expression affect trust and reputation, which may be indirectly related to cooperation and efficiency, but no efficiency or payoff data are reported. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment, but may be useful for understanding reputational mechanisms and behavioral trust effects.

- source: Dato, S., & Friehe, T. (2025). Punishment for intentions or outcomes: the role of gender and social norms. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that second movers punish first movers more harshly for selfish intentions than for considerate ones, even when the outcome is held constant. The effect of the realized outcome (equal vs. unequal payoffs) on punishment is weak or absent overall, but women are more likely than men to increase punishment in response to unequal outcomes, while men are more responsive to unkind intentions. Social norm elicitation reveals that punishment is seen as more appropriate after norm violations (selfish choices or unequal outcomes), and these perceptions partially explain gender differences in punishment. The study does not report on efficiency, group welfare, or total payoffs, and the game is not a public goods game but a two-player lottery allocation game.
