  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the psychological and social-norm drivers of costly punishment in a two-player sequential lottery game, not a public goods game. While it demonstrates that punishment is sensitive to intentions and outcomes (with gender differences), it does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it use a multi-player or repeated public goods game structure. Thus, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited. However, it may inform mechanism design by highlighting that punishment is more likely when norm violations are perceived as intentional, and that gender composition may moderate punishment behavior. The absence of efficiency or group payoff analysis means it cannot be used to predict treatment efficiency from design dimensions or control efficiency.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2025). Protecting Public Goods or Helping Free Riders? A Real-Life Moral Dilemma in Interethnic and Intraethnic Encounters. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds robust evidence that bystanders are more likely to help in-group (native-majority) free riders and more likely to sanction out-group (ethnic-minority) free riders in a real-life public transport context. The behavioral outcome is whether the bystander helps the free rider (enabling norm violation) or sanctions (refuses or disapproves). The effect is consistent across locations, age groups, and sessions. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any explicit payoff-based outcome. The findings highlight the role of group membership in informal norm enforcement and the potential for double standards in sanctioning free riders.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on informal sanctioning of free riders in a real-world context, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves a public good (fare compliance) and the possibility of informal punishment (sanctioning) or anti-punishment (helping). The main insight for prediction is that the presence and effectiveness of informal sanctions (and their social selectivity) can depend strongly on group membership, and that in some real-world settings, bystanders may choose to help free riders rather than sanction them, especially for in-group members. However, because no efficiency or group payoff is measured, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in standard PGG-like games. Its main value is in highlighting the complexity and social contingency of informal punishment in naturalistic settings.

- source: Kodaka, F., Takahashi, H., Yamada, M., Takano, H., Nakayama, K., Ito, H., & Suhara, T. (2012). Effect of Cooperation Level of Group on Punishment for Non-Cooperators: A Functional Magnetic Resonance Imaging Study. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the degree of punishment assigned to a non-cooperator is significantly higher when the rest of the group is highly cooperative compared to when the group is less cooperative. This is reflected both in behavioral choices and in increased activation in the right dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and left anterior cingulate cortex. The results suggest that the social context of group cooperation modulates punitive behavior, but the study does not address efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the social context—specifically, the overall cooperation level of the group—modulates the severity of punishment assigned to non-cooperators. However, it does not provide any data on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is in highlighting that punishment behavior is context-dependent, which may be important for interpreting or modeling punishment effects in environments where group cooperation levels vary.

- source: Mitsuishi, K., & Kawamura, Y. (2025). Avoidance of altruistic punishment: Testing with a situation-selective third-party punishment game. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across three preregistered experiments, participants consistently avoided situations where they might have to punish unfairness, especially when direct punishment (with a real-effort cost) was possible. Avoidance was less pronounced when only indirect punishment (e.g., negative feedback) was available, and lowest when no punishment was possible. When participants could not avoid observing unfairness, they were likely to punish unfair behavior, regardless of their prior avoidance. The study provides evidence that the prevalence of third-party punishment in standard lab games may overstate its real-world frequency, as people often avoid such situations if possible. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that the opportunity for punishment (especially direct, costly punishment) leads to avoidance of situations where punishment decisions are required, and that indirect punishment options reduce this avoidance. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of the punishment opportunity (direct vs indirect, costliness) may affect participation rates in punishment, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Shen, Y., Deng, Z. X., Kang, H. W., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of tax-based rewards and mercenary punishment on public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that among German teachers and headmasters, willingness to sanction violations of the mask-wearing norm is significantly predicted by higher conscientiousness and neuroticism, but not by economic preferences such as risk or time. Leadership (headmaster) willingness to sanction is a strong predictor of teachers' willingness to sanction. Females are less willing to impose high fines than males. The outcome is self-reported fine size, not actual behavior or group payoff. The context is adjacent to public goods games, as mask-wearing is framed as a public good, but the study does not use a PGG or measure efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding individual differences in willingness to sanction in real-world cooperation norm contexts, especially newly introduced norms, and highlights the importance of personality and leadership. However, it does not report on actual punishment behavior, group cooperation, or efficiency outcomes, so it cannot be used to calibrate the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. Its main value is in identifying predictors of sanctioning attitudes, not payoff consequences.

- source: Zhang, Z., Li, M. H., Liu, Q. Y., Chen, C., & Qi, C. H. (2023). Group membership and adolescents' third-party punishment: a moderated chain mediation model. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Adolescents are more willing to assign harsher third-party punishment to outgroup (stranger) offenders than to ingroup (classmate) offenders in a hypothetical unfair allocation scenario. This ingroup favoritism is stronger for boys than for girls. The effect of group membership on punishment is mediated by perceived unfairness and anger. The study uses a self-report, costless punishment paradigm and does not involve real monetary incentives or actual group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. Its findings are limited to psychological and behavioral responses (punishment, unfairness, anger) in a third-party punishment context, using hypothetical scenarios. Therefore, it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff-based outcomes in public goods games or their variants. However, it may inform expectations about the social and psychological moderators of punishment behavior, such as group membership and gender.

- source: White, C. J. M., & van de Vondervoort, J. W. (2025). Third-party punishment, vigilante justice, or karma? Understanding the dynamics of interpersonal and cosmic justice. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three vignette-based experiments, the paper finds that: (1) third-party punishment (i.e., antisocial behavior directed at a transgressor) is evaluated more negatively than impersonal misfortune or reprimand, even when the punisher knows the victim's past bad behavior; (2) knowledge of the victim's past transgression makes punishment slightly less negative, but not positive; (3) reprimand is evaluated much more positively than actual punishment; (4) belief in karma predicts more positive evaluations of impersonal misfortune for bad actors, but does not increase approval of interpersonal punishment; (5) costliness of punishment to the punisher does not significantly affect evaluations. No efficiency, payoff, or group outcome measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or similar economic games with or without punishment. Its findings are about social and moral evaluations of punishment, not about behavioral or payoff consequences. It does, however, suggest that third-party punishment is often viewed negatively by observers, and that alternative responses (reprimand, impersonal punishment) are preferred, but this is not linked to efficiency or group payoff. The paper is not informative for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency.

- source: Shen, J. Y. (2024). Preferences for punishments: explorations from a stated-choice experiment in Japan. *Applied Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that there is substantial heterogeneity in preferences for different types of punishment (e.g., length of must-attend meetings, number of reprimands, pay cut duration, scope of name disclosure) that could be imposed on uncooperative team members. Four latent classes of punishment preference are identified, ranging from those with positive preferences for all punishments to those with negative preferences for all. Socioeconomic characteristics such as age, employment status, management experience, fairness, and trust are associated with class membership. The study does not measure or report on actual cooperation, contribution, or efficiency outcomes, nor does it manipulate or observe real punishment behavior in a public goods or similar game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It only reports on stated preferences for punishment types in a hypothetical scenario. No data is provided on actual game design dimensions, control efficiency, or treatment efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Davies, E., & Fafchamps, M. (2021). When no bad deed goes punished: Relational contracting in Ghana and the UK. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In repeated gift-exchange games, UK subjects use wage reductions as punishment for low effort and wage maintenance/increases as reward for high effort, resulting in higher effort, higher employer payoffs, and higher efficiency. Ghanaian subjects do not systematically punish low effort or reward high effort, and punishment (wage reduction) does not deter shirking—in fact, it may increase it. As a result, efficiency and employer payoffs are much lower in Ghana. Competition and reputation mechanisms do not close this gap. The findings show that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is context-dependent and not universal.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated principal-agent or gift-exchange games is highly context-dependent. In environments where punishment is used and anticipated (as in the UK), enabling punishment increases efficiency and employer payoffs. In environments where punishment is not used or is counterproductive (as in Ghana), enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and may even worsen outcomes. For prediction, the paper suggests that the presence of a punishment mechanism alone is not sufficient to guarantee higher efficiency; the effectiveness depends on whether participants use and respond to punishment as intended. Game design dimensions such as the ability to punish (via wage adjustment), the number of players, and the presence of reputation or competition do not guarantee efficiency gains from punishment unless the behavioral norms support its use.

- source: Suzuki, K., & Ishiwata, R. (2022). Impact of a Carbon Tax on Energy Transition in a Deregulated Market: A Game-Based Experimental Approach. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study used a 4-player, 30-round online game simulating energy companies in a deregulated market, with and without a carbon tax (punishment for fossil fuel use). The carbon tax was introduced in the second half of the game and was known to players in advance. The main findings are: (1) The carbon tax significantly increased renewable energy usage after its implementation, accelerating the energy transition. (2) Total group profit (efficiency) was higher with the tax, but the difference was not statistically significant. (3) The tax did not significantly affect R&D investment or market price in aggregate, but did increase market price after the tax was imposed. (4) Information about future taxation alone did not change behavior or reduce uncertainty; only actual taxation affected actions. (5) Players' perceptions of uncertainty and obstacles to transition were not reduced by the tax. Overall, the carbon tax improved efficiency-related outcomes by promoting renewables and increasing group profit, but only after its implementation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (carbon tax) in a competitive, multi-player, repeated game increases group efficiency (total profit) and accelerates the transition to socially optimal behavior (renewable energy use). The effect is most pronounced after the punishment is actually implemented, not merely announced. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar games is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is salient and directly affects payoffs. The game structure is adjacent to a public goods game, with competition and investment choices, so the findings are most applicable to PGG variants with market competition and investment dynamics. The effect size on efficiency is positive but may not always reach statistical significance, especially in small samples.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., Quezada, F. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2023). The endogenous formation of common pool resource coalitions. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous coalition formation, combined with the ability to monitor and sanction outsider poaching (via government-imposed fines), leads to larger coalitions, reduced resource exploitation, and higher individual and group earnings compared to open access. These effects are strongest when monitoring costs are low, enabling the grand coalition to form and fully deter poaching. When monitoring is not possible or is too costly, only small coalitions form and efficiency gains are minimal. Among fishers, coalition formation and monitoring are frequent and effective, while among students, coalitions are smaller, monitoring is rare, and efficiency gains are weak or absent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling punishment (here, government-imposed fines for poaching, triggered by coalition monitoring) increases efficiency in a CPR game, especially when monitoring costs are low. The effect is mediated by coalition size and the ability to deter outsiders. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the cost and effectiveness of monitoring and punishment, the ability to form coalitions, and the baseline efficiency under open access. The results suggest that, in environments where monitoring and punishment are feasible and not too costly, enabling punishment will substantially increase efficiency relative to control. However, if monitoring is impossible or prohibitively expensive, the efficiency gains from punishment are minimal. The findings are most applicable to real-world user groups with experience in collective management.

- source: Bar-Gill, O., & Engel, C. (2018). How to Protect Entitlements: An Experiment. *Journal of Law & Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the probability of efficient outcomes (good stays with owner) is high but not perfect, and is affected by the strength of the legal remedy (punishment for taking). Stronger remedies (higher damages or property rule) do not always increase efficiency; in some cases, weaker remedies lead to higher willingness to pay by owners and lower willingness to accept by takers, increasing the chance of a successful deal and thus efficiency. The heterogeneity of fairness norms (positive, negative, flat) and role-dependent beliefs (owners more negative, takers more positive) create information asymmetries that reduce the likelihood of efficient trade. The experiment shows that behavioral factors, not just economic incentives, shape the efficiency of bargaining under different sanction regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment or sanction strength on efficiency is not monotonic: increasing the severity of punishment (stronger legal remedy) does not always increase efficiency and can sometimes reduce it by increasing the gap between parties' willingness to pay and accept. For prediction, this suggests that the relationship between punishment severity and efficiency is mediated by behavioral fairness norms and is not simply positive. The findings are most relevant for settings where efficiency depends on successful bargaining under threat of sanction, and less so for standard public goods games with group punishment. The results caution against assuming that stronger punishment always increases efficiency, especially when fairness norms are heterogeneous.

- source: Brunner, M., & Ostermaier, A. (2018). Implicit communication in the ultimatum game. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated ultimatum game, disclosing responders' decisions to proposers (enabling implicit communication of disapproval/punishment) increases rejection rates for low offers. Proposers anticipate this and make higher offers, but not enough to fully offset the increased rejections. As a result, total group earnings (the sum of proposer and responder payoffs) are slightly lower with disclosure, though the difference is not statistically significant. The effect is most pronounced for moderately unfair offers. The study also finds that when offers are random (not chosen by proposers), disclosure reduces rejection rates, as responders avoid signaling envy. Overall, the main efficiency-relevant result is that punishment via rejection, when made salient through disclosure, does not increase group efficiency and may slightly reduce it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that enabling punishment-like behavior (rejection of unfair offers) through increased transparency (disclosure) does not improve efficiency in a bargaining game adjacent to public goods games. Instead, it may slightly reduce efficiency, as the cost of increased rejections is not fully offset by higher offers. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar environments where punishment is enabled or made more salient, efficiency may not increase and could decrease slightly, especially if the punishment is costly and not perfectly anticipated by those being punished. The findings are most directly relevant to bargaining/ultimatum games, but the mechanism—costly punishment reducing group surplus—may generalize to PGG-like settings.

- source: Johnson, D. B. (2016). (Please Don't) Say It to My Face! The Interaction of Feedback and Distance: Experiments with Vulgar Language. *Kyklos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The possibility of non-monetary punishment via unrestricted feedback increases proposer offers in the lab (where social distance is low), but not in online settings (where social distance is high). In the lab, known possible feedback leads to significantly higher offers, suggesting that the threat of negative feedback (punishment) can increase pro-social behavior and potentially improve monetary outcomes. However, in online/anonymous settings, the same feedback mechanism does not affect offers. The effect of punishment via feedback is thus moderated by social distance/proximity. The paper does not directly measure group efficiency as in public goods games, but the findings are relevant for understanding when non-monetary punishment can improve monetary outcomes in bargaining environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (here, non-monetary feedback) in increasing efficiency or pro-social monetary outcomes depends strongly on social distance and context. In environments with low social distance (e.g., lab, face-to-face), the threat of punishment can increase offers and thus improve monetary outcomes. In high social distance or anonymous environments (e.g., online), punishment has little or no effect on offers. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive only when social distance is low and the punishment is salient to the decision-maker. The findings are adjacent to public goods games but provide informative indirect evidence about the context-dependence of punishment effects.

- source: Song, X. Y., Lin, L. A., Wang, Z. C., Li, Y., & Liu, L. (2024). Preemptive strikes or not: the role of emotion on intergroup defensive aggression. *Current Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a group preemptive strike game, introducing punishment for non-contributors or assigning leadership increases the likelihood that group members will engage in preemptive attack (press the red button). This effect is mediated by increased fear (which increases attack) and hope (which decreases attack). However, the main outcome is behavioral (attack frequency), not efficiency or group payoff. The game rules indicate that preemptive attacks are costly for both groups, suggesting that increased attack rates likely reduce group efficiency, but this is not directly measured or reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that introducing punishment increases costly preemptive attack behavior in a group conflict game, which by the game rules reduces group welfare. However, the study does not report efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the game structure. The findings suggest that in environments where punishment increases costly defensive aggression, efficiency may decrease. The game is not a standard public goods game, but the mechanism (punishment increasing costly group-protective action) is adjacent. Use this paper as indirect evidence that punishment can increase costly, inefficient group-protective behaviors in some intergroup settings.

- source: Bshary, A., & Bshary, R. (2012). Individual Differences in Foraging Strategies of Parasitic Sabre-Tooth Blennies. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that individual blennies differ markedly in their foraging strategies, including how they respond to punishment by victim fish. Some blennies are more likely to switch victim species after being punished, while others are not. Laboratory experiments show that punishment can reduce the likelihood of attack or cause switching, but these effects are highly individual-specific. The paper discusses how only certain patterns of blenny response to punishment (specifically, switching to another species) would generate a public good for the victim group, while other patterns would not. However, the study does not measure group efficiency or payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment might or might not generate public goods in a natural system, depending on the behavioral rules of the punished party. However, it does not provide quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate game design dimensions in a way that would allow direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting that the effect of punishment on public good provision (and thus efficiency) may depend on the behavioral heterogeneity of the punished agents and the structure of the interaction (e.g., whether punishment causes switching to other species or not).

- source: Cisarovsky, G., Bshary, A., Bouzelboudjen, M., & Bshary, R. (2012). Spatial Group Structure as Potential Mechanism to Maintain Cooperation in Fish Shoals of Unrelated Individuals. *Ethology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that anthias in natural shoals have partially non-overlapping home ranges, and blennies (parasites) show site fidelity, attacking more frequently in certain areas. Individuals more exposed to the blenny may benefit more from punishing (chasing) the blenny, making punishment potentially self-serving rather than purely altruistic. This spatial structuring could help maintain the public good of punishment even in large, unrelated groups. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or direct fitness outcomes; it infers potential payoff implications from spatial and behavioral patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that spatial structure and local exposure can moderate the effectiveness and self-serving nature of punishment in public-goods-like dilemmas in natural systems. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate game design dimensions in a controlled way. For prediction tasks, the main value is in highlighting that spatial structure and heterogeneity in exposure can affect the distribution and potential benefits of punishment, but the paper does not provide direct input for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in standard PGG designs.

- source: Meiske, B., Alvarez-Benjumea, A., Andrighetto, G., & Polizzi, E. (2024). Nudging punishment against sharing of fake news. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that when participants observe others posting corrective comments in response to fake news, they are significantly more likely to post corrective comments themselves, and rate such enforcement as more socially appropriate. This effect is robust to controls for prior knowledge, political orientation, and other individual characteristics. The effect is stronger for more politically divisive headlines. There is no significant effect on the total number of comments (engagement), nor on empirical expectations about others' enforcement. The study does not measure or report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that visible social punishment (corrective comments) increases enforcement behavior and its perceived appropriateness in an online setting, which is adjacent to public goods game environments with peer punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most useful for understanding behavioral and normative mechanisms of enforcement, especially in online or social media contexts, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes in PGG-like games.

- source: Di Guida, S., Han, T. A., Kirchsteiger, G., Lenaerts, T., & Zisis, I. (2021). Repeated Interaction and Its Impact on Cooperation and Surplus Allocation-An Experimental Analysis. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: (1) Baseline (forced cooperation, exogenous re-matching), (2) Re-match (voluntary cooperation, exogenous re-matching), and (3) Endogenous-match (voluntary cooperation, endogenous group formation). Allowing refusal to cooperate (punishment) increases average contributions and receiver earnings, and decreases distributor earnings, relative to forced cooperation. However, allowing repeated interaction with the same group (endogenous matching) does not further increase average efficiency or payoffs compared to exogenous re-matching. Instead, repeated interaction leads to self-selection: some groups are highly cooperative and efficient, while others are unstable and inefficient. The most efficient groups are composed of 'greedy' distributors (who contribute more to avoid rejection) and 'modest' receivers (who accept lower offers). Overall, the possibility of punishment (refusal to cooperate) increases efficiency, but repeated interaction does not further increase average efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment via refusal to cooperate (i.e., making cooperation voluntary) increases efficiency and average payoffs compared to forced cooperation in a surplus allocation game with asymmetric bargaining power. However, simply allowing repeated interaction and endogenous group formation does not further increase average efficiency or payoffs, though it does create heterogeneity in group outcomes. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (even if only via refusal to cooperate) is likely to increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment. The effect of repeated interaction/endogenous matching is not to further increase average efficiency, but to increase variance across groups. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as the ability to refuse cooperation, but less so for costly punishment mechanisms typical in standard PGGs.

- source: Gordon, D., & Puurtinen, M. (2025). Fairness is What You Can Get Away With: Proposer and Responder Behaviour in a Collective Action Ultimatum Game. *Social Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The ease of collective action (operationalized as the probability of successful group punishment for a given investment) strongly affects both proposer and responder behavior in a multiplayer ultimatum game. When punishment is easy and effective, proposers immediately offer more equal splits, and group earnings are more equalized, with no significant difference between proposer and responder earnings. When punishment is hard or impossible, proposers keep more, and earnings are more unequal. Responders contribute more to punishment attempts when punishment is harder, but are also more likely to accept unfair offers as the game progresses. The main efficiency-related finding is that easier, more effective punishment leads to more equal and efficient group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness and cost of punishment (here, the ease of collective action) is a key moderator of the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments. When punishment is easy and effective, efficiency (measured as equality of earnings and group-level payoffs) increases substantially compared to control (no punishment). When punishment is hard or impossible, efficiency gains are minimal or absent. For prediction, this suggests that the punishment cost and effectiveness dimensions (punishment_cost, punishment_tech) are critical: low cost/high effectiveness punishment is likely to yield large efficiency gains, while high cost/low effectiveness punishment may not. The study also shows that group size (player_count = 4) and repeated rounds (num_rounds = 20) can support these effects, but the main moderator is the punishment mechanism.

- source: Kamei, K., & Tabero, K. (2025). Free riding, democracy, and sacrifice in the workplace: Evidence from a real-effort experiment. *Journal of Economics & Management Strategy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a real-effort lab experiment with teams of three, groups could either democratically vote or have randomly imposed a policy to reduce the private return from shirking (playing Tetris). The reduction was small and nondeterrent. Teams in the democratic (ENDO) condition exhibited significantly higher work productivity (correct answers per minute of teamwork) than those in the random (EXO) condition, regardless of whether the reduction policy was enacted. This effect (the 'dividend of democracy') was about 20% and persisted over time. However, democracy also led to more shirking (less work time), so total group output and payoffs did not significantly increase. The reduction policy itself had little effect, as shirking remained privately optimal for almost all teams. Communication analysis showed that support for the reduction policy was driven by intentions to work and beliefs about deterring others' shirking, but the policy's weakness limited its impact.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a democratic process (even with a weak, nondeterrent sanction) can substantially increase work productivity (efficiency per unit work time) in team-based, real-effort social dilemmas. However, unless the sanction is strong enough to change private incentives, total group output and payoffs may not increase, as increased productivity is offset by increased shirking. For prediction, the presence of a democratic process is a strong positive moderator of efficiency per work time, but the effectiveness of punishment/sanction depends on its deterrence strength. The experiment's design is adjacent to PGGs: it features a real-effort team task with revenue sharing and a private alternative activity, but not a standard PGG or explicit punishment tokens.

- source: Jiang, S. G., & Villeval, M. C. (2024). Dishonesty as a collective-risk social dilemma. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated group lying game with a probabilistic collective sanction (if group claims exceed a threshold, all group members lose their period payoff with 60% probability), most groups fail to coordinate on honest reporting. As a result, average payoffs are significantly below the efficiency benchmark (full honesty). Lying is common, and collective sanctions are frequent. Preferences for truth-telling (moral costs of lying) reduce the frequency of collective sanctions by over 40% compared to a no-lying variant, but are not strong enough to sustain efficiency. Smaller groups have lower average claims and fewer collective sanctions, leading to higher efficiency than larger groups. Mindset priming (collectivist vs. individualist) has only a modest, short-lived effect on claims and no robust effect on efficiency. Risk aversion is associated with lower claims and higher efficiency. Experience with sanctions does not lead to learning or improved efficiency; in fact, some participants increase lying after sanctions to recover losses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that in a collective-risk social dilemma with group-level, probabilistic sanctions (not targeted punishment), the introduction of such a sanction mechanism does not sustain high efficiency: most groups earn less than the full-cooperation benchmark. Efficiency is higher in smaller groups and when moral costs of lying are present, but the effect is partial. The findings suggest that, in public-goods-like environments with only collective (not targeted) punishment, efficiency is likely to be low unless additional mechanisms (e.g., peer punishment, communication) are present. The paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in games with group-level, probabilistic punishment, especially regarding the negative effect of group size and the limited effect of moral costs or mindset priming. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with targeted punishment, but the efficiency loss from non-targeted, collective punishment is clear.

- source: Wei, J., Zhong, Z. W., Chen, H. R., Arango-Aramburo, S., & Zhao, X. L. (2025). Exploring green electricity certificate purchasing behavior via a laboratory experiment. *Renewable & Sustainable Energy Reviews*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This laboratory experiment tests whether job rotation can reduce the ratchet effect in a principal-agent setting. Three treatments are compared: no rotation (baseline), exogenous rotation (forced switch), and endogenous rotation (principal can pay to switch). In the baseline, workers often restrict effort to avoid revealing private information, resulting in lower efficiency. Exogenous rotation nearly eliminates the ratchet effect, with almost all workers providing high effort and firms earning significantly more. Endogenous rotation, where the principal can pay a small cost to rotate workers, also reduces the ratchet effect and increases high effort, but the increase in firm earnings is not statistically significant. The findings support the idea that job rotation (especially exogenous) can improve efficiency by mitigating strategic output restriction, and that even a costly, non-credible threat of rotation (punishment) can have positive behavioral effects.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency, this paper provides strong evidence that introducing a costly punishment option (here, job rotation) can increase efficiency by reducing strategic shirking, especially when the punishment is exogenously imposed. The effect is weaker but still present when the punishment is endogenous and costly. The experimental design is not a standard public goods game, but the principal-agent structure with costly punishment and efficiency outcomes is closely related. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment (even at a cost) is likely to increase efficiency, with the effect size depending on whether the punishment is exogenous or endogenous and on the costliness of punishment.

- source: Hoffmann, R., Kittel, B., & Larsen, M. (2021). Information exchange in laboratory markets: competition, transfer costs, and the emergence of reputation. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that voluntary, costless information sharing among trustors in a repeated investment game leads to higher trustworthiness (trustees return more), higher trust (trustors send more), and higher payoffs for both trustors and trustees. When direct costs are imposed on information sharing, or when competition among trustors is introduced (making information sharing privately costly), information sharing drops sharply, and the efficiency gains from reputation mechanisms disappear. The effect of direct costs is highly non-linear: even small costs cause a large reduction in information sharing and efficiency. Competition among trustors increases their risk-taking (sending more), but reduces information sharing and trustworthiness, so the net effect on efficiency is ambiguous or negative. The study provides direct evidence that barriers to information sharing (costs or competition) undermine the emergence of reputation and reduce market efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costless, voluntary information sharing (a reputation mechanism) in repeated trust/investment games increases efficiency (group payoffs) relative to a control with no information sharing. However, even small costs to information sharing, or the introduction of competition among information holders, sharply reduce information sharing and eliminate efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of a costless, voluntary reputation/information sharing mechanism is a strong moderator of the efficiency effect. The findings are most directly relevant to trust/investment games, which are adjacent to PGGs, and to endogenous, voluntary, non-institutionalized punishment/sanctioning via information sharing. The results suggest that in environments where information sharing is costly or where competition discourages sharing, the efficiency benefits of reputation mechanisms will be minimal or absent.

- source: Muñoz-Herrera, M., & Reuben, E. (2023). The role of personal and impersonal relational contracts on partner selection and efficiency. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated trust game with and without competition and with either personal (free-form chat) or impersonal (numeric-only) communication, the study finds: (1) Without competition, personal relational contracts significantly increase market efficiency compared to impersonal ones, both when agreements are non-enforceable and partially enforceable. (2) With competition (i.e., when a more productive entrant is available), personal relational contracts do not increase efficiency relative to impersonal ones; in fact, impersonal contracts may yield higher efficiency, especially when agreements are partially enforceable. The mechanism is that personal contracts foster loyalty to incumbents, leading senders to continue trading with less productive partners, which reduces overall surplus. Impersonal contracts facilitate switching to more productive partners, capturing the efficiency gains from competition. The effect of communication on efficiency is thus context-dependent: positive without competition, mixed or negative with competition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling personal communication (chat) on efficiency is moderated by the presence of competition. In environments without competition, enabling chat (personal relational contracts) increases efficiency, regardless of enforceability. In environments with competition, enabling chat does not increase efficiency and may reduce it, especially when agreements are partially enforceable, because it increases loyalty to less productive partners. For prediction tasks, the presence of chat should be expected to increase efficiency only in non-competitive settings; in competitive settings, chat may have no effect or a negative effect on efficiency. The study does not use a standard PGG or explicit punishment, but the mechanisms (trust, reciprocity, loyalty, switching) are adjacent to those in public goods and punishment games.

- source: Bruttel, L., & Eisenkopf, G. (2012). No contract or unfair contract: What's better?. *Journal of Socio-economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a lab experiment comparing a sequential prisoner's dilemma (PD) to a vertically integrated version with incentive-compatible contracts (ICC), the introduction of contracts—regardless of fairness—increases joint payoffs from an average of 7.33 (PD) to 9.33 (ICC). Even unfair contracts yield higher welfare (9.02) than the best possible matching in PD. Most players cooperate under contracts, and the risk of defection in response to unfairness does not offset the efficiency gains. The study also finds that individual reciprocity types in PD do not predict negative reciprocity (defection) in ICC. Thus, the presence of incentive-compatible contracts robustly increases efficiency, even when some contracts are perceived as unfair.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player sequential prisoner's dilemma, introducing an incentive-compatible contract mechanism (analogous to a principal-agent or vertical integration structure) increases efficiency (joint payoff) compared to the baseline without contracts. The effect is robust to the fairness of the contract: even when the contract is unfair (unequal payoffs), efficiency is higher than in the baseline. The mechanism for increased efficiency is that contracts induce more cooperation, and the risk of 'punishment' via defection in response to unfairness is not large enough to negate the efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling a contract-like mechanism (even if not perfectly fair) in a similar environment is likely to increase efficiency relative to the control. However, the setting is a sequential PD, not a standard PGG, and punishment is not implemented as a costly action but as defection in response to unfairness.

- source: Riedl, A., Rohde, I. M. T., & Strobel, M. (2021). Free neighborhood choice boosts socially optimal outcomes in stag-hunt coordination problem. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that free neighborhood choice in a repeated stag-hunt game (coordination problem) substantially increases the frequency of efficient coordination and group welfare compared to imposed (fixed) neighborhoods. In the fixed-neighborhood condition, groups mostly converge to the inefficient equilibrium, while in the free-choice condition, efficient outcomes become dominant over time. The ability to exclude inefficient players (a form of endogenous, costly social sanction) is the key mechanism: excluded players are much more likely to switch to efficient behavior, and overall exclusion is infrequent but sufficient to sustain high efficiency. Welfare gains in the free-choice condition are significantly higher, especially in later rounds, despite exclusion being costly. The results suggest that even limited, endogenous social sanctions (via exclusion) can robustly promote efficient group outcomes in coordination settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous, costly exclusion (a form of social sanction) in a repeated coordination game can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when exclusion is rare and costly. While the setting is a stag-hunt (coordination) game rather than a public goods game, the mechanism—costly exclusion of inefficient actors—parallels punishment in PGGs. The findings suggest that, in environments where players can choose their partners and exclusion is possible but costly, efficiency can be much higher than in fixed-group settings. For prediction tasks, this implies that the presence of endogenous exclusion or partner choice should be expected to have a large positive effect on efficiency, especially over repeated rounds, even if the cost of exclusion is nontrivial. However, the results may not transfer directly to standard PGGs with explicit punishment stages, as the underlying game is a coordination problem, not a social dilemma.

- source: Pulcu, E. (2024). Individualistic attitudes in Iterated Prisoner's Dilemma undermine evolutionary fitness and may drive cooperative human players to extinction. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that human players, when interacting with individualistic (extortionate) computerized agents in an iterated UG/IPD setting, are unable to avoid being exploited, leading to significantly lower evolutionary fitness compared to their opponents. In contrast, interactions with prosocial agents result in higher mutual payoffs and more stable cooperation. Over time, cooperation erodes, and human players are at risk of 'extinction' (i.e., being outperformed in payoff terms) in 70% of cases against individualistic agents. The paper demonstrates that the structure of the opponent's strategy (prosocial vs. individualistic) is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes, but does not directly manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in iterated dyadic games structurally adjacent to public goods games, the presence of extortionate (individualistic) strategies leads to lower efficiency and higher risk of poor payoff outcomes for cooperative human players. While explicit punishment is not present, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where exploitation or extortion is possible. The results suggest that, absent effective countermeasures (such as punishment or reputation), efficiency will be lower when players face individualistic, non-cooperative strategies. For the prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency will be low when extortionate strategies are possible, and that mechanisms to counteract exploitation (such as punishment) may be necessary to sustain high efficiency.

- source: Kaplan, T. R., Ruffle, B. J., & Shtudiner, Z. (2018). Cooperation through coordination in two stages. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments of a two-player entry game: simultaneous moves (Now), endogenous timing (Wait), and exogenous sequential moves (Seq). In all treatments, players have private values and must decide whether to enter or exit, with payoffs depending on both players' choices. The main finding is that the Seq treatment yields the highest average pair profits (mean 170.2), followed by Wait (167.9), and Now (163.2). The difference between Seq and Now is statistically significant. The two-stage games allow cooperative pairs to coordinate more efficiently and achieve higher profits, but also allow uncooperative pairs to do worse, leading to a wider distribution of outcomes. The Wait and Seq treatments reduce double exit and increase efficient cooperation compared to Now, but also see a modest increase in double entry. The paper does not study punishment as a formal mechanism, but discusses how uncooperative behavior in the two-stage games can be met with retaliatory entry (a form of endogenous punishment).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that changing the timing structure of a two-player entry game (from simultaneous to sequential or endogenous timing) can increase average efficiency, but with substantial heterogeneity: cooperative pairs benefit, while uncooperative pairs may do worse. The results are highly relevant for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the timing of moves, but less so for games where formal punishment mechanisms (with explicit costs and impacts) are present. The findings suggest that enabling sequential or observable timing can improve efficiency, especially when players can coordinate, but may also increase the risk of low efficiency if players are uncooperative. The paper does not manipulate or analyze formal punishment costs or technologies, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled public goods games is limited, but it is informative about the role of timing and endogenous retaliation.

- source: Arai, S., Tooby, J., & Cosmides, L. (2023). Why punish cheaters? Those who withdraw cooperation enjoy better reputations than punishers, but both are viewed as difficult to exploit. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that efficiency (normalized group payoff) in repeated prisoners' dilemma games is highest when public monitoring is perfect and decreases as noise in the public signal increases. For low and moderate noise, efficiency is significantly above the one-shot Nash equilibrium, but for high and infinite noise, efficiency approaches or falls below the one-shot level. Subjects use simple threshold strategies, conditioning their cooperation on the most recent public signal. The effect of noise is robust and statistically significant. The study does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms; instead, punishment is implemented as a phase of mutual defection triggered by low public signals. The results provide strong evidence that the ability to sustain efficient cooperation in repeated games is highly sensitive to the quality of public monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence that, in repeated games with endogenous punishment (via reversion to Nash equilibrium), efficiency is strongly moderated by the quality of public monitoring. When public signals are accurate (low noise), efficiency is high; as noise increases, efficiency declines. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous punishment, but less so for public goods games with explicit costly punishment options. The results suggest that, holding control efficiency constant, the effect of enabling punishment (via reversion to Nash) is highly dependent on the observability of others' actions. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that efficiency gains from punishment are greatest when monitoring is accurate, and that these gains diminish or disappear as monitoring becomes noisy.

- source: Balafoutas, L., Dimant, E., Gächter, S., & Krupka, E. (2024). Social norms: Enforcement, breakdown & polarization. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Participants learned to cooperate and achieved high average payoffs against both tit-for-tat and Pavlov strategies in a standard context. However, when the context signaled the computer's probability of cooperation (spinner), cooperation and payoffs were higher against tit-for-tat than Pavlov. When participants believed they were playing another person, cooperation and payoffs were higher against Pavlov than tit-for-tat. Thus, the effect of the 'punishment-like' feedback embedded in tit-for-tat and Pavlov depends on both the strategy and the context. The paper provides direct evidence on average payoffs (efficiency) under different strategy-context combinations, but does not manipulate or isolate explicit punishment mechanisms as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how feedback strategies (tit-for-tat, Pavlov) and context affect efficiency (average payoff) in repeated, alternating-move prisoner's dilemma games. While not a public goods game, the payoff structure and the embedded reinforcement/punishment mechanisms are closely related. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like feedback on efficiency is context-dependent: tit-for-tat (which punishes defection) increases efficiency in some contexts but not others, and Pavlov (which is less punitive) can also support high efficiency depending on context. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the design of feedback/punishment and the social framing of the game can moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms, even when control efficiency is known.

- source: Flannery, T., & Wang, S. Y. (2023). Is the smoke-filled room necessary? An experimental study of the effect of communication networks on collusion. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: In a repeated three-player Bertrand game, the ability for all players to communicate (full network) leads to the highest group payoffs and cooperation rates. Treatments with only partial communication (either a leader or a pairwise link) do not improve payoffs over the no-communication baseline, and in some cases, payoffs are lower due to the use of punishment strategies (promise and threat) that are followed through after deviations. The structure of communication networks shapes both the strategies communicated and the realized efficiency: full communication supports more 'pure promise' messages and higher payoffs, while asymmetric or partial communication leads to more threats and lower efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that communication structure is a key moderator of the efficiency gains from communication/collusion in repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated collusion games structurally similar to public goods games, the efficiency gains from enabling communication depend critically on the communication network structure. Full, symmetric communication among all players leads to the highest efficiency, while partial or asymmetric communication can result in lower efficiency than even the no-communication baseline, due to increased punishment and failed coordination. For prediction tasks, the presence and structure of communication (chat) is a key moderator: enabling chat among all players is likely to increase efficiency, but partial or asymmetric chat may not. The study does not include explicit monetary punishment or reward mechanisms, but does show that endogenous punishment via threat/follow-through in communication can reduce efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated games with communication as a design dimension.

- source: dos Santos, M., Placì, S., & Wedekind, C. (2015). Stochasticity in economic losses increases the value of reputation in indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that stochasticity in economic losses does not increase mean helping rates or mean group earnings, but it does increase the payoff advantage of having a good reputation. Under stochastic losses, generous players are helped more and selfish players are helped less, leading to a steeper positive relationship between generosity and final earnings. In stable environments, this relationship is absent. Thus, stochasticity increases the efficiency of reputation-based cooperation by making investment in reputation more beneficial, even though overall group efficiency does not increase.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in repeated helping games with indirect reciprocity, environmental stochasticity (randomness in losses) increases the payoff return to cooperative behavior (reputation), even if mean group efficiency does not change. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion or helping) on efficiency may be more positive in environments with stochastic payoffs or risks, because the social system more effectively rewards cooperators and penalizes defectors. However, the game is not a standard public goods game and does not feature explicit costly punishment; rather, it is an indirect reciprocity helping game. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation or exclusion is the main sanctioning mechanism, and where environmental risk is present.

- source: D'Exelle, B., & Riedl, A. (2013). Resource Allocations and Disapproval Voting in Unequal Groups. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Disapproval voting as a punishment mechanism in unequal groups does not consistently improve efficiency or prosocial allocations. Instead, its effectiveness is highly sensitive to institutional details. When villagers have only private information about their share and voting is public, disapproval rates are low and representatives often use a minimal coalition (exclude-one) strategy, which avoids disapproval and maintains higher group earnings, though at the cost of excluding the poorest villager. When allocations are common knowledge or voting is secret, disapproval rates are higher, leading representatives to frequently switch to the exclude-all strategy, which is inefficient as it maximizes the representative's payoff at the expense of the group. The punishment mechanism can thus backfire, reducing efficiency, especially when disapproval is frequent. The study highlights the importance of information structure and voting rules in moderating the effects of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment mechanisms (here, disapproval voting) on efficiency in resource allocation games is not universally positive and depends critically on the design dimensions, especially information disclosure and the publicity of voting. In settings where punishment is possible but disapproval is rare (private information, public voting), efficiency is higher, but this is achieved by excluding the least powerful group member. In settings where punishment is more likely (common information, secret voting), efficiency is lower due to frequent costly disapproval and representatives' defensive strategies. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the effect is moderated by how information and punishment are structured. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported, but the treatment effects indicate that punishment can reduce efficiency if it triggers defensive, exclusionary, or retaliatory behavior by the allocator.

- source: Müller, J., Schwieren, C., & Spitzer, F. (2022). How to prevent destruction - On the malleability of anti-social behavior. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper demonstrates that anti-social destruction of group resources in a laboratory setting is highly sensitive to contextual factors. When the destruction opportunity is presented in a transactional, market-like framing and is the only available activity, subjects destroy a large share of the group's resources, sharply reducing group payoff. However, if the destruction is framed as harming jointly owned property, or if subjects have a parallel, meaningful activity (real-effort task) to occupy them, destruction is almost eliminated and group payoffs are preserved. The effect of these interventions is large and robust. The study does not use a public goods game or punishment of free riders, but the findings are relevant for understanding how institutional design and framing can prevent anti-social, payoff-reducing behaviors in group settings.
  prediction_guidance: While this study does not directly test public goods games or punishment, it provides strong evidence that institutional context (such as framing and the presence of alternative activities) can dramatically affect the prevalence of anti-social, payoff-reducing behaviors. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in group games, the results suggest that interventions making the negative consequences of anti-social actions salient to all, or providing alternative meaningful activities, can greatly improve group efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where group resources can be destroyed at a cost, but the general principle—that context and framing can strongly moderate destructive or anti-social behavior—may inform predictions about punishment and efficiency in related environments.

- source: Büyükboyaci, M., Karagözoglu, E., & Küçüksenel, S. (2025). Productive and Unproductive Effort Choices in Groups and Sharing: An Experimental Study. *Managerial and Decision Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the opportunity cost of unproductive investment (i.e., making productive investment more attractive) does not significantly increase productive investment rates. Subjects' own allocation choices do not significantly affect their giving in the distribution stage, but they give less to human partners who chose unproductive investment, indicating a punishment mechanism. This punishment is not applied when the unproductive choice is made by a computer. The size of the surplus (group payoff) is determined by the sum of productive investments, but the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or compare group payoffs across treatments. The main behavioral outcome is the use of distributive punishment in response to unproductive choices by humans.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment. The design is adjacent to a PGG: resource allocation between productive and unproductive uses, with endogenous surplus and a contest for control. There is no explicit punishment stage, but distributive punishment (giving less in the dictator game) serves a similar function. The main implication is that increasing the marginal return to productive investment (analogous to MPCR) does not necessarily increase productive investment or efficiency. Punishment (via lower giving) is selectively applied to human partners who make unproductive choices, but this does not feed back to increase productive investment rates. Thus, the presence of a punishment-like mechanism does not guarantee higher efficiency in this context. For prediction, this suggests that punishment may not always improve efficiency, especially if it is not structured as a direct, costly sanction and if it does not change initial contribution behavior.

- source: Gillenkirch, R. M., & Kreienbaum, H. (2017). What guides subjective performance evaluation: Incentive alignment or norm enforcement?. *Review of Managerial Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that superiors are primarily motivated by norm enforcement: they pay more for information that allows them to reward high and punish low effort, and their allocation decisions reflect a willingness to punish norm violations. Subordinates increase their effort when they know their effort can be observed and evaluated. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total surplus, but higher effort levels in conditions with more informative evaluation suggest that group output and subordinate payoffs increase when norm enforcement is possible. However, the focus is on behavioral mechanisms (effort, information acquisition, allocation) rather than on efficiency as a formal outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling punishment (via subjective evaluation and information acquisition) increases effort and likely increases group output in a team production setting with a free rider problem. However, it does not report efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The design is adjacent to PGGs: two subordinates choose effort (costly), output is the sum of efforts plus noise, and a superior (not a group member) allocates the output. The 'punishment' is implemented via allocation decisions, not explicit costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that mechanisms enabling norm enforcement (punishment) can increase effort and likely improve group outcomes, but the lack of direct efficiency data and the non-standard game structure limit its direct applicability to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Watson, R., Morgan, T. J. H., Kendal, R. L., van de Vyver, J., & Kendal, J. (2024). Investigating the effects of social information on spite in an online game. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants overwhelmingly chose altruism over spite when given a costly choice to increase or decrease an anonymous partner's payoff. Exposure to social information indicating others had behaved spitefully (vs. altruistically) reduced the degree of altruism, but did not substantially increase spiteful acts. The source of social information (majority vs. most successful) had no effect. The study does not report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the design is a one-shot, two-player, non-iterated game with a continuous choice from costly spite to costly altruism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated group games. The design is a one-shot, two-player, continuous-choice game where participants can pay to harm or help an anonymous partner, and the main outcome is a behavioral slider, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no repeated interaction, no group context, and no explicit public goods structure. The findings suggest that when both costly spite and costly altruism are available, spite is rare and social information can reduce altruism, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Geschwind, S., & Lambsdorff, J. G. (2025). Does scarcity induce hostility? An experimental investigation of common-pool resources. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when scarcity is perceived as human-induced (overexploitation), participants are more likely to destroy each other's resources in the subsequent stage, indicating increased hostility.
