When scarcity is exogenously imposed (low reproduction), destruction is lower, suggesting that environmental scarcity can foster restraint and cooperation. The destruction mechanism is not a standard punishment stage: destruction is costless, partially hidden (due to random 'bear' destruction), and not directly strategic. The study finds that reciprocity (retaliation for overexploitation) is a key driver of destruction, while inequality aversion plays a limited role. Overharvesting is substantial in the CPR game, leading to welfare losses, but the main focus is on behavioral responses (hostility) rather than efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency outcomes for a standard PGG with or without punishment, it shows that the presence of a destruction (punishment-like) stage increases hostile behavior when scarcity is human-induced, and decreases it when scarcity is exogenous. The destruction mechanism differs from standard costly punishment: it is costless, partially hidden, and not designed to change future behavior. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency may depend on the perceived cause of scarcity and the design of the punishment stage. The paper is most informative for understanding how the framing and cause of scarcity moderate the impact of punishment-like options on group behavior, but does not provide direct data for predicting efficiency changes from enabling punishment in standard PGGs.

- source: Leppänen, I., & Hämäläinen, R. P. (2017). Emotions in a Repeated Cournot Duopoly Game: A Psychophysiological Experiment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that emotional responses are closely tied to reciprocal adjustments in a repeated Cournot duopoly game. When a player observes that the other did not cooperate (i.e., the other increased production), the player is more likely to make an upward adjustment (interpreted as punishment), and the size of this adjustment is positively correlated with emotional arousal. Disgust is associated with advantageous inequity (own one-sided gain), and positive affect is associated with mutual gains. The study provides evidence that emotions play a role in motivating reciprocal (including punitive) behavior in repeated social dilemmas, but it does not directly measure or report the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The Cournot duopoly is adjacent to public goods games, and punishment is implemented as endogenous behavioral adjustment rather than as a discrete, explicit punishment option.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the emotional and motivational underpinnings of reciprocal (including punitive) behavior in repeated social dilemmas with continuous strategy spaces. It suggests that endogenous punishment-like responses (upward adjustments after defection) are emotionally motivated and can be costly for both players, potentially reducing efficiency. However, because the study does not manipulate explicit punishment mechanisms or report efficiency outcomes as a function of punishment, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding the psychological processes underlying endogenous punishment in adjacent game structures, but should not be used as direct quantitative evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Kaba, M., Koyuncu, M., Schneider, S. O., & Sutter, M. (2024). Social norms, political polarization, and vaccination attitudes: Evidence from a survey experiment in Turkey. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both pro- and anti-vaxxers discriminate against each other in allocation tasks, with pro-vaxxers showing stronger outgroup bias (interpreted as 'punishment' of anti-vaxxers for norm violation). Political polarization amplifies this discrimination. Randomized informational treatments (reminders of pandemic health/economic costs or the Russia-Ukraine war) reduce outgroup bias, especially the war treatment among pro-vaxxers. However, the main outcome is behavioral (allocation bias), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The allocation tasks and modified public goods game measure outgroup discrimination, not group efficiency or total earnings. The findings may be useful for understanding how social identity and polarization affect norm enforcement and discrimination, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Batsaikhan, M., & Putterman, L. (2019). An Honest Day's Pay: Cooperation among Entrepreneurs vs. Students, and Linkages to Real-World Business Success. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Entrepreneurs in Mongolia display higher and increasing cooperation in a repeated property rights dilemma game compared to students, as measured by more tokens allocated to production and collective protection and fewer to theft. The opportunity to vote for mandatory collective protection (akin to a tax) is more frequently chosen by entrepreneurs, but this does not substantially change their already high cooperation levels. The chat treatment (pre-scripted messages) is associated with higher collective protection among entrepreneurs. There is no formal punishment mechanism (costly sanctions) as in standard PGGs; theft can be used as retaliation, but this is not a direct analog. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but discusses how behavioral allocations relate to social optimum. More successful entrepreneurs in real life are more cooperative in the lab. The main outcomes are behavioral, not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. The experimental design lacks a formal costly punishment mechanism; instead, it features theft (which is a form of negative externality, not a sanction) and collective protection (akin to public good provision). While the paper discusses how cooperation (less theft, more production, more collective protection) would increase efficiency, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, nor does it manipulate punishment in the relevant sense. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, though it provides contextual evidence about cooperation, institutional choice, and behavioral trends in repeated social dilemmas.

- source: Cao, N. M., Miao, R. R., Sun, B. H., Ren, Z. R., & Yue, G. A. (2024). Exemplary Self-Discipline, Leniency towards Others: Competitive Contexts Amplify the Black Sheep Effect in Restoring Ingroup Trust. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that after a trust violation and apology, participants are more willing to restore trust (measured by increased investment) with out-group members than with in-group members, but this effect is only present in competitive contexts. In cooperative contexts, trust is restored equally for both in-group and out-group members. The results are interpreted as evidence for the 'black sheep effect' in trust repair, moderated by the nature of the interaction (competition vs. cooperation). The outcome is investment behavior, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a repeated trust game (not a PGG), and the main outcome is trust restoration (investment behavior) rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no explicit punishment mechanism; the 'punishment' is implicit in reduced trust/investment after a violation. The findings may be relevant for understanding how group identity and context affect willingness to restore cooperation after norm violations, but they do not provide quantitative or direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with explicit punishment options.

- source: Baik, K. H., Chowdhury, S. M., & Ramalingam, A. (2021). Group size and matching protocol in contests. *Canadian Journal of Economics-revue Canadienne D Economique*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Tullock contests, partner matching (fixed pairs) leads to lower bids and less overbidding compared to random stranger matching, but only in two-player groups. In three-player groups, matching protocol has no significant effect on bids or overbidding. Bid dispersion is also lower in smaller groups. The results suggest that identifiability and repeated interaction facilitate tacit collusion in small groups, reducing wasteful overbidding. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral measures (bids and overbidding) rather than direct efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency in contest-like environments, especially regarding the effects of group size and repeated interaction (partner matching). While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the finding that partner matching reduces overbidding in two-player groups suggests that efficiency losses from overbidding may be mitigated in such settings. For prediction tasks, the results imply that small group size and repeated interaction can reduce wasteful resource expenditure (overbidding), potentially increasing efficiency, but this is inferred from bid behavior rather than direct efficiency measurement. The absence of punishment or public goods framing limits direct transfer to PGG-like environments.

- source: Hoffmann, L. (2022). Cooperation in the name of God? Experimental evidence from Ghana and Tanzania. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that religious reminders of punishment or reward decrease cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma among Christian and Muslim participants in Ghana and Tanzania. The punishment prime reduces cooperation by about 8 percentage points, and the reward prime by about 6 percentage points, relative to the control. There is no evidence that religious primes increase cooperation. The study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and beliefs/expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma (not a repeated PGG), and the 'punishment' and 'reward' treatments are religious primes, not institutionalized peer punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. Thus, the paper is only weakly informative for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that not all forms of punishment/reward framing increase cooperation, and that context and mechanism matter.

- source: Cagala, T., Glogowsky, U., Grimm, V., Rincke, J., & Tuset-Cueva, A. (2019). Rent extraction and prosocial behavior. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when an administrator actively chooses to extract a 10% rent from contributions, average contributions to non-profit associations drop significantly (by 41%) compared to a control where the same share is assigned passively. This effect is robust to framing and is interpreted as a motivational effect: rent extraction undermines contributors' general willingness to give, rather than triggering targeted negative reciprocity. The effect persists even when contributors interact with a new, uninvolved administrator in a subsequent round, indicating a spillover on generosity. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the design is a donation game with some similarities to public goods games but is not a standard PGG.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that visible anti-social actions by an authority figure (rent extraction) can sharply reduce prosocial contributions in a donation context, even when the price of giving is held constant. However, because the outcome is contribution rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a donation game rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The result suggests that introducing a salient, self-serving authority or punishment-like mechanism that is perceived as unfair or anti-social may reduce cooperation and thus efficiency, but the direct quantitative effect on efficiency in a PGG is not measured here.

- source: Lu, X. Q., Li, T., Li, B. M., & Yang, Z. (2025). The influence of third-party's power on multi-player fairness decision-making. *Acta Psychologica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that participants care about fairness towards third parties in three-person ultimatum games, but their concern is modulated by whether the third party has decision power. When the third party is powerless, participants enforce fairness norms and show both disadvantageous and advantageous inequity aversion. When the third party has power, participants incorporate both fairness and social comparison norms, showing greater disadvantageous inequity aversion and even advantageous inequity preference towards the third party. The presence of a powerful third party leads to diffusion of responsibility, reducing the participant's willingness to punish unfairness. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to efficiency prediction in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and norm enforcement mechanisms may operate in multi-player bargaining contexts, especially regarding the psychological mechanisms (inequity aversion, responsibility, social comparison) that underlie acceptance/rejection of unfair offers. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting that the structure of power and responsibility among players can modulate willingness to punish norm violations, which may, in turn, affect efficiency in related but not identical game structures.

- source: Stiff, C. (2008). Are They Bothered? How the Opportunity to Damage a Partner's Reputation Influences Giving Behavior in a Trust Game. *Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The opportunity to leave feedback (damage a partner's reputation) increases the amount participants are willing to give in a one-shot trust game, but only when the partner is perceived to care about their reputation. This effect is strongest when the partner's future interaction group consists of strangers rather than friends. The effect of feedback opportunity and group familiarity on giving is mediated by the participant's perceived control over the partner's reputation. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total welfare, and the design is a dyadic trust game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based indirect sanctions (opportunity to leave feedback) can increase cooperative behavior (giving/trust) in dyadic trust games, especially when the target is reputation-sensitive. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of reputation-based sanctions depends on the perceived concern for reputation and the social context of future interactions. For prediction tasks, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that indirect, reputation-based sanctions can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency effects or direct evidence for public goods games with explicit punishment mechanisms.

- source: Liu, W. X., Wang, H., Zhu, H. J., Zhu, X. Y., He, X. Y., & Zhang, W. (2022). Morality is Supreme: The Roles of Morality, Fairness and Group Identity in the Ultimatum Paradigm. *Psychology Research and Behavior Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, responders are more likely to accept offers (including unfair ones) from proposers with positive moral backgrounds than from those with negative moral backgrounds. This effect is robust across fair, moderately unfair, and extremely unfair offers. Group identity, manipulated via moral priming, has only a partial effect: positive or negative moral priming increases acceptance rates for positive moral proposers in unfair allocations, but does not significantly affect responses to negative moral proposers. The gender of the proposer does not significantly affect acceptance rates. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are not directly transferable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in ultimatum game settings, the perceived morality of the proposer strongly influences acceptance rates, even for unfair offers. However, because the outcome measured is acceptance rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the ultimatum game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms, costs, or efficiency, so it does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of social/moral context in responder behavior, which may inform broader models of cooperation and norm enforcement.

- source: Bengart, P., Gruendler, T., & Vogt, B. (2021). Acute tryptophan depletion in healthy subjects increases preferences for negative reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that lowering serotonin via ATD increases negative reciprocity in the Hawk-Dove game: participants in the ATD group are more likely to choose Hawk in response to an expected Hawk, compared to placebo. This effect is specific to negative reciprocity and does not extend to positive reciprocity or beliefs about the opponent's action. The incentive for mutual cooperation (payoff for mutual Dove) reduces Hawk choices. Risk attitude and mood are unaffected by ATD and do not predict strategy choice. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that biological factors (serotonin availability) can modulate negative reciprocity in a competitive, dyadic game, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or sanctions in public goods or similar games. The Hawk-Dove game is structurally adjacent to public goods and punishment games, but lacks explicit punishment or sanctioning stages, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, the findings are only weakly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The main relevance is in showing that negative reciprocity (costly, non-payoff-maximizing responses to perceived unkindness) can be increased by serotonin depletion, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff in multi-player, repeated, or sanction-enabled games is limited.

- source: Kusakawa, T., Ogawa, K., & Shichijo, T. (2012). An experimental investigation of a third-person enforcement in a prisoner's dilemma game. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that when a third party (M) can observe the actions of two players in a one-shot PD, the cooperation rate between those two players is significantly higher than when M cannot observe. This is attributed to the threat of future punishment by M in subsequent repeated PD games. The effect is measured in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or total payoff. The study provides evidence that third-party observation and the possibility of future sanctioning can facilitate cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a third-party observer with future sanctioning power can increase cooperation rates in a prisoner's dilemma setting. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or directly measure efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The main implication is that third-party monitoring and the threat of future punishment can be a strong facilitator of cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in related settings, but this is not directly tested. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate standard punishment mechanisms (e.g., costly punishment within the group), so caution is warranted in transferring the findings.

- source: Bica, A., & Cretu, R. Z. (2023). The carrot and the stick: How guilt and shame facilitate reciprocity-driven cooperation. *Cognition & Emotion*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Inducing guilt in participants led to more generous offers to fair partners (positive reciprocity), while inducing shame led to higher rejection rates of unfair offers and lower offers to unfair partners (negative reciprocity). Both emotions influenced fairness-related decision-making, but via different mechanisms. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral responses (acceptance, proposal amounts) in the Ultimatum Game. The findings suggest that moral emotions can facilitate cooperation, but the pathways differ: guilt promotes generosity toward fair actors, while shame motivates punishment of unfairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the behavioral mechanisms (reciprocity, punishment, reward) that can affect cooperation in economic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment-enabled games. The Ultimatum Game is adjacent to public goods games, and the punishment here is endogenous (rejection of unfair offers) rather than a separate costly punishment mechanism. The findings may inform expectations about how emotional states or social context could moderate the impact of punishment or reciprocity on cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes under different game design dimensions.

- source: Gomes, C. M., Mulhinch, M. R., & McCullough, M. E. (2025). People decide whether to share social information-and whether to share it accurately-on the basis of self-interest. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that people are more likely to share reputational information (gossip) when they have behaved generously in a prior economic game, and less likely when they have behaved greedily. The tendency to share information honestly or dishonestly is strongly influenced by whether the participant stands to benefit from being chosen as a partner for a subsequent economic game. When lying is possible and being chosen is incentivized, greedy or indifferent participants are more likely to share dishonest, self-serving gossip. Generous participants are more likely to share honest information, especially when honesty is required and being chosen is incentivized. The results support reputation-based partner choice and social competition theories over indirect reciprocity theory. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it offers informative context about the strategic use of social information (gossip) in economic games with partner choice and reputation. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of information-sharing mechanisms (and their honesty) depends on participants' incentives and prior behavior, which may be relevant for understanding the role of reputation and information in environments with punishment or partner choice. Since no efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, this paper should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment, but may inform mechanistic expectations about when information-based interventions (including punishment or reputation) are likely to be effective or undermined by dishonesty.

- source: Zhang, Q. W., Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, X. R. (2024). Reputation-based disconnection-reconnection mechanism in Prisoner's Dilemma Game within dynamic networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that a reputation-based mechanism, where players can disconnect from low-reputation neighbors and reconnect with others, significantly increases the frequency of cooperation in repeated PDG simulations on dynamic networks. The effect is stronger when the reputation factor (alpha) is high and when the network's information connectivity (environmental degree r, rewiring probability p) is high. Conversely, when the selection intensity (sigma) is high, the effect of reputation is diminished and cooperation collapses. The results are consistent across both square lattice and small-world network topologies. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it shows that reputation-based dynamic linking mechanisms can strongly increase cooperation rates in repeated PDG environments. The findings suggest that, in analogous PGG-like settings, mechanisms that allow for social selection or partner choice based on reputation may increase cooperation, which could plausibly translate into higher efficiency if payoffs are aligned with cooperation. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency changes. The results are most relevant for settings where dynamic partner selection or reputation-based exclusion is possible.

- source: Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., & Suzuki, T. (2025). Gradual reputation dynamics evolve and sustain cooperation in indirect reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The experimental results show that people use a three-level (good, neutral, bad) reputation system and update reputations gradually: cooperation increases reputation by one level, defection decreases it by one, except that defection against a bad recipient (justified defection) leaves reputation unchanged. Theoretical evolutionary game modeling demonstrates that, under certain conditions, tolerant norms that cooperate with good and neutral and defect only against bad can dominate and sustain high cooperation rates. However, the study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency or payoff; it focuses on reputation dynamics and the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its findings are adjacent: it informs how nuanced reputation and justified defection (a form of social sanction) are perceived and how these perceptions can support cooperation in indirect reciprocity. However, it does not manipulate or measure punishment costs, punishment effectiveness, or group payoffs, nor does it use a PGG or similar economic game. Thus, it should be used only as indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of social sanctions and cooperation.

- source: Oria, P. A., Wijnands, M., Alaii, J., & Leeuwis, C. (2018). Options for sustaining solar-powered mosquito trapping systems on Rusinga Island, Western Kenya: a social dilemma analysis. *Bmc Public Health*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that community members prefer individual responsibility for maintaining the mosquito trapping systems, with selective support for small, self-selected group savings (not large-scale collective action). Distrust in leaders, fear of misappropriation, and lack of confidence in group sanctions are major barriers to collective solutions. Some success is seen in small, trusted women's savings groups that can impose sanctions (e.g., expelling non-compliant members), but large governing bodies or compulsory group contributions are widely distrusted. The study highlights the importance of trust, group size, and leadership in sustaining public goods, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides qualitative, contextual evidence about the challenges of sustaining public goods in real-world settings, especially regarding trust, group size, and sanctioning. It suggests that effective punishment or sanctioning is difficult without strong trust and group identity, and that collective action may fail in large, heterogeneous groups. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions of treatment efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of social context and group structure as moderators of collective action and sanctioning effectiveness.

- source: Gao, J., Yan, Y., Li, J. Y., Bai, W. Q., & Geng, Y. Q. (2025). Research trends of cooperation in social dilemmas from 2003-2023: A bibliometric study using CiteSpace. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper identifies that research on cooperation in social dilemmas is multidisciplinary and growing, with key topics including public goods games, punishment, reward, social value orientation, procedural fairness, and group cooperation. It highlights that punishment and reward are frequently studied as mechanisms to promote cooperation, and that public goods games are a common paradigm. However, the paper does not provide quantitative synthesis or meta-analysis of the effects of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. Instead, it summarizes the literature's thematic structure and research frontiers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding the breadth of research topics, the prominence of punishment and public goods games in the literature, and the importance of various design dimensions (e.g., group size, communication, network structure) as studied factors. However, it does not report or synthesize quantitative results on efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, and thus cannot directly inform the prediction task. It may be useful for identifying influential works or trends to consult for more targeted evidence.

- source: Gao, J., Geng, Y. Q., Jiang, X. Y., Li, J. Y., & Yan, Y. (2024). Social dilemma for 30 years: Progress, framework, and future based on CiteSpace analysis. *Medicine*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that social dilemma research is highly interdisciplinary, with public goods games and related paradigms (including punishment and reward mechanisms) as central topics. Punishment and reward are frequently discussed as strategies to promote cooperation, and their effectiveness is a recurring research theme. The field has shifted from describing cooperation to identifying factors and methods that promote it, such as communication, fairness, and institutional incentives. However, the paper does not provide quantitative estimates or meta-analytic synthesis of the effects of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for mapping the landscape of research topics, identifying that punishment, reward, communication, and fairness are widely studied as cooperation-promoting mechanisms. However, it does not report or synthesize quantitative results on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may help identify which dimensions are commonly studied and which mechanisms are considered important, but it does not provide parameter-level or effect-size evidence.

- source: Skreli, E., Xhoxhi, O., Imami, D., & Rama, K. (2024). What explains collective action: The impact of social capital, incentive structures and economic benefits. *Journal of International Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that higher tolerance in reciprocity disposition, perceived positive leadership, and perceived economic benefits from cooperation are all associated with greater willingness to cooperate among Albanian farmers. The effect of reciprocity is stronger in the presence of leadership, though the interaction is not statistically significant in the main regression. Buyer power over price/payments is weakly positively associated with willingness to cooperate, while buyer power over quality is negatively associated. All outcomes are based on self-reported attitudes and perceptions, not on observed behavior or payoffs. Punishment is discussed as a theoretical solution to second-order collective action problems, but is not empirically measured or tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a game-theoretic or experimental design, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding factors that correlate with willingness to cooperate in real-world collective action, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment or other game design dimensions. The discussion of punishment is theoretical and not empirically grounded in this study.

- source: Xu, L. Y., Wang, B. Y., & Guo, W. (2022). The Effect of Task Difficulty and Self-Contribution on Fairness Consideration: An Event-Related Potential Study. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that self-contribution strongly affects fairness-related decisions: participants are much less likely to accept unfair offers when they have contributed more to the joint task, compared to when their partner contributed more or both contributed equally. Task difficulty (hard vs. easy) does not significantly affect acceptance rates or reaction times, but does modulate neurophysiological responses (FRN amplitude is more negative for unfair offers in hard tasks). The main behavioral outcome is the acceptance rate of unfair offers, interpreted as a form of costly punishment, but the paper does not analyze group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about punishment-like behavior (rejection of unfair offers) in a two-player ultimatum game, not a public goods game. The findings suggest that willingness to punish unfairness (by rejecting offers) increases with self-contribution, but the effect on group efficiency or total payoff is not analyzed. The design does not include a baseline without punishment, nor does it report efficiency outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. However, it does suggest that the salience of individual contribution can increase punitive responses to unfairness, which may be relevant for understanding when punishment is more likely to be used in related games.

- source: Li, O., Shi, Y., & Li, K. (2025). Red, rather than blue can promote fairness in decision-making. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the color red (vs. blue) increases (vs. decreases) the amount offered by proposers in the ultimatum game, and this effect is mediated by perceived competitiveness. In the impunity game, where responders cannot punish proposers by rejecting offers, color has no effect on offers. The results suggest that contextual cues like color can influence fair behavior in allocation games, but only when strategic motives (i.e., the threat of rejection/punishment) are present. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and does not use a public goods game or direct punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers indirect evidence that contextual cues (like color) can affect proposer behavior in allocation games when strategic punishment is possible (as in the UG), but not when it is absent (as in the IG). The findings suggest that the presence or absence of punishment mechanisms (or their analogs) can moderate the effect of contextual factors on fair behavior, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Participants in a hypothetical group task scenario evaluated free riders negatively, regardless of gender. Contrary to hypotheses, female free riders were sometimes evaluated less harshly than male free riders, especially by male participants. There were no significant differences in behavioral responses (e.g., willingness to work with, grading) toward free riders based on gender. Some spillover effects were observed: when the free rider was female, the cooperative partner was evaluated more negatively. No actual group task, contribution, or payoff occurred; all outcomes are self-reported attitudes and intentions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a hypothetical scenario to study perceptions and intended responses to free riding, not actual behavior or payoffs. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. It may be relevant for understanding social-psychological responses to free riding, but not for payoff-based modeling.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The experiments show that participants are generally neutral in judging justified defection (not helping a person with a bad reputation). When asked to accept the judgement of a manager (AI or human) who rates the defector as good or bad, participants' acceptance depends on the combination: they only prefer the AI's positive judgement over a human's negative judgement. In all other cases, acceptance is neutral. The study does not measure or report any efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze game-theoretic parameters such as MPCR, punishment cost, or group size.
