It does not report or analyze any payoff-based outcomes, nor does it manipulate or report on game design dimensions such as MPCR, punishment cost, or group size. Its findings are limited to attitudinal acceptance of AI versus human judgements in a scenario of justified defection. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Albergaria, M., & Saes, M. S. (2018). Measuring externalities in an information commons: the case of libraries. *Journal of Cleaner Production*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a library (information commons) setting, users subject to non-monetary sanctions (professors, employees) generate negative externalities for users subject to monetary sanctions (students): an increase in non-student borrowing is associated with a nearly one-to-one decrease in student borrowing. Peer effects are positive but small. The analysis is based on observational data and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding behavioral externalities and the impact of different sanction types in common-pool resource settings, but do not directly inform about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are limited to behavioral externalities (borrowing counts) in a field setting with different sanction types. While it highlights that sanction type can affect user behavior and externalities, it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the setting is only adjacent to public goods games. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited and indirect.

- source: Hirama, C., Zeng, Z. C., Nawa, N., & Fujiwara, T. (2022). Association between Cooperative Attitude and High-Risk Behaviors on the Spread of COVID-19 Infection among Medical Students in Japan. *International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that students who self-identify as 'cooperative' or 'punishment' types (in a hypothetical task-sharing scenario) are significantly more likely to engage in high-risk behaviors (eating/drinking out) during the COVID-19 pandemic than 'noncooperative' types. There is no significant association between cooperative attitude and vaccine hesitancy. The 'punishment' type is defined as not cooperating and letting the partner do the task alone after being let down, which is interpreted as a desire to punish free riders. The study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The study uses a hypothetical scenario to classify cooperative attitudes, including a 'punishment' type, but does not implement or analyze any economic game, nor does it measure payoff, efficiency, or group outcomes. The findings are about associations between self-reported attitudes and real-world risk behaviors, not about the effect of punishment mechanisms in controlled game settings. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task, though it may provide weak, indirect context about the social meaning of punishment attitudes.

- source: Benard, S., Doan, L., Nicholson, D. A., Meanwell, E., Wright, E. L., & Lista, P. (2024). An Eye For an Eye Versus Turning The Other Cheek? The Status Consequences of Revenge and Forgiveness in Intergroup Conflict. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Forgiveness in intergroup conflict is perceived as more status-worthy than revenge, across national, political, and sports identities. (2) Justifying actions as group-motivated (versus individually motivated) has little effect on perceived status, except for a small positive effect for forgiveness in the sports scenario. (3) Open-ended responses show that forgiving actors are more often described as competent, warm, and high-status, while vengeful actors are more often described as dominant and conflict-seeking. (4) There is some evidence that group-motivated revenge is seen as making the group look bad. (5) The study does not measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any material or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it conceptually links intergroup conflict to public goods problems and discusses status as a selective incentive, all outcomes are attitudinal (perceived status, trait attributions) rather than behavioral or payoff-based. There is no manipulation of actual contribution, punishment, or reward in a game-theoretic or economic sense, and no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Thus, the paper is not informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Bruttel, L., Güth, W., Nithammer, J., & Orland, A. (2022). Inefficient Cooperation Under Stochastic and Strategic Uncertainty. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Allowing voluntary ex-post transfers in a two-player stochastic Ultimatum Game does not significantly increase efficiency (total profit) compared to a baseline without transfers. Proposers claim less and acceptance rates are somewhat higher when transfers are available, but responders do not transfer enough to equalize payoffs, and proposers do not trust responders to do so. The result is that the efficiency-enhancing potential of the transfer mechanism is not realized. The study provides direct evidence that, under high stochastic and strategic uncertainty, voluntary reward-like mechanisms may fail to improve efficiency, in contrast to some public goods game findings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary signal for predicting efficiency gains from enabling voluntary reward or transfer mechanisms in environments with high stochastic and strategic uncertainty, especially in two-player, sequential, asymmetric games. Even when such mechanisms reduce claims and increase acceptance rates, they may not translate into higher efficiency if trust and conditional cooperation are lacking. For prediction tasks, the presence of a voluntary reward/transfer mechanism should not be assumed to increase efficiency in such settings, especially when the control efficiency is already low due to uncertainty and lack of coordination.

- source: Kamei, K. (2021). Incomplete Political Contracts with Secret Ballots: Reciprocity as a Force to Enforce Sustainable Clientelistic Relationships. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Incomplete political contracts (vote buying, promises) are common even with secret ballots and no monitoring, especially when interactions are repeated. Candidates spend significant resources to influence voters, and voters reciprocate, leading to frequent reversals of the election outcome compared to the rational baseline. These reversals almost always reduce group total payoff, i.e., efficiency. Redistribution from candidates to voters reduces within-group inequality but does not increase total welfare. Repetition amplifies these effects: candidates spend more, voters receive more, and outcome reversals are more frequent. The design does not include explicit punishment, but the costly transfers function as a form of strategic expenditure to influence behavior, with negative efficiency consequences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in election-like games with costly, non-enforceable transfers (vote buying, promises), the introduction of such mechanisms reduces group efficiency when they distort the outcome. The effect is more pronounced with repeated interactions. For prediction tasks, the presence of costly, voluntary transfer mechanisms (even without explicit punishment) is likely to reduce efficiency relative to a baseline with no such mechanisms, especially when repetition is present. The design is not a standard PGG, but the evidence is informative for adjacent settings where costly, strategic transfers are possible. The paper does not study punishment per se, but the efficiency logic is similar: costly, strategic expenditures to influence others can reduce group welfare if they distort optimal outcomes.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Hauert, C., & Walker, R. (2019). Effort Perception is Made More Accurate with More Effort and When Cooperating with Slackers. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that participants are generally accurate in perceiving their partner's effort in a cooperative task, with higher accuracy when the partner is a slacker and when the participant invests more effort. The experimental design involves a two-player online game where both invest effort to move balls into a bucket, with rewards split 50/50. The main outcome is the accuracy of effort perception, not efficiency or payoff. Theoretical modeling suggests that strategies based on effort perception can help avoid inefficient cooperation, but this is not empirically tested in terms of group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding how effort perception may support cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions. The theoretical discussion of effort-prober strategies is adjacent to efficiency but not empirically linked to payoff outcomes.

- source: Borzino, N., Fatas, E., & Peterle, E. (2023). In transparency we trust an experimental study of reputation, transparency, and signaling. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that providing ex-post transparency about receiver actions (Transparency treatment) significantly increases trust (the amount sent by senders) compared to both Baseline and Reputation (ex-ante information) treatments. However, neither information treatment increases trustworthiness (the amount returned by receivers) on average. The effect of the non-binding recommendation is positive but declines with higher suggested values and over time. The study does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include a punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. Its findings are only weakly relevant for the prediction task, as it focuses on how information and recommendations affect trust and trustworthiness in a networked trust game. The design and outcomes are adjacent to public goods games, but not directly transferable for the purpose of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Sääksvuori, L., & Ramalingam, A. (2015). Bargaining under surveillance: Evidence from a three-person ultimatum game. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that third-party surveillance (the ability of an inactive third-party to observe pre-play chat between proposer and responder) does not affect the allocation of resources in a three-person ultimatum game. Whether the right to observe is exogenously granted or endogenously purchased, and whether the third-party can signal their presence, has no significant effect on the amount allocated to the third-party. Third-parties are willing to pay for the right to observe, and are over-optimistic about the effect of their observation, but their presence does not change outcomes. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The design is a one-shot three-person ultimatum game with pre-play chat and optional third-party monitoring. The main finding is that third-party surveillance alone (without punishment or sanctioning power) does not affect allocations or outcomes. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that mere monitoring or transparency, in the absence of sanctions, may not improve efficiency or resource allocation.

- source: Ocampo, J., & Keltner, D. (2024). Dispositional compassion shifts social preferences in systematic ways. *Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that people higher in dispositional compassion express less satisfaction with both advantageous and disadvantageous monetary allocations relative to others, indicating a preference for equality. These effects are robust to controls for prosociality, positive affect, and other personality traits. Experimental inductions of state compassion or pride do not reliably shift these social preferences. The tasks are abstract, one-shot, and hypothetical, with no real economic consequences or group interaction. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to economic games but lacks key features: there is no group interaction, no repeated rounds, no punishment or reward, and no real payoffs. The findings may inform how dispositional compassion relates to preferences for equality in allocation, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is very limited.

- source: Yang, X. H., Huang, H. Y., Zhang, Y. C., Wang, J. S., Guan, J. H., & Zhou, S. G. (2023). Short Memory-Based Human Strategy Modeling in Social Dilemmas. *Mathematics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that individual accumulated payoff is the most important predictor of cooperation/defection decisions, more so than the outcome of the previous round. Behavioral clustering improves the predictability of individual moves, with F1-scores for predicting cooperation/defection reaching up to 95% in some clusters. The diversity of human strategies is substantial, but clustering allows for more accurate modeling. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes. The findings are most relevant for understanding individual decision-making and the role of payoff history, rather than for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is a repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with optional partner selection (refusal to play), which is only weakly analogous to punishment. The main insight is that accumulated payoff is a strong driver of individual cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding the importance of payoff history in individual decision-making, but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Nockur, L., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Intuitive decision-making promotes rewarding prosocial others independent of the personality trait Honesty-Humility. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that instructing participants to make intuitive decisions (as opposed to reflective or control) increases the amount of reward given to prosocial allocators in a third-party reward task. Honesty-Humility, a personality trait, is not significantly associated with reward behavior. The effect of intuition is robust to controls for other personality dimensions and social value orientation. The study does not examine group efficiency, total payoff, or public goods game settings, and does not include punishment or sanctioning options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot third-party reward task based on a dictator game, not a repeated or group-based public goods game. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The findings may be weakly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of rewarding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the effect of punishment or the efficiency consequences of institutional design in public goods games.

- source: Robbins, B., Karell, D., Siegenthaler, S., & Kamm, A. (2023). Pathways to prosocial leadership: an online experiment on the effects of external subsidies and the relative price of giving. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: External subsidies (private piece-rate transfers) to leaders increase the proportion of community tokens given to recipients, thus increasing group welfare. The effect is robust to the timing of subsidy introduction or removal, with no evidence of crowding out or reduced giving after subsidy removal. The relative price of giving (multiplier) does not significantly affect giving. Norms about giving are not affected by either subsidies or the multiplier. The design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game, but the main outcome is directly related to group welfare/efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that external subsidies to leaders (akin to positive incentives or rewards) can increase group welfare in resource allocation settings, even when leaders have full discretion. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves redistribution rather than voluntary contribution to a public pool. The findings suggest that positive incentives can increase efficiency, but cannot be directly transferred to punishment effects. The absence of punishment, the dictator game structure, and the focus on leader-driven redistribution limit its direct applicability to PGG-like punishment predictions, but the evidence is informative for the effect of positive incentives on efficiency.

- source: Serdarevic, N., Stromland, E., & Tjotta, S. (2021). It pays to be nice: The benefits of cooperating in markets. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that enabling mutual partner choice in repeated prisoner's dilemma games leads to higher overall earnings (efficiency) compared to random matching. This effect is robust across three lab experiments with different group sizes (5 or 9) and information conditions (reminder of full vs. current history). Cooperators benefit most from partner choice, earning significantly more than free riders, who actually earn less under partner choice than under random matching. The effect of partner choice on efficiency is larger in groups of 9 than in groups of 5, but the difference is not statistically significant. Reminding subjects only of their current period's information (rather than full history) increases efficiency more. There is no punishment or explicit sanctioning; exclusion from play is endogenous to the partner choice process.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous partner choice (mutual consent matching) in repeated social dilemma games increases efficiency relative to random matching, especially for cooperative types. While not a public goods game per se, the repeated prisoner's dilemma with continuous contributions and endogenous exclusion is closely related. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment, mechanisms that allow for partner selection and exclusion can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The results also indicate that group size and information about partner history can moderate the efficiency gains from partner choice, though the group size effect is not statistically significant. For prediction tasks, the presence of partner choice (vs. random matching) should be expected to increase efficiency, particularly for cooperators, even without punishment.

- source: Camera, G., Kim, J., & Arjona, D. R. (2025). Choice flexibility and long-run cooperation. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in indefinitely repeated helping games with groups of strangers, expanding the action set to allow partial cooperation (rather than only all-or-nothing) increases both cooperation and realized efficiency, but only when the payoff structure makes partial cooperation sufficiently rewarding. In the control (Baseline, binary choice), efficiency among strangers is much lower than in fixed pairs. Introducing flexible choices (Linear and Low treatments) raises efficiency among strangers by up to 50%, closing much of the gap with fixed pairs. The effect is not present in the High treatment (where partial cooperation is less rewarding) or in fixed pairs (where efficiency is already high). The mechanism appears to be that flexibility allows for trust-building and coordination on cooperation norms among strangers, shifting the distribution of player types toward more cooperative strategies. No punishment or sanctioning is present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in indefinitely repeated helping games without punishment, increasing the flexibility of contribution choices (moving from all-or-nothing to continuous/partial) can substantially increase efficiency among strangers, especially when partial cooperation is sufficiently rewarded. The effect is not observed in small, fixed pairs where efficiency is already high. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games that manipulate the all-or-nothing dimension, the payoff structure for partial cooperation, and group size. However, the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency in control games (without punishment) as a function of action set flexibility and payoff structure.

- source: Johnson, T., & Obradovich, N. (2025). Testing for completions that simulate altruism in early language models. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that certain LLMs (notably text-davinci-003 and GPT-4) generate completions that simulate both payoff-maximizing behavior in non-social tasks and altruistic sharing in dictator game scenarios. The simulated sharing rates for text-davinci-003 are close to those observed in human dictator game experiments, while less advanced models do not consistently maximize payoffs or share resources. The findings are robust to prompt variations, model parameters, and resource framing. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game (PGG) structure is present; the focus is on one-shot dictator games and non-social maximization tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer high-quality evidence on LLMs' simulated behavior in dictator games and non-social maximization tasks, including payoff-based outcomes. Since there is no punishment or sanctioning, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding LLMs' simulated social preferences or for comparison with human dictator game data, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Huber, J., Shubik, M., & Sunder, S. (2018). Financing of public goods through taxation in a general equilibrium economy: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enforceable taxation—especially when set by democratic vote—sustains high efficiency and public good provision, while voluntary anonymous contributions (VACs) lead to rapid decline in both. Endogenous (voted) tax rates result in higher efficiency and private good production than fixed exogenous tax rates. When subjects can choose, they almost always vote for taxation over VACs. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment. The results suggest that, in this dynamic general equilibrium setting, enforceable taxes are a robust and efficient solution to the public goods provision problem, while VACs are unreliable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in dynamic public good economies with and without enforceable taxation, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. The findings show that, in the absence of punishment, enforceable taxes (especially when set endogenously) yield much higher efficiency than voluntary contributions. The design is adjacent to standard PGGs: it is a dynamic, general equilibrium model with investment, depreciation, and a government actor, rather than a simple linear PGG. The results are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing enforceable taxation (not punishment) on efficiency, but do not inform about punishment effects. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper provides a strong control (no-punishment) baseline, but no evidence on the effect of adding punishment.

- source: Schories, F. E. (2022). The Influence of Indirect Democracy and Leadership Choice on Cooperation. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the way an institution is implemented (democratically by an elected leader vs. exogenously or by a random dictator) has a substantial effect on cooperation and group efficiency. When the group leader is elected and chooses to implement a more cooperative payoff structure, both cooperation rates and average earnings are significantly higher than in otherwise identical conditions where the change is imposed exogenously or by a randomly assigned leader. The democracy premium is large and statistically significant in the representative democracy (elected leader) treatment, but not in the random dictator treatment. The effect is especially pronounced among subjects who initially did not prefer the institutional change. The paper provides direct evidence that procedural legitimacy (democratic election) increases efficiency in social dilemma settings, even when controlling for selection and information effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in social dilemma environments structurally similar to the prisoners' dilemma (and by extension, public goods games), the introduction of a more cooperative institution (e.g., a coordination game) by a democratically elected leader leads to higher efficiency (group payoff) than if the same institution is imposed exogenously or by a randomly assigned leader. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of institutional changes on efficiency, the method of implementation (democratic legitimacy) is a critical moderator. However, the results do not inform about the effects of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, as these are not present in the design. The evidence is most relevant for settings where institutional change is possible and the legitimacy of the process is a variable of interest.

- source: Sonntag, A., & Zizzo, D. J. (2019). Personal accountability and cooperation in teams. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing group members the option to acquire information about the true source of others' output (endogenous accountability) leads to the highest average effort and group output, even though almost no one actually chooses to acquire the information. High accountability (where the source is always revealed) leads to the lowest effort and the fastest decline in cooperation over time. Low accountability (no source information) results in intermediate effort. The results suggest that the mere possibility of being observed (the 'Damocles effect') can sustain higher effort, even if observation is rare. There is no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the study does not report efficiency as a ratio, but group output is a direct function of effort.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or team production. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of information/accountability structures on group output in a repeated team production context. Since there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism, the findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with punishment enabled. However, the results suggest that making accountability optional (even if rarely used) can sustain higher group output than either always-on or always-off accountability. For prediction tasks, this paper is most relevant as a control or comparison for environments without punishment, but with varying information about individual contributions.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Gerson, J., Powdthavee, N., & Riyanto, Y. E. (2023). Large losses from little lies: Strategic gender misrepresentation and cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when participants are randomly allowed to misrepresent their gender to their partner in a one-shot Golden Balls game, the average defection (steal) rate increases by about 10-12 percentage points compared to control conditions (blind or true gender revealed). Those who actually choose to misrepresent are about 32 percentage points more likely to defect. The possibility of misrepresentation also increases defection among those who do not misrepresent, due to increased distrust. The result is a substantial reduction in overall cooperation and group welfare, as measured by the proportion of participants receiving the maximum possible payoff (£10 each for mutual cooperation) and the average payment per participant. The effect is robust across lab and online samples, and is not explained by differences in personality traits or risk/trust attitudes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing opportunities for strategic misrepresentation of identity (here, gender) in a one-shot social dilemma with communication reduces cooperation and group welfare, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. While not a public goods game, the Golden Balls game is a close variant of the prisoner's dilemma, and the findings are likely to generalize to other one-shot or anonymous social dilemmas with communication. The results suggest that any design feature that increases uncertainty about partner identity or intentions (such as allowing misrepresentation) can reduce efficiency. For the prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling deception or increasing uncertainty will lower efficiency, even if punishment is not present. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is included in the design.

- source: Huang, L. B., DeScioli, P., & Murad, Z. (2021). Pulling for the Team: Competition Between Political Partisans. *Evolutionary Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In deterministic contest games where participants can spend money to increase their team's chance of winning a prize, people exert significantly less effort when in teams (3-on-3 or best-of-3) than when competing individually (1-on-1), with about a 20% reduction in effort. This pattern holds both for arbitrarily assigned teams and for teams based on political partisanship, even under strong partisan cues and during a highly salient election period. The results support theories of shirking and free-riding in group settings and challenge the idea that group identity or partisanship alone is sufficient to mobilize greater costly effort. No evidence is provided about the effects of punishment, communication, or other mechanisms that might increase group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, group/team settings in contest-like environments lead to lower total effort (and thus lower group payoff/efficiency) compared to individual settings. The findings are adjacent to public goods games: the contest game is not a standard PGG, but the group incentive structure and the opportunity for free-riding are similar. The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is lower in group settings. However, the paper does not test punishment, so it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that group identity or partisanship alone is not sufficient to overcome shirking. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in the absence of punishment, group efficiency will be lower than in individual settings, and that adding group identity cues is unlikely to increase efficiency without additional mechanisms.

- source: Chang, C. C., Kristensen, N. P., Nghiem, T. P. L., Tan, C. L. Y., & Carrasco, L. R. (2021). Cooperating with the future through natural resources restoration. *Sustainability Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that all groups overexploited the resource during extraction, but some pools were rescued through restoration. The likelihood of sustaining the resource pool was lower when previous generations were unsustainable. Individuals extracted more and had higher final payoffs when inheriting from unsustainable previous generations. Restoration behavior was positively associated with feelings of guilt and anger, but not with cognitive style. Deliberate (vs. intuitive) thinkers extracted more and had higher payoffs. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public-goods-game-like environments. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or analyzed. The findings are only indirectly relevant, as they pertain to restoration and intergenerational reciprocity, not to punishment or its efficiency effects.

- source: Schippers, A. L., & Soetevent, A. R. (2024). Sharing with minimal regulation? Evidence from neighborhood book exchange. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: Neighborhood book exchanges operate with minimal regulation and no formal punishment or sanctioning. Despite this, users maintain high levels of reciprocal exchange, returning nearly as many books as they take, and of similar value. Free riding exists but is limited. Owners' restocking compensates for the small deficit. Social norms and strong cooperative preferences among participants are likely responsible for this outcome. The system is stable over time, and a supply shock of high-quality books does not lead to persistent changes in usage or quality. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, high efficiency (measured as reciprocal exchange) can be sustained in some real-world sharing environments, likely due to strong norms and self-selection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a real-world, unregulated sharing environment with no punishment or sanctioning, efficiency (measured as reciprocal exchange) can remain high if strong social norms and cooperative preferences are present. However, the setting is only adjacent to public goods games: there is no explicit group payoff, no monetary contributions, and no formal punishment. The findings suggest that the absence of punishment does not necessarily lead to collapse in efficiency, but this may depend on context-specific factors like self-selection and social norms. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that in environments lacking punishment, efficiency may be higher than standard PGG models predict if strong informal norms exist, but the transferability to standard PGGs is limited.

- source: Zou, M., Feng, J. Q., Qin, N., Diao, J. D., Yang, Y., Liao, J. J., Lin, J. B., & Mo, L. (2022). The Effect of the Quantity and Distribution of Teammates' Tendency Toward Self-Interest and Altruism on Individual Decision-Making. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that participants' willingness to choose the collective solution (akin to cooperation in a public goods game) increases with the observed altruism of teammates and decreases with observed self-interest. There is a ceiling/floor effect: once teammates' altruism/self-interest passes a threshold, further increases have little effect. When a fixed amount of altruism is distributed across more teammates, trust and cooperation increase (decentralized effect); when a fixed amount of self-interest is distributed across more teammates, trust and cooperation decrease (convergent effect). The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. However, it provides informative behavioral evidence about how the perceived distribution of cooperation and defection among group members affects individuals' willingness to cooperate. The absence of punishment or reward means it cannot inform the effect of those mechanisms. The design is adjacent to standard PGGs, as it is a step-level game with both individual and collective solutions, but the main outcomes are behavioral choices, not payoffs or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant.

- source: Yang, C. L., & Yue, C. S. J. (2019). Cooperation in an Assortative Matching Prisoners Dilemma Experiment with Pro-Social Dummies. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that assortative matching based on recent cooperation history can increase cooperation rates compared to random matching, but the effect is inconsistent across groups. Introducing pro-social dummies (always-cooperating computer players) into the assortative matching environment (WHc) leads to a substantial and robust increase in cooperation rates for all types of players, including those initially inclined to defect. The presence of dummies shifts the distribution of cooperation rates to higher values and reduces the prevalence of all-defection. Payoff is positively correlated with cooperation in the assortative matching treatments, but efficiency or group welfare is not directly measured or reported. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or similar games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the effects of assortative matching and exogenous pro-social agents on cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does show that structural features (like matching rules and initial population composition) can have large effects on cooperation rates, which may be conceptually relevant for understanding moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Mitani, Y. (2022). Is a PD game still a dilemma for Japanese rural villagers? A field and laboratory comparison of the impact of social group membership on cooperation. *Japanese Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that social group membership (in-community vs. out-community) has a strong positive effect on cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma game, both in the field (with rural villagers) and in the lab (with students). Villagers achieve near-universal cooperation with in-community matching, and still high cooperation with out-community matching. Students cooperate much less, but the group identity effect is still present. The paper does not include any punishment or reward treatments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are relevant for understanding the effect of social context and group identity on cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that social group identity can strongly increase cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma, especially among rural villagers. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but not for predicting the effect of adding punishment.

- source: Li, T., Li, H., & Zheng, J. D. (2024). Promoting cooperation in collective actions: Evidence from the reserve fund for maintenance in China. *International Review of Economics & Finance*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that requiring additional cash payments from homeowners (when the reserve fund is insufficient) increases the consent rate for maintenance projects. This effect is robust across OLS, IV, and Tobit models, and is stronger for projects proposed by homeowners' associations and for elevator maintenance. The mechanism appears to be increased salience and information provision, which motivates participation. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment/sanctions, and the outcome is the behavioral approval rate, not a payoff-based measure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The findings are about how additional required payments (a form of cost salience or information) increase behavioral consent rates in a real-world collective action setting. While the context is adjacent to public goods games, the lack of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task. It may be useful for understanding how cost salience or information affects participation, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Columbus, S., & Böhm, R. (2021). Norm shifts under the strategy method. *Judgment and Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a continuous two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, participants are more cooperative under the simultaneous decision method than under the strategy method, especially among conditional cooperators with low expectations of others' cooperation. This is explained by a shift in the salient social norm: the simultaneous method foregrounds an egalitarian fairness norm (give half), while the strategy method foregrounds a reciprocity norm (match the other's behavior). Norm elicitation confirms that fairness is more normative under the simultaneous method, and reciprocity is more normative under the strategy method. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about how elicitation methods and norm salience can affect cooperation rates in two-player continuous social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, except as a caution that ancillary design features (like elicitation method) can shift cooperation via norm salience, which could interact with punishment in other designs.

- source: Molho, C., Soraperra, I., Schulz, J. F., & Shalvi, S. (2025). Guilt drives prosociality across 20 countries. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 20 countries, participants were more likely to make prosocial choices in dictator games when they had full information about the consequences of their actions (guilt condition) compared to when they could remain ignorant. Making choices observable to others (shame condition) had negligible or inconsistent effects on prosociality. Individual-level guilt-proneness predicted greater responsiveness to information, but country-level differences were not robust moderators. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or group interaction, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings on the effect of information and observability on individual prosocial choice in dictator games may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms of prosociality, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Song, F., & Zhong, C. B. (2015). You scratch his back, he scratches mine and I'll scratch yours: Deception in simultaneous cyclic networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in simultaneous cyclic networks, participants are more likely to engage in deception (lying about the endowment size) on behalf of others than when acting for themselves. This effect is present both in small (dyadic) and larger (20-30 person) cyclic networks, and is driven by the anticipation of indirect reciprocity. Breaking the reciprocity loop reduces deception. The study uses a modified ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and does not involve punishment or sanctions. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a modified ultimatum game rather than a public goods game. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Jones, M. I., & Christakis, N. A. (2024). Bringing leaders of network subgroups closer together does not facilitate consensus. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing noise in the information network makes it less likely for groups to reach consensus and increases the time required to do so. The geodesic distance between leaders of opposing subgroups does not have a significant effect on the probability or speed of consensus formation. Leadership labeling affects individual behavior, making leaders more likely to compromise when their neighbors do, but this does not translate into group-level differences in consensus based on leader distance. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or any efficiency/payoff-based outcome. The findings are specific to consensus formation in a networked coordination game with leader labeling and noise, and do not transfer to the prediction of efficiency under punishment in PGG or similar games.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., & Schulhofer-Wohl, J. (2022). The impact of distance on parochial altruism: An experimental investigation. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that parochial altruism (allocation to the between-group account, which benefits the in-group and harms the out-group) is higher when both in-group and out-group distances are high. Low distance to the out-group reduces both altruism and parochial altruism. The main behavioral outcomes are the propensity and amount allocated to within-group and between-group accounts. There is no analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The game does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are robust to alternative distance measures and controls for group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The behavioral outcomes (parochial altruism, altruism) are not directly transferable to payoff-based efficiency predictions. The game is an intergroup conflict variant (IPD-MD), not a standard PGG, and the main manipulations concern group distance, not institutional design features like punishment or reward. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Cassar, A., Grosjean, P., Khan, F. J., & Lambert, M. (2023). Mothers, fathers, and others: Competition and cooperation in the aftermath of conflict. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that exposure to conflict increases egalitarianism (interpreted as a proxy for cooperation) among non-parents, especially non-parent males, but not among parents. Conflict exposure reduces competitiveness for most groups, except for mothers, who become more competitive after victimization, particularly when they are single, have many children, or have young children. The study does not use a public goods game or any punishment/reward mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on how conflict exposure affects behavioral proxies for cooperation and competition may be of limited indirect interest for understanding how adversity or group context shapes prosociality, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Sequeira, M. E. (2023). Fairness, partner choice, and punishment: An ethnographic study of cooperative behavior among children in Helsinki, Finland. *Ethos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that among Helsinki-based children, fairness is a central value, and children resolve conflicts or perceived unfairness primarily through partner choice strategies (avoiding or disengaging from unfair partners) rather than through punishment. Both children and adults show a strong aversion to punishment, which is rare in both peer and adult-child interactions. Autonomy and independence are emphasized in socialization, and norm enforcement is achieved through persuasion and shaming rather than coercion or punitive measures. The study does not use experimental games or report on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it is an ethnographic observational study of real-world child interactions, not a controlled experiment or game. However, it does provide strong qualitative evidence that in this context, punishment is rare and not socially valued as a means of sustaining cooperation. If one were to extrapolate, it suggests that in environments with strong norms of autonomy and aversion to punishment, introducing punishment mechanisms may not increase cooperation or efficiency, and may even be resisted or ignored. However, the lack of payoff or efficiency data means this paper cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bharti, N., & Malik, S. (2023). History of Agricultural Collectives in India: Past, Present and Future. *Hse-social and Education History*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that agricultural collectives in India have played a significant role in supporting smallholder farmers, but their success has varied across time and context. Key challenges include free riding, working capital, legal and management issues, and group structure. The paper highlights that voluntary membership and social capital are important for collective sustainability. It references theoretical work suggesting that both rewards and punishments can address free riding, but rewards may be more effective. However, these points are discussed at a conceptual level, not tested empirically in the paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It offers contextual and theoretical discussion about the importance of addressing free riding in collectives, and mentions that both punishment and reward have been considered as solutions in the literature. However, it does not analyze or report on game design dimensions, efficiency, or payoff outcomes in a way that would inform quantitative prediction. Its main value is in highlighting the relevance of free riding and the potential role of sanctions in collective action problems, but it does not provide data or experimental results.

- source: Radkani, S., Tenenbaum, J. B., & Saxe, R. (2025). What people learn from punishment: A cognitive model. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that observers' inferences about the wrongness of an act and the motives of an authority after observing punishment are interdependent and strongly shaped by prior beliefs about norms and authority. Punishment can communicate norm violation and enhance or damage the authority's reputation, depending on context. The authors' Bayesian inverse planning model quantitatively predicts these belief updates and outperforms alternative models. The study also shows that repeated observation of punishment can either reduce or sustain polarization in beliefs, depending on initial priors. However, all outcomes are belief-based; the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about belief updating and interpretation of punishment, not about actual contributions, cooperation, or payoffs. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding how punishment is perceived or how norm communication and legitimacy are inferred, but not for payoff-based outcome prediction.

- source: Brouwer, T., Galeotti, F., & Villeval, M. C. (2023). Teaching Norms: Direct Evidence of Parental Transmission. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: Parents are significantly more likely to directly punish a norm violator (verbal confrontation) and to help a stranger in need when their child is present, compared to when they are alone. There is no significant increase in indirect punishment (withholding help) in the presence of a child. The findings support the idea that parents use opportunities to teach social norms to their children by modeling both norm enforcement and prosocial behavior. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcomes.

Its relevance is limited to understanding behavioral mechanisms of norm enforcement and punishment in naturalistic settings, not to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in economic games.

- source: Alvarez-Benjumea, A., & Valentim, V. (2024). The Enforcement of Political Norms. *British Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that individuals in Spain perceive public displays of support for the radical-right party Vox as less socially and morally appropriate and more harmful than support for other parties. Respondents are more likely to view sanctioning (especially indirect sanctions like gossip and avoidance) as appropriate and report greater willingness to engage in such sanctions against radical-right supporters. The willingness to sanction is not strongly predicted by political ideology or interest, but is correlated with age, gender, education, local support for Vox, and emotional reactions (anger, disgust). The study does not measure or discuss any payoff-based or efficiency outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or similar economic game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG or similar environments.

- source: Stanley, M. L., Huang, S. Y., Marsh, E. J., & Kay, A. C. (2023). The Role of Structure-Seeking in Moral Punishment. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that (1) individuals with a higher need for structure are more likely to endorse punishment for moral rule violations, (2) experimentally inducing a threat to structure increases punitive judgments, and (3) recalling unpunished wrongdoing (vs. punished wrongdoing) reduces perceived structure in the social world. All outcomes are attitudinal or perceptual; there are no behavioral or payoff-based measures. The studies use hypothetical or autobiographical scenarios about moral rule violations, not economic games or public goods dilemmas.
